Noise and learning-curve evaluations demonstrate sustained predictive gains under perturbed inputs and limited supervision, while inference measurements quantify computational cost. This article extends our ICCS 2026 paper, Modeling Nonlinear Feature Interactions with Product-Unit Residual Networks (doi:10.1007/978-3-032-29921-5_6).

new Deep Latents, Long Memory: Latent persistence explains temporal correlations in transformer activations

Authors: Francesco Cagnetta, Santiago Acevedo

Abstract: The hidden layers of Large Language Models (LLMs) are believed to encode representations of the data whose abstraction increases with depth. However, in the absence of an operational notion of abstraction, this statement is hard to test quantitatively. Here, we propose an alternative view, building on the principle that the correlations of a sequence reflect the structure of the process that generated it. We define the autocorrelation function of a layer as the average dot product between its activations at two context positions separated by a lag. Latent variables encoded persistently along the context create slowly decaying tails in the autocorrelation, and we find these tails to grow across layers in both synthetic and natural data. In synthetic data with controlled hierarchical latent structure, this growth stems from the encoding of abstract latent variables, which govern long stretches of the input. Indeed, the persistence of latent codes predicts the qualitative structure of autocorrelations and accounts for most of the empirical measures. In real text, we take sparse-autoencoder features as proxies for latent variables, and find that their persistence correlates strongly with the magnitude of autocorrelations across datasets and LLMs. In particular, the persistence time increases with depth, as do autocorrelations, implying that deeper latent features persist for longer context spans. We complement this analysis by explicitly ablating the persistent sparse-autoencoder features and observing a consequent decrease in the autocorrelations.

new Efficient Poincar\'e Convolutions through Patch-Statistics Factorization

Authors: Luk\'a\v{s} Jochymek, Mark\'eta Va\v{s}inkov\'a, Petr Gajdo\v{s}

Abstract: Published implementations of the Poincar\'e convolution in Hyperbolic Neural Networks++ (HNN++) map every overlapping image patch to the Poincar\'e ball before evaluating its score. We show that the clipped score depends on each patch only through two statistics, its squared norm and its inner products with the filters, which a native convolution and a single-channel box filter compute directly. The factorized layer therefore evaluates the specified score exactly, without constructing ball-valued patches, and its single-precision outputs and gradients agree with double-precision evaluations at least as closely as those of the explicit reference. On an RTX A6000 under two versions of JAX, complete training updates of a Poincar\'e U-Net and a Poincar\'e ResNet-20 are 1.5 to 1.8 times faster than the faster of two explicit implementations that map patches to the ball (1.2 to 1.4 times against an explicit implementation that skips this mapping); on a V100, the gain over the gather-based one persists as the ResNet-20 is widened eightfold. In 15 paired training runs per task, updates are 2.0 and 1.8 times faster than a reference that follows the published execution order, with no consistent change in final segmentation or classification quality, and peak memory falls by 38 to 55% in a U-Net scaling study. Replacing the convolutions of the official PyTorch Hyperbolic U-Net makes its training step 1.4 times faster with half the memory. Evaluating an existing layer from its patch statistics thus makes Poincar\'e convolutional networks cheaper to train in the tested models and software stacks.

new When Copying Is Hard to Beat: A Negative Result in Clinical Assessment Forecasting

Authors: Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler

Abstract: We report a negative result in next-day clinical assessment forecasting: the evaluated models show no consistent advantage over strong persistence-based baselines. Clinicians can view the previous day's questionnaire answers, a plausible source of documentation persistence. Predicting 45 items on 29,715 ICU day-pairs with patient-disjoint splits, a physiology-informed gated model improves item-macro-F1 over a one-line clinical rule by +0.0019 [+0.0006,+0.0031], while losing accuracy. No macro-F1 advantage is established when the physiology window ends at the previous form's reference time (+0.0001 [-0.0006,+0.0008]), or when scoring excludes the 68.5% of cells derived from parent answers; combining both changes yields a small disadvantage. Four checkpoints across three families underperform persistence, and a fine-tuned one differs from it on only two cells. A categorical read-out raises changed-cell accuracy eightfold, but a state-only read-out scores higher overall than forecast-only decoding and matches a transition matrix. This negative result highlights how the documentation process, input cutoff, observation mask and decoding rule shape conclusions about forecasting recorded assessments.

new STAMP: Manufacturing-Informed Generative Design and Shape Optimization of Sheet Panels

Authors: Jihoon Kim, Jun Hyeok Song, Wonho Ha, Namwoo Kang

Abstract: Generative design of formed sheet panels must preserve geometric manufacturing conditions while exploring layouts and improving performance. STAMP embeds these conditions directly in the shape representation. Each panel is described by its material layout and a reference surface with one height at each occupied position in a plane perpendicular to the drawing direction. Nominal thickness is prescribed along the normal. The representation allows curved surfaces, ribs, and openings. It excludes reference-surface undercuts and keeps nominal thickness fixed without manufacturing penalty terms. Because these conditions belong to the representation, every generated and optimized panel satisfies them regardless of training-set size. Boundary-conditioned diffusion generates alternative layouts, and predictor gradients guide a surface optimization that retains outlines, openings, and designated fixed regions. We demonstrate STAMP on automotive hood inner panels using CarHoods10k. With a comparable number of stored values, depth maps reconstruct surfaces more accurately than point-cloud and voxel baselines. From only five training designs, one of three training runs yields novel and varied layouts. A generator trained on 500 designs, 6.5% of the training set, already reaches the layout variety of the full-data model. Across 11 held-out design families, predictor-based selection among existing dataset designs yields an average gap of 0.18 mm to the best available FEA deformation, versus an expected 1.59 mm for random selection. It does better than random selection in all 11 families. At a 10% depth-change bound, a network not used in the search predicts an average deformation reduction of 24.8% for six generated and existing panels, with a 3.2% average mass increase computed from geometry. Engineers can thus explore and optimize panels within this manufacturing-informed design space.

new Which Model, For Which Variable? Per-Variable Forecast Combination in Sparse ICU Time Series

Authors: Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler

Abstract: Clinical forecasting papers typically report a single best architecture. We show that on multivariate ICU physiology this question conceals variable-specific differences: no architecture is best on every variable, and which model wins changes from variable to variable. Across five ICU databases (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, eICU, HiRID, and UMM (University Medicine Mannheim), a German single-center ICU) we forecast 94 channels over a 24-hour horizon with a pool of 14 models, and combine them with weights fitted per variable. On test, the combination beats the best single model on all five databases by 6.1-11.0% mean squared error (mean 8.7%). The gain is concentrated where clinical data is thinnest: the sparsest third of channels improves by 11.9% and laboratory values by 7.5%, against 3.1% for the densest third. Rank-based harmonic weighting of the best five models per variable, with no fitted weights at all, recovers about four-fifths of the gain. Extending the pool afterwards with a per-channel gradient-boosted tree, ARIMA and ETS makes the tree the validation-selected best single model on all five databases; per-variable combination still improves on it by 2.9-5.9% (significant on four of five), while equal weighting of the same members does not. As on univariate benchmarks, learned combination beats simple averaging - but only once the weights are constrained. We also report which model to prefer when, and show that the ordering changes with database size.

new On the Limits of Sufficient Explanations

Authors: Klaus Weinbauer, Peter F. Stadler, Thomas G\"artner, Sagar Malhotra

Abstract: We formally characterize the conditions under which sufficient explanations exist for classifiers on structured data such as graphs and strings. We focus on post-hoc explainers that select parts of the input as sufficient explanations. An explanation is sufficient if the selected part alone ensures the predicted label. Sufficient explanations have been widely adopted across data modalities, including graphs, strings, and images. However, a unified formal analysis of sufficient explanations remains missing. We bridge this gap with four main contributions: (i) We define a general explanation formalism consisting of an instance space, a classifier, and a select relation. The select relation specifies which potential sub-instances or feature subsets can be selected as explanations from each instance; (ii) We show that the existence of sufficient explainers is fully characterized by the interplay between the classifier and the select relation; (iii) We extend this analysis to practically relevant variants of sufficiency, including probabilistic sufficiency and deletion sufficiency; (iv) Based on the formal analysis, we devise statistics that bound the maximum achievable sufficiency for a given task. We evaluate these statistics on language, protein, and molecular classification datasets and find that full sufficiency is unattainable on most of them. Since these bounds hold for every explainer, they provide a task-level reference against which any explainer's sufficiency can be judged in absolute terms.

new MarathonTS: a benchmark of long synthetic time series from window assembly and long-series generators

Authors: Ilyas Varshavskiy, Sergey Shulgin, Shuhrat Khalilbekov, Bonu Boboeva, Azizjon Azimi

Abstract: Many applications of synthetic time series require a series that is much longer than the windows on which a generative model is trained. Such a series can be assembled from a pool of short generated windows, which requires deciding which windows are taken, in what order, and how their seams are processed, or it can be produced directly by a long-series generator. This study presents MarathonTS, to our knowledge the first open benchmark that compares both families under one protocol. Every method produces a series of the same length on 9 open hourly datasets with 10 seeds. The series is evaluated by its fidelity to the real data (83 metrics in 12 groups, against three real references: train, validation and an out-of-time split), by its utility (models are trained on the synthetic series only and tested on real data, TSTR against TRTR, in 14 classification and regression tasks), by its memorisation of the training data and by its computational cost. The statistical analysis is carried out as a separate stage that reads the published tables and gives the rankings of every method in every slice, the answers to the research questions with tests and uncertainty, and the results of a pre-specified confirmatory family. In the main run, the methods differ in both confirmatory cells; the choice of windows matters more than the processing of their seams, which does not improve the series, and no method trains the downstream models as well as the real series. The top tier of the leaderboard is reproduced on other hardware, whereas the exact places within it are not. For the reproducibility of the study, the code, the data hashes and all results are publicly available (links below).

new T2ST: Data-Efficient Spatio-Temporal Foundation Models via Temporal Data and Knowledge Transfer

Authors: Yue Xu, Kaiyun Wei, Wenying Duan, Zichen Xu, Shuai Ma, Xiaoxi He

Abstract: Spatio-temporal foundation models (STFMs) require large-scale training corpora, yet synchronized observations and spatial metadata are costly to acquire and curate, limiting the availability of suitable data. By contrast, time-series data are abundant and easier to collect. Since observations at each location form a time series, this motivates a natural question: can abundant time-series data enhance STFMs when spatio-temporal observations are scarce? To bridge this gap, we propose T2ST, a data-efficient training framework that augments limited spatio-temporal observations with selected auxiliary time series and transfers knowledge from a frozen time-series foundation model. Our key insight is that temporal pattern learning can benefit from abundant external time series, while spatial dependency learning remains grounded in real spatio-temporal observations. Guided by this insight, T2ST retrieves matched sequences to reinforce sparsely observed temporal patterns and selects reliable complementary sequences to broaden temporal coverage. Temporal relation distillation aligns relationships among historical patches, while multi-scale prediction distillation transfers forecasting behavior at fine and coarse temporal resolutions, enabling knowledge transfer across heterogeneous architectures. Using only 10\% of the original spatio-temporal data, T2ST enables STFMs to achieve overall forecasting performance competitive with or superior to their full-data counterparts across six long-term and twelve short-term forecasting datasets. These results establish a new paradigm for data-efficient training of spatio-temporal foundation models. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/T2ST-65F3/.

URLs: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/T2ST-65F3/.

new NeoResearch: Literature- and Experience-Guided Autonomous Time Series Model Discovery

Authors: Ze Guo, Mingyue Cheng, Xiaoyu Tao, Shuo Yu, Shangtong Ye

Abstract: Developing effective forecasting models for diverse time-series domains requires substantial expert knowledge, evidence from scientific literature on the web, and iterative experimentation. However, translating this knowledge and research experience into model designs requires preserving source context and testing applicability under task-specific assumptions. In this paper, we propose NeoResearch, a literature- and experience-guided autonomous research framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) as researchers to discover lightweight, domain-specific forecasting models. Specifically, NeoResearch establishes an expert-inspired dual-loop research workflow, coupling hypothesis-driven architecture exploration with iterative training and hyperparameter optimization through experimental feedback. To enable knowledge-grounded discovery, the agent autonomously mines scientific literature from the Web, extracting modeling mechanisms, empirical evidence, and applicability conditions to formulate task-grounded, testable architecture hypotheses. Furthermore, a hierarchical experience memory links literature findings to experimental outcomes and consolidates conditional lessons, guiding architecture discovery and training optimization while supporting reuse across forecasting tasks. Experiments on ten datasets show competitive performance and support dual loops, literature extraction, and memory reuse. Our code is available.

new Small Is Not Dead: Distance-Aware Cluster Revival for e-means

Authors: Eyal Weiss

Abstract: e-means (Bessa et al., 2026) is a recent mini-batch k-means algorithm, now part of the FAISS library, that reaches the error of Lloyd's algorithm about an order of magnitude faster on large embedding datasets. Every five mini-batches, it removes every cluster whose running point count falls below a tenth of the average and reuses its centre to split a large cluster. In the authors' ablation, removing this mechanism, called dead-cluster revival, raises the error more than removing any other component added to the moving-average update. The rule, however, considers only cluster size: it cannot distinguish a cluster that is small because it is redundant from one that covers a rare, isolated group of points, and on data with such groups it repeatedly removes centres whose removal is costly. We add a second condition, based on the distance between centres: a small cluster is removed only if its centre is close to another centre, relative to a running estimate of the mean squared error. When a centre equals the mean of its points, this squared distance is an upper bound on the average increase in squared error, per point of the cluster, caused by removing the centre. The condition costs one nearest-centre search for the small clusters every five mini-batches and adds no measurable running time. On the e-means paper's embedding benchmarks, it never prevents a removal, so the results are identical to those of FAISS. On datasets with small isolated groups not used during development, it reduces the test error after two epochs by roughly 10% to 80% relative to count-based revival, and it preserves the advantage of k-means++ initialization; on the other evaluation datasets, it stays within about 1% of the better of revival enabled and disabled. We also report two failure modes that the analysis predicts. This is a CPU proof of concept; GPU implementations are left to future work.

new Neural Classifiers as Error-Correcting Codes

Authors: Sergey Salishev, Anton Makarov, Oleg Granichin, Renata Avros, Zeev Volkovich

Abstract: A unified framework is proposed that characterizes supervised deep classification as the decoding of error-correcting codes in the Shannon sense. Cross-entropy optimization is shown to align softmax classifiers with maximum-likelihood decoding, where logits map to Hamming distances to class-specific codewords. This correspondence explains the emergence of standard divergence objectives (e.g., Kullback-Leibler, Jeffreys) and provides a geometric basis for the stability of decision boundaries. Furthermore, the geometries of Neural Collapse and LeJEPA are identified as optimal constellations for decoding under isotropic noise. Unlike classical Error-Correcting Output Codes, deep networks are shown to learn effective codebooks in the feature space implicitly. This insight is leveraged to derive novel criteria for lossless quantization, improved knowledge distillation, and architectural regularization based on code redundancy. Finally, this perspective is extended to self-supervised encoders, offering a cohesive explanation for the geometry of latent representations in modern neural models.

new ParetoM$^3$: Learning on the Pareto Set under Preference Guidance via Min-Max-Min Optimization

Authors: Pei Tang, Songtao Lu

Abstract: Learning across multiple inherently conflicting objectives requires selecting desirable trade-offs from the Pareto set. In this work, we study preference-guided multi-objective optimization, where a predefined preference function is minimized over the weak Pareto set of multiple nonconvex objectives. Leveraging a merit function that vanishes on the weak Pareto set, we propose a penalty-based min-max-min formulation, termed the M$^3$ problem, which unifies preference optimization and the enforcement of weak Pareto optimality in a single objective. We further develop ParetoM$^3$, a single-loop first-order algorithm for computing stationary solutions of the resulting M$^3$ problem. Under a local Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz assumption, we prove that ParetoM$^3$ finds an $\epsilon$-stationary point of the M$^3$ problem that is also approximately weakly Pareto optimal for the original multi-objective problem when the penalty parameter is sufficiently large, with explicit non-asymptotic convergence rates. We also show that this stationarity notion recovers existing optimality criteria, including approximate preference stationarity and approximate Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions, under the same assumptions used in prior works. Experiments on a synthetic benchmark, image classification, regression, and math reasoning demonstrate that ParetoM$^3$ reliably attains preferred weak Pareto optimal solutions while achieving strong practical performance. Code is available at https://github.com/tpgoodboy/ParetoM3.

URLs: https://github.com/tpgoodboy/ParetoM3.

new Right Token, Right Time: From Per-Token Gating to Trajectory-Aware Activation Steering

Authors: Nima Eshraghi, Lovedeep Gondara, Sagarika Suresh, Xiaotong Xu, Jithin Pradeep, Fanny Chevalier

Abstract: Activation steering via sparse autoencoders (SAEs) enables behavioral control of large language models without task-specific fine-tuning. Yet, standard methods apply the steering signal at every generated token, displacing representations from the distribution the model was trained on and eroding generation quality. We show that \emph{when} along a generation the intervention is applied is a decisive axis of control. We cast steering as a per-token allocation problem under a fixed budget of steerable tokens, so that methods differ only in which tokens they select, making allocation the sole variable. Within this framework we introduce two gates. The first, Contrastive Feature Projection (CFP), is memoryless and selects the tokens least aligned with the target behavior through a sparse, interpretable feature projection. The second, Closed-Loop Adaptive Bernoulli Steering (CLABS), is trajectory aware. It treats allocation as closed-loop feedback control, tracking the alignment a generation has accumulated so far and spending budget where the trajectory falls behind. On a linear steering-response model we derive an explicit gain region that guarantees closed-loop stability of CLABS and show that its integral term reduces the steady-state tracking offset. Empirically, CFP and CLABS achieve the largest behavioral shift among budgeted gates at matched budgets, with CLABS strongest under the tight budgets where allocation matters most, while best preserving fluency.

new Clean Tickets, Wrong Orders: Catching Silent Errors with a Language Model in Your Browser Tab

Authors: Umar Ahmed, Maxim Dunaev, Mohammad Nour Al Awad, Sergey Ivanov, Ivan Khodnenko

Abstract: A small language model that turns a typed order into structure can fail in two ways. It can print something broken, which any system rejects. Or it can print a clean, well formed order that nobody placed. Our companion work calls the second kind a silent error, and it is the dangerous one. We present a web demo in which a fine-tuned 0.8B parameter model runs entirely inside the visitor's browser tab, through llama.cpp compiled to WebAssembly. No order text leaves the device. The page parses orders as the visitor types, scores every ticket with the PIZZA benchmark's own matcher ported to TypeScript, refuses anything off the menu with "?" so the model can face the open web, and lets visitors hunt for silent errors or try to cause one. Run in the browser on all 1,357 PIZZA test orders, the deployed model writes the same output as the server evaluation for 99.0% of them and reaches the same exact match (0.882); we also report its latency on 3 consumer laptops. Demo: https://wrong.cuyor.com

URLs: https://wrong.cuyor.com

new Emulating physics-based XCO2 retrievals across OCO-2 viewing modes with compact neural networks

Authors: Xianlei San, Chengxing Zhai, Hui Su, Kuai Dai, Shuangchen Du

Abstract: Operational full-physics retrieval of the column-averaged dry-air mole fraction of carbon dioxide from satellite spectra is computationally demanding. We use singular value decomposition to compress spectra from the Orbiting Carbon Observatory-2 separately by band. Compact multilayer perceptrons use the resulting spectral scores, prior information, and viewing geometry to predict the difference between the full-physics retrieval and its operational prior. Adding the predicted difference to the prior yields an estimate of the retrieved mole fraction. The models were trained on 2015-2019 observations and validated on 2020 observations. In 2021-2025 tests, the mean annual root-mean-square errors relative to the Atmospheric Carbon Dioxide Observations from Space retrieval product were 0.89, 1.35, and 1.43 parts per million for ocean glint, land glint, and nadir observations, respectively. On East Asian nadir observations screened using the published criteria for the spectral Transformer, our model yielded a lower mean annual error than reported for that model in 2020-2022. These results show that compact neural networks using compressed spectra can efficiently emulate the full-physics retrieval across multiple viewing modes and years.

new DECODE: Decomposable Environments for COntrolled Sequential-Decision Evaluation

Authors: Diego Silva Prado, Bruno Caldas de Souza, Tiago Amaral Penteado de Jesus, Ricardo Guimar\~aes Herrmann, Cristiano Breuel, Cristiano Bevitori Maffia De Oliveira

Abstract: When a sequential decision system falls short of an optimum, its score does not reveal which modifications would improve its decisions. DECODE is a benchmark for testing such modifications through controlled experiments on planning and available information. Its consumer-credit collections generator provides five partially observed environments that share known dynamics matrices and vary a memory parameter and process noise. The experiments change selected inputs or planning rules while keeping the rest of the solver fixed. Each comparison specifies the solver's state inputs, response inputs and planning horizon. On the deterministic base environment, which was selected for a large gap between six-month and one-month planning with the hidden state, extending planning from one to six months increases the scores of two gradient-boosted solvers by 15 to 17 percentage points of the exact optimum, computed with the hidden state and borrower traits. Even at this horizon, the best graded solver, whose repayment response is fitted on logs, remains 15.6 points below that optimum. In a specified intervention sequence, three changes to its state inputs and their evaluation during search lower its score by 6.3 points, and replacing its fitted repayment model with the true response, evaluated with hidden borrower traits, then raises it by 15.6, a net recovery of nine of those points. Because the intermediate effects are conditional on the repayment model and the order of the changes, they do not uniquely assign the gap to separate causes. Within these limits, DECODE makes hypotheses about solver performance testable through specified interventions under known dynamics and explicit information conditions.

new f-Divergence Self-Play for Tabular Anomaly Detection via Large Language Models

Authors: Hoang Tran Vuong, Linh Ngo Van, Dang Nguyen, Thin Nguyen, Phuoc Nguyen, Mehrtash Harandi, Trung Le

Abstract: Anomaly detection in tabular data poses significant challenges due to heterogeneous feature types-mixing numerical, categorical, and textual attributes, which complicate learning meaningful representations of normality. Recent work has applied large language models (LLMs) to this problem by serializing table rows as text sequences, yet these approaches rely on one-shot supervised fine-tuning that offers limited signal to tighten the model's description of normality. We propose DiSPaT, a self-play fine-tuning framework that strengthens the model's understanding of normal data. Building on the theoretical foundation of f-divergence minimization, we derive a tight approximation connecting our training objective to reducing the distributional gap between real normal data and model-generated samples. DiSPaT operates through an alternating optimization: at each iteration, the current policy generates synthetic samples that serve as pseudo-anomalies, while a critic discriminator learns to distinguish these from real normal samples; this signal drives policy updates that progressively align the model distribution with the true normal-data distribution. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that DiSPaT consistently outperforms prior LLM-based methods, deep learning approaches, and classical unsupervised detectors for tabular anomaly detection.

new Where Tree Ensembles Decide: Structural Local Explanation with Decision Predicate Graphs

Authors: Sylvio Barbon Junior, Luca Longo

Abstract: Tree ensembles are among the most accurate models for tabular data, yet the computation behind a single prediction is spread over hundreds of decision rules. Feature attributions and counterfactuals explain such predictions through the influence of features or a change of the input, leaving open which of the thresholds applied by the trees a prediction responds to, while structural explanations compress these rules and lose the trees behind them. To address this gap, we propose a local Decision Predicate Graph (local DPG), which gathers the rules executed for a sample into a verifiable record of the computation, with a class support that reproduces every explained prediction. From these rules we select the critical predicate, the executed predicate whose crossing is estimated to move the model most strongly toward a chosen class, which offers the user a concrete threshold and the trees that share it, without an external explainer. Across 16 datasets and four ensemble families, it separates differently predicted neighbours better than random and nearest-threshold predicates. A single crossing recovers the true class in 46-69% of wrong predictions, against 29-50% when the same rule is applied to the feature ranked first by TreeSHAP, a measure close to the selection criterion, and on planted faults it finds the faulty threshold in 46-71% of the resulting errors, against 6-21% at random. Although training-free edits of these predicates did not reduce the error on unseen data, the critical predicate complements attributions and counterfactuals by naming where a prediction can be changed.

new WeatherDreamer: Learning a World Model from Multimodal Earth Observations

Authors: Kate Duffy, Elise Penn, Tristan Ballard, August Posch, Thomas J. Vandal

Abstract: Machine learning weather models now rival operational global forecasts but still start from analyses produced by separate, expensive data assimilation (DA) systems, which for global models are typically updated every six hours. Observation-to-observation models avoid this dependence, but most omit the recursive filtering cycle that lets DA systems carry a background state through sparse, irregular observations. We introduce WeatherDreamer, a world model that learns this cycle end-to-end from 18 global observing systems, without reanalysis. It infers a latent state from observations, carries it forward hour by hour with learned dynamics, corrects it each hour with new observations, and can predict what any of its observing systems would measure at any location. At held-out stations, its ensembles beat NOAA's Global Ensemble Forecast System (GEFS) for temperature, dew point and wind out to 12 h and are better calibrated than GEFS and the ECMWF ensemble. Precipitation, cloud-top height and solar irradiance, never inputs at inference, are inferred with skill matching or exceeding operational products at short leads. Against the first guess of NOAA's Gridpoint Statistical Interpolation (GSI) DA system, WeatherDreamer's short-range departures are within 10% of GSI's at surface stations and within 25% for moisture-sensitive microwave channels, but up to five times larger for temperature-sounding channels. Never given observation errors, its learned update assumes errors close to known instrument and retrieval errors of microwave sounders and scatterometer winds. WeatherDreamer also separates viewing geometry from atmospheric state, recovering 83-96% of observed limb darkening and producing seamless nadir-view imagery. Producing an hourly global analysis in about 1.5 minutes on one GPU, WeatherDreamer shows that assimilation and forecasting can be learned together directly from observations.

new Deep Neural Networks Training Efficiency: Does Grokking Modify its Dynamics?

Authors: Eduardo Cueto-Mendoza, John D. Kelleher

Abstract: We study whether grokking, the delayed emergence of out-of-distribution generalization after apparent overfitting, alters training efficiency in deep neural networks, where efficiency is measured as accuracy per unit energy. Using two canonical settings, modular arithmetic and MNIST, we analyze two neural network models under 16-, 32-, and 64-bit precision and use Orthograd to test whether grokking produces noticeable deviations from the typically decreasing dynamics of training efficiency. We consider both short training runs of up to 500 epochs and long training runs of up to 20,000 epochs, and in both cases identify grokking as a sharp improvement in test accuracy after a sustained train-test gap. In the short-horizon experiments, applying Orthograd at progressively stronger levels during early training does not induce grokking, and efficiency remains smoothly decreasing. In the long-horizon experiments, Orthograd induces grokking, but the efficiency curves remain approximately monotonic and show no appreciable local increase at the grokking transition. These results suggest that, in our setting, grokking does not materially alter overall training-efficiency dynamics.

new Fuse-PINN: Combining Finite Element Methods and Physics-Informed Neural Networks to enable massively parallel simulations of non-linear PDEs

Authors: Stanislas Pamela, Nicolas Pailliez, Emmanuel Franck, Victor Michel-Dansac, Laurent Navoret, Vignesh Gopakumar

Abstract: Research in the domain of Physics-Informed Neural Networks applied to PDE modelling has advanced significantly in recent years, providing a wide ecosystem of novel methods. However, one frequent limitation is the inability of PINNs to scale out on High-Performance Computing architectures to address more ambitious challenges, particularly non-linear physics models with turbulent dynamics in large spatial domains. Typical studies restrain demonstrations to toy models with simple physics equations, few variables, and rarely any complex non-linear dynamics evolved over long timescales. Part of the AI community has even adopted the idea that "PINNs don't scale". This work proves otherwise. We present a mathematically robust formulation of Finite-Element-Method for Neural Networks (FEM-NN) implemented into a comprehensive, modular framework: Fuse-PINN. This formulation provides the basis for a sclable parallelisation of Physics-Informed Neural Networks on GPU-based HPC systems. Furthermore, an Energy Natural Gradient Descent optimiser is implemented to allow high-precision convergence. Fuse-PINN is demonstrated on physics models relevant to Magnetic Confinement Fusion, including non-linear convective models with fine-scale dynamics. Near-perfect scaling is achieved on multi-GPU runs with increasing problem size.

new PACT: A Decoder-Confidence-Guided Two-Round Receiver for Joint Channel and Phase-Noise Estimation in Sub-THz OFDM

Authors: Ruilin Wang, Xiaodai Dong

Abstract: Frequency-selective fading and oscillator phase noise (PN) jointly impair coded sub-THz OFDM reception. We propose a decoder-confidence-guided two-round receiver combining a {pilot-aware complex transformer} (PACT) estimator, banded linear minimum mean-square error equalization, and neural normalized min-sum decoding. PACT jointly estimates the frequency-domain channel and effective time-domain PN through interacting complex-valued branches. Decoder-derived pseudo-pilots and continuous constellation-aware confidence guide second-round refinement through PACT inputs and attention, with parameters shared across rounds. At $E_b/N_0=30$~dB, the PACT receiver achieves approximately $70\%$ and $57\%$ lower code-block error rates than the best-performing learning-based baseline in the two benchmark settings, respectively. Frozen-weight tests further demonstrate applicability across channel/PN models, carrier frequencies, and FFT/bandwidth configurations.

new Black-Box Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Ensemble-of-Ensembles

Authors: Wang Ma, Debarun Bhattacharjya, Junkyu Lee, Nhan H Pham, Harsha Kokel, Qiang Ji

Abstract: Reliable use of large language models (LLMs) requires uncertainty estimates that separate ambiguity intrinsic to a prompt from genuine knowledge gaps. Bayesian and deep-ensemble methods deliver this aleatoric/epistemic decomposition in principle, but are computationally prohibitive at LLM scale and require white-box access. Existing black-box methods, in contrast, summarize to a single scalar that cannot make the distinction. We introduce a two-level ensemble that bridges this gap. Stochastic decoding of a fixed prompt forms the inner ensemble; meaning-preserving semantic perturbations of the prompt form the outer ensemble. Variability is measured in a continuous embedding space, and the law of total covariance gives an exact within-/between-perturbation decomposition to estimate aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty (AU/EU); it requires only black-box sampling access. On top of this estimator, we contribute three theoretical results that ground the framework: (i) a finite-sample bias identity for nested Monte Carlo with a closed-form bias-corrected epistemic estimator; (ii) a Bayesian bridge bounding the gap between perturbation-population AU/EU and Bayesian targets; and (iii) a separation result identifying a class of failure modes that any consistency-only black-box method provably misses while perturbation-based EU detects. Across five short QA benchmarks and four instruction-tuned LLMs spanning two families and two scales, our estimator is competitive with strong white-box baselines, and at a matched generation budget it matches leading black-box methods on error ranking while decomposing AU and EU; controlled paired interventions show that AU and EU respond selectively to distinct, actionable sources of failure: prompt ambiguity and model knowledge gaps; and a long-form pilot on the NQ long-answer split shows the framework extends to claim-level factuality without retraining.

new Sample-efficient prediction of high-entropy alloy strength through outcome-aware robust sequential learning

Authors: Ahmed Shoyeb Raihan, Md Habibor Rahman, Ashish Nimbarte, Imtiaz Ahmed

Abstract: Accurate prediction of high-entropy alloy (HEA) strength can guide composition and processing decisions; however, obtaining tensile-test data for HEAs can be costly and experimentally demanding. This study proposes Robust Outcome-aware Sequential Experimentation for High-Entropy Alloys (ROSE-HEA), a sample-efficient framework for predicting ultimate tensile strength (UTS) from alloy composition and processing conditions. Its acquisition policy anticipates how possible UTS outcomes update predictions and the support for complementary Gaussian process (GP) descriptions. It then reassesses the worst-case expected predictive risk remaining across a reference domain to select informative candidates. A retrospective evaluation on 501 HEA records compares ROSE-HEA with expected-improvement (EI) and upper-confidence-bound (UCB) Bayesian optimization (BO), random GP sampling, and eight conventional regressors across 30 matched runs with shared initial observations and test conditions. Starting from 25 initial observations and acquiring 100 additional responses, ROSE-HEA achieves the lowest mean root-mean-square error (RMSE), at 152.84 MPa, and the highest mean R^2, at 0.7443. Mean RMSE is reduced by 6.16% and 6.37% relative to EI and UCB, respectively. The selected observations improve prediction across six regressor families and provide broader design-space coverage. ROSE-HEA also surpasses the strongest conventional regressor's mean predictive performance using 125 rather than 185 observations, corresponding to 32.4% fewer training observations. These findings demonstrate the potential of outcome-aware robust sequential learning to improve UTS prediction in HEAs under constrained training budgets.

new Asymptotic Margins and Feature Confinement in Conic Classification Heads under Softmax Cross-Entropy

Authors: Omer Nezih Gerek

Abstract: Classification heads built on conic functions give each class a bounded region of the feature space, and such regions are used to reject samples of unseen classes. We ask whether these regions also keep the features bounded when the head is trained with softmax cross-entropy. For a head whose class scores combine a linear term with a Euclidean norm, we first show that the region of each class is bounded by a quadric of revolution, whose eccentricity is the reciprocal of one learned ratio. We then attach to each class an asymptotic margin, which compares the growth of its score with the growth of all the other scores and can be computed by a second-order cone program. The loss of a class has infimum zero exactly when its margin is positive, and it then approaches zero only along diverging features. When the margin is negative, the loss is coercive and its minimizers lie in an explicit ball. Since softmax depends only on the differences between scores, a class with a bounded region can still have a positive margin, so bounded regions do not confine the features. The result extends to other norms, including the polyhedral conic functions. On CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, all 1210 cells of 67 trained conic heads have positive margins, whereas a prototype head keeps nine of its ten cells bounded, as the theory predicts.

new On numerical issues of divergences between nearly equal distributions

Authors: Brandon Amos

Abstract: Barron et al. (2026) recently identified numerical issues in the softmax and soft cross-entropy loss. We show that similar issues are present in standard divergence operations between categorical distributions. This includes the forward and reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence and generalized Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD), as well as Monte Carlo KL estimates and entropy. When the distributions are nearly equal, rounding and cancellation issues affect the forward and gradient computations. These issues can affect settings using divergences in optimization objectives, such as knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, and proximal methods. We propose numerically stable reformulations and provide example implementations for each setting, validated by property-based tests.

new WeightBench: Evaluating Language Models as Weight-Space Programmers

Authors: Aayush Marasini, Barsat Khadka, Nick Rahimi

Abstract: Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can operate directly on the parameters of other neural networks. They can read what a network was trained to do, fix a network that fails, and write working networks from scratch, all without gradient descent. This capability has been termed Weight-Space Programming (WSP). Exploring this new paradigm systematically requires a robust benchmark. We introduce WEIGHTBENCH, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs of increasing capability on 42 WSP tasks across five tracks: reading, fixing and writing weights, recognizing edits that are impossible, and data-free compression. The networks whose weights the LLMs read and edit range from 2K-parameter control policies to 7B-parameter language models. Experimental benchmark results show that frontier agents can already read small networks and write working control policies without ever running them, but cannot yet patch language models, compress them without data, or tell an impossible edit from a hard one. On all 42 tasks, Claude Opus 5.5 reaches a macro score of 0.535 (Sonnet 5.5 0.466, Haiku 5.5 0.402, DeepSeek v4-flash 0.215); on the 38 tasks it was run on, GPT-6.1-sol reaches 0.568 against Opus's 0.546. Code, the task registry, our OS-level sandbox harness, and the live leaderboard are publicly available at https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench.

URLs: https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench.

new Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion

Authors: Daniel Grimm, Kyra Jongman, Renato Cordeiro Ferreira, Giuseppe Cascavilla, Alfredo Cuzzocrea, Willem-Jan Van Den Heuvel

Abstract: Monitoring maritime activities is important for safety and security, particularly for detecting illegal activities, but remains challenging due to the size and complexity of the maritime domain. The Automatic Identification System (AIS) is commonly used for this purpose, despite its known vulnerabilities. In contrast, space-based radio frequency (RF) observations provide an independent source of vessel activity but lack ship identification and therefore cannot identify vessels on their own. Following the CRISP-DM process, this study applies a Pair Hidden Markov Model (PHMM) to align individual RF detections with AIS trajectories, using temporal and spatial information while modeling noise and timing discrepancies. We evaluate it on NOAA AIS data with synthetic RF detections against nearest-neighbor baselines and analyze its errors with bias tests on trajectory length, sampling density, stationarity and AIS--RF distance. The length-corrected Forward score reaches a top-1 precision of $0.7776$ on the validation split of multi-candidate detections, where errors are possible, and an AUC of $0.637$ for detecting dark vessels among the detections that pass the prefilter. With a single RF detection, the score mostly reflects the AIS trajectory itself, and its errors favor longer and more stationary trajectories. A log-odds score, which compares the full model with a null model without match transitions, raises precision to $0.8580$ and AUC to $0.852$. Its length-corrected variant no longer shows a significant bias towards stationary trajectories and short inter-message distances, but still favors candidates farther from the RF detection. A time-weighted nearest-neighbor baseline without fitting remains ahead (precision $0.9013$, AUC $0.904$), so this single-detection, hand-designed PHMM formulation approaches but does not beat it.

Abstract: Verifiers provide important reward signals for reinforcement learning of large language models (LLMs). cross Curvature-Aware Edge Dynamic GNNs for Low-$p_T$ Track Reconstruction at HL-LHC

Authors: Syed Haider Ali, Nouhaila Innan, Muhammad Shafique

Abstract: Particle track reconstruction in high-energy collider experiments is a computationally demanding combinatorial problem, traditionally solved by Kalman filters that scale poorly with increasing detector occupancy. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have recently emerged as a promising alternative. In this work, we introduce the Manifold Helical-IN, a novel Interaction Network architecture that explicitly embeds detector spacepoints onto a helical manifold chart using curvature-aware features. Furthermore, the model maintains good track coherence, as quantified by our proposed Mean Hit Recovery Fraction (MHRF) of 0.858, and achieves a highly efficient inference latency of approximately 150 ms per event on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU. These results represent a critical step toward real-time soft particle tracking for the High-Level Trigger (HLT). cross Enumerating Posterior-Supported Priors: First-Order Error of Finite-Integral Cross-Categorical Measurement Morphisms

Authors: Ning Ni, Yongxin Zhu

Abstract: In generative modeling, standard measure theory adopts the a.e. convention, which legitimately ignores any error component carried by a null-support set: a single null element can hide out-of-domain error inside null support and never be explicitly accounted for. The contribution of this paper is not a new distributional distance, but rather a verifiable structure that writes down which underlying predicate supports a high-level object, when it is invertible, and at which layer the error resides, from which we propose the enumerating posterior-supported prior framework. Four results consolidate into Thm. 7: binary quotient-partition meta-categorical objects support the set categorical functional along categorical support via measure descent--recovery, enumerating the morphism itself rather than its preimage and object; their multiplicity is normalized through replica-index injection and accounted as an explicitly first-order error. The contribution of this paper is a transformation from nontrivial-input construction -> engineering-computable first-order output: the binary quotient-partition meta-categorical objects, supported via categorical support measure descent--recovery, constitute a substantive nontrivial input---precisely because they lift the out-of-domain error that hid inside null support into a first-order falsifiable coefficient mu_err, a finitely obtainable finite integral first-order error.

cross Can LLMs Write Backdoors? An Empirical Security Study of Weight-Space Programming

Authors: Zhaojia Wang, Jiucui Lu, Zheng Li

Abstract: Recent advances in weight-space programming (WSP) have demonstrated that large language models (LLMs) can directly inspect and modify neural network weights without conventional gradient-based training. While this capability opens new possibilities for model improvement, it also raises an important security question: Can LLMs exploit weight-space programming to inject backdoors into neural networks? To answer this question, we present an empirical security study of LLM-driven weight-space programming for backdoor injection. Specifically, we investigate whether general-purpose LLMs can directly modify the weights of target models according to natural-language instructions, without accessing task-specific data, performing forward passes, or computing gradients. We evaluate five LLMs as weight editors across three target architectures: an MLP on MNIST, ResNet-18 on CIFAR-10, and Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct on AG News. Our experiments reveal three key findings. First, LLM-driven weight editing can successfully inject backdoors across different architectures while largely preserving clean performance. Second, successful attacks can require remarkably few parameter modifications, including only six parameters for the MLP and 0.02% of the weights for a fine-tuned Qwen model. Third, editing capabilities vary substantially across LLMs and target architectures: although all five LLMs successfully backdoor the MLP, only GPT-6 Astra succeeds across all three targets. These findings highlight an emerging security risk of weight-space programming and underscore the importance of safeguarding LLM-driven weight editing.

Authors: Ibragim Badertdinov, Alexander Golubev

Abstract: Standard coding-agent benchmarks primarily evaluate binary task success, treating execution as a black box and obscuring the behaviors that produce those outcomes. cross Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search

Authors: Maitree Meher, Siming He, S. Shankar Sastry, Jitendra Malik, Claire Tomlin, Jonas Frey

Abstract: A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. Yet vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. We study budget-conditioned object search: given a target category and an explicit action budget, an agent must find as many distinct target instances as possible before the budget expires. We investigate how to supervise VLNs for this task by comparing a privileged TSP expert, which plans near-optimal routes using the full map and true target locations, with a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability using high-level cross-scene semantic priors. Despite the TSP expert achieving substantially higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration: on held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN consistently outperforms the TSP-supervised VLN, with the advantage becoming more pronounced at longer budgets. At 2000 steps (500m), the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable target instances, compared with 65.5% for the TSP-supervised VLN and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results highlight a distinction between optimal routing and optimal exploration: effective exploration supervision should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate routes that are optimal only once the environment is fully known.

Authors: Andrea Borghesi, Julia van Beurden, Maarten van Alphen, Michiel van den Brekel, Luc Karssemakers, Jan-Jakob Sonke, Baris Karakullukcu, Jonas Teuwen, George Yiasemis

Abstract: 2D-to-3D image registration is a key component for several medical imaging purposes such as intraoperative guidance, enabling the alignment between preoperative 3D volumes and real-time 2D images. While conventional optimisation-based methods often struggle with non-convex objectives, sensitivity to initialisation, and high computational demands, deep learning (DL) has emerged as a promising alternative, offering improved alignment robustness and the potential for real-time performance. This review provides a comprehensive analysis of DL frameworks developed to address the challenges inherent in 2D-to-3D registration. We first outline how core concepts of DL are adapted to the registration problem. We then examine recent methodological advancements from multiple perspectives, including pipeline design, architectural innovations, and domain adaptation strategies. Particular attention is given to quantitative metrics, highlighting how performance is defined and measured across studies. We conclude by identifying the absence of per-case uncertainty estimation and the strong neglect of temporal or sequential modelling as the two methodological gaps most consequential for clinical readiness, and discuss state-space models as a way to add temporal memory within intraoperative latency budgets. cross Creating Steering Vectors via Input Optimisation

Authors: Batuhan Akar

Abstract: Activation steering influences a language model's behaviour by adding a steering vector to its residual stream during generation, but it requires white-box access to modify the hidden states at every generation step. We introduce ActGCG, a modification of Greedy Coordinate Gradient which optimises a suffix to match the target activations obtained by adding a steering vector to the residual stream. cross Convergence Properties of PF-Exec: Homogeneous Structure, Anchored Closed Form, and Gain Dynamics

Authors: Hanzhang Jia, Liheng Zeng, Yiran Li, Yi Gao, Bo Ma

Abstract: This paper establishes a layered mathematical char acterisation of PF-Exec, the algorithm of the companion system paper. Demand-proportional bandwidth allocation yields an exact first-order homogeneous decomposition, from which the equal-load bandwidth target and its feasibility bound follow in closed form. The logarithmic knee view preserves strict load order and shares its global optimum with the Jain fairness index, while the demand-weighted centroid minimises the position potential. cross An Operationalization of Opaque Serial Depth

Authors: Nathan Sheffield, Alek Westover, Lukas Finnveden, Alexa Pan, Julian Stastny, Ryan Greenblatt

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) is currently a valuable tool for overseeing AI models, but some architectural shifts could significantly reduce CoT monitorability. We operationalize a measure that serves as a proxy for the amount of unverbalized serial cognition a model can perform, as a specific instantiation of the notion of opaque serial depth: roughly, the length of the longest path through a model's computation that does not pass through an interpretable bottleneck. Our main contribution is a particular standard for what counts as such a bottleneck. We define natural-language-rooted (NL-rooted) nodes as nodes that were initialized from a pre-training prior to produce human-understandable outputs, whose output space has not been expanded beyond that prior, and whose outputs the model has not been modified to interpret as a different type of data. By construction, the resulting measure, NLS depth, can be adjudicated before training from an architecture, training recipe, and deployment configuration alone, and is insensitive to ordinary post-training such as prompting, distillation, and RL. We find that, across open-weight models, NLS depth correlates strongly with layer count, with some exceptions, and has grown slowly over recent years. It would likely grow slowly under continued scaling of standard transformers, while latent-reasoning architectures such as full-bandwidth transformers could increase it by a large amount. We work through how NLS depth applies to a range of existing and proposed architectures, discuss alternative operationalizations, and give a worked example bounding the depth of Gemma3-1B. We argue that AI developers should track and report the NLS depth of their models as a complement to direct measurements of CoT monitorability.

Abstract: Continuous blood pressure (BP) monitoring could improve hypertension management by capturing changes that intermittent cuff measurements miss. cross Multifidelity Operator Inference for Dynamical Systems

Authors: Vignesh Sella, Anirban Chaudhuri, Karen Willcox

Abstract: This paper presents a multifidelity (MF) formulation of Operator Inference (OpInf) for learning non-intrusive, physics-based reduced-order models (ROMs) of nonlinear dynamical systems. OpInf learns reduced operators from projections of snapshot data by solving a least squares regression problem. However, generating high-fidelity snapshots can be computationally expensive, limiting parameter coverage and the temporal extent of training trajectories for many aerospace applications, which can lead to inaccurate or unstable ROM predictions. This work proposes MF-OpInf, which combines limited high-fidelity snapshots with more plentiful low-fidelity snapshots through a control-variates-inspired MF least squares formulation to learn a single set of reduced operators across multiple trajectories. Low-fidelity trajectories provide additional parameter coverage and extend beyond the high-fidelity training time horizon. The low-fidelity snapshots are also used to construct an improved reduced basis through weighted proper orthogonal decomposition. We demonstrate MF-OpInf on a nonlinear reaction-diffusion problem with parametrized sinusoidal forcing and a two-dimensional incompressible flow past a cylinder with a parametrized inlet pulse. In these experiments, under an equal training simulation budget, MF-OpInf reduces the mean relative prediction error at unseen parameters by up to $87\%$ for the reaction-diffusion problem and $28\%$ for the cylinder flow problem over the test time regime, which extends beyond the high-fidelity training time horizon. These results demonstrate that MF-OpInf can improve temporal extrapolation and prediction at unseen parameters without increasing the training simulation budget.

cross Model Annealing: Data-Efficient Learning through Review and Consolidation

Authors: Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Rezvan Abouzadeh

Abstract: A small labelled dataset is memorized long before the training budget runs out, and the one-hot labels that remain to be fitted then mostly sharpen confidence. Model annealing spends the rest of the budget on review and consolidation, relearning from the network's own knowledge. Training is divided into episodes whose learning rate is raised again and cooled, every cooled state joins a memory of past selves, and each new episode learns from the labels, from the tempered predictions of this memory, and from adversarial dreams sought where student and memory disagree most. The memory averages predictions rather than weights, and the deployed network is the last student, so inference costs nothing extra. Across CIFAR-100, Tiny-ImageNet and Imagewoof, four architecture families and 5\% to 100\% of the labels, model annealing raises the top-1 accuracy of the AdamW-trained ConvNeXt, ViT and MLP-Mixer over cross-entropy training in 28 of 33 configurations, by 1.3 points on average, and is on par in accuracy with snapshot distillation, the closest competitor. On these families it is better than both in likelihood, calibration and robustness to corruptions and small adversarial perturbations. For an SGD-trained ResNet-18 it lowers accuracy but still improves robustness to small adversarial perturbations. Separating the reheated schedule from relearning shows that relearning helps every family and that the losses come from reheats, which undo most of this network's fit to its training set. How early the accuracy of a cross-entropy run saturates largely separates the AdamW families from ResNet-18 but does not predict the gain within them. With little data, relearning from the latest snapshot without dreams, as snapshot distillation does, compounds overconfidence in the AdamW families from episode to episode. The memory and the dreams each curb most of this drift, and together they remove it.

cross User-Schedulable Approximations for Quantized Tensor Programs

Authors: Alex Reinking

Abstract: Quantization lets language models run on edge devices, but it sharply increases the number of tensor kernels a library must maintain: every weight format must be paired with every activation format on every instruction set and GPU, with an encoder and a decoder that agree on the stored bytes. In GGML, the tensor library used by llama.cpp, these kernels are written by hand, and only 8 of the 54 weight-activation pairs it runs on our Arm CPU have a tiled matrix-multiplication kernel. We extend Halide's separation of algorithm and schedule to the representation of values. Each approximation is a codec, an encoder paired with a decoder, composed from reusable units: quantizers, integer representations, bit packers and layouts. A new directive, approximate_by, applies a codec to one stage of a program and returns the encoder and decoder as ordinary stages that Halide can schedule, so both decoders fuse into the register tiles of a matrix product. Optional contracts, such as a round-trip law, are checked by property tests; the law exposed an inconsistent block sum in GGML's q8_1. One algorithm and one schedule library generate 347 kernels for 31 of GGML's and KleidiAI's formats on Arm CPUs and Apple GPUs, covering 38 weight-activation pairs for which GGML has no kernel, in 3.7x less code than GGML and KleidiAI. On an Apple M3 Max, our kernels reach at least 95% of the speed of GGML's format-specific kernels in 91.0% of the cases that have one, with a median speedup of 1.13x, and are faster still where GGML falls back to blocked dot products. Counting Apple's BLAS library, which is usually faster for larger f32 products, we still reach that threshold in 86.9% of CPU cases. A port to x86 with Intel's AMX adds 793 lines of code and matches GGML in 83.5% of its cases.

cross Learning Latent Goal Costs for Visual World-Model Planning with an Expanding Curriculum

Authors: Konstantinos Mylonas, Thrasyvoulos Spyropoulos

Abstract: Image-goal planning with a frozen visual world model depends on how predicted observations are scored against the goal. Mean-squared error (MSE) between embeddings from a pretrained visual encoder can provide a useful signal near a goal, yet favor states across an obstacle that are visually similar to the goal and difficult to reach. We fit a small residual adapter on top of frozen I-JEPA embeddings to improve this latent goal cost. Starting near each goal, a planner collects trajectories from progressively wider regions of initial states. The adapter learns from the order of observations along successful trajectories, with additional comparisons inferred from failed rollouts. No task-specific progress labels are used for fitting, so the procedure can be applied to other goal-reaching tasks. DotWall and PointMaze are used in this work. In paired 50-case evaluations with identical starts, goals, world models, and planner settings, success increases from 54% to 98% in DotWall and from 82% to 96% in PointMaze. The results indicate that planning experience can improve a frozen visual representation's goal cost without retraining the encoder or the world model.

cross Uncertainty-Aware Continual Learning for 2D XRD Interpretation Using Simulated and Experimental Data

Authors: Zihan Wang, Hyun Sang Park, Ali Rachidi, David Elbert, Todd C. Hufnagel, Wei Chen

Abstract: Autonomous experimentation systems require characterization models that can interpret heterogeneous measurements and maintain predictive performance as data accumulate. Developing such models involves addressing domain mismatch between data sources, overconfident predictions on unfamiliar inputs, and catastrophic forgetting during sequential updates. In this work, we present a proof-of-concept framework for uncertainty-aware continual interpretation of two-dimensional X-ray diffraction (XRD) data. Predictive uncertainty is also evaluated on unfamiliar and noisy inputs. The contribution is the integration and evaluation of these capabilities for sequential XRD interpretation. cross Contrastive local learning in large nonlinear analog circuits

Authors: Pragyan Pandey, Peter Wu, Deema Alyahya, Eric Y. Ma

Abstract: Machine learning in physical hardware promises to reduce training cost by colocating computation, memory and adaptation within device physics. Contrastive local learning networks (CLLNs) offer a compelling route: an electrical circuit relaxes to equilibrium in free and weakly clamped states, and each adaptive element updates from local voltage contrasts rather than backpropagated error signals. Yet whether nonlinear transistor implementations can scale beyond proof-of-concept circuits, and what design principles control that scaling, have remained unknown. Here we show that nonlinear analog circuits trained by contrastive local rules can learn larger machine-learning tasks and remain robust under hardware nonidealities. Using device-level circuit simulations, we systematically explore initialization, operating regime, loss-dependent clamping and training parameters, and scale CLLNs beyond ten thousand adaptive edges. The same local rule trains classifiers that reach the accuracy regime of conventional digital methods on Ionosphere and handwritten-digit benchmarks, and a network that autoregressively generates language over a 122-token vocabulary. These results establish the feasibility of local learning in large nonlinear analog circuits.

cross Benchmarks Don't Talk Back: Learning Through Self-Disagreement

Authors: Soheil Abbasloo, Peng Cheng

Abstract: A benchmark usually measures a language model alone with a question, and deployment rarely looks like that: answers get contested, and models give up correct ones under counter-reasoning that is itself wrong. We show that what fails there is not a shortfall of accuracy but a capability in its own right -- knowing when to hold a position and when to yield to a better one -- which we call dialectical competence. It stands on first-answer accuracy without following from it: a model can be made materially better at answering and no better at this, and training for accuracy alone can drift against it. Nor can it be bought at inference, by self-reflection or by sampling more. Taking a cue from the dialectical tradition in philosophy, in which productive opposition arises within a position rather than being imposed on it from outside, we hypothesize that this capability can be trained from opposition the model supplies itself. It states a position, generates the strongest case against that position -- its antithesis -- rules on whether the case stands, and is trained on that ruling, with no second model at training or at deployment. We call this learning through Self-Disagreement: reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards throughout, where the same check that scores an answer against its reference scores the ruling too, so no reward model, no preference data and no human annotation enter anywhere. The capability is trained rather than elicited: weak in the base model, it arrives with the reward, is not paid for out of first-answer accuracy, and holds against challengers the model never trained against, including ones stronger than itself. The opposition a model needs in order to improve, it turns out, need not come from outside it.

cross Performance-Portable Exact Gaussian Process Regression in Python via SYCL and oneMath

Authors: Alexander Strack, Marcel Graf, Alexander Van Craen, Dirk Pfl\"uger

Abstract: Modern multi-vendor GPU environments force a choice between portable code and native performance. GPRat is an open-source Python library for exact Gaussian process regression built on the HPX asynchronous many-task runtime, tiled algorithms, and CPU/CUDA BLAS backends. We contribute a UXL Foundation oneMath backend with a hybrid HPX/SYCL design, extending GPRat to AMD and Intel GPUs. The new SYCL backend comes within 3% of native CUDA on an NVIDIA A30 at the largest problem size and outperforms the GPflow and GPyTorch libraries by up to 1.75x and 2.17x on the A30 and up to 1.79x and 4.62x on an AMD Instinct MI210. On an Intel Arc B580, a consumer-class card with FP64 throughput below our CPUs, the unmodified SYCL backend trails GPRat's CPU backend. Overall, oneMath's dispatch to vendor-native BLAS lets a single SYCL codebase match CUDA on NVIDIA and outperform established Python GP frameworks on AMD, without vendor-specific code.

cross Tractable Importance Sampling for Monte Carlo Privacy Accounting

Authors: Galen Andrew, Arun Ganesh

Abstract: Evaluating tight Differential Privacy (DP) parameters for DP matrix factorization with privacy amplification by sampling requires analyzing high-dimensional, correlated Gaussian mixtures. Because integrating these mixture distributions to find the exact privacy failure probability ($\delta$) is analytically intractable, these mechanisms are typically analyzed using Monte Carlo accounting. However, standard sampling suffers from prohibitive variance when estimating small $\delta$ associated with strict privacy guarantees, which in turn requires a large sample complexity, making Monte Carlo accounting inefficient. We demonstrate that Monte Carlo accounting can be made much more efficient via importance sampling. We give a shifted importance sampling proposal distribution parameterized by a stretch factor $\alpha > 1$. We prove a closed-form bound $B(\alpha) < 1$ on the support of the importance-weighted estimator that holds identically for both directions of the hockey-stick divergence, show that $B$ is log-convex so that the optimal $\alpha$ is a one-dimensional root-finding problem, and show that the resulting estimator seamlessly plugs into the KL-Hoeffding verification protocol used by past work, with a sample complexity reduced by exactly a factor of $1/B(\alpha^*)$.

cross Choosing Quantized Code LLMs by File Size and Energy per Solved Task

Authors: Aditi Patodiya

Abstract: A developer running a code assistant on a laptop downloads one model file, and local inference moves the energy of every request onto that machine. cross More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? Untangling Coordination, Ensembling, and Test-Time Compute in Multi-Agent LLMs

Authors: Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi

Abstract: Evaluations of multi-agent language models often compare communicating architectures against a single greedy pass. Against baselines with a single pass (Category A), multi-agent accuracy differences are negative overall and near zero when omitting the largest contributing study. When evaluated against baselines matched on sample count (Category B) or resource budget (Category C), multi-agent systems average -0.042 in accuracy, and multilevel random effects modeling on the log odds scale remains negative across sensitivity checks. cross LLM-Guided Cluster Lifecycle Management: Deciding When and How to Evolve Behavioral Clusters

Authors: Himanshu Kulkarni, Siddharth Krishnamurthy, Jianhong Xin

Abstract: Behavioral clusters learned over user or item interactions drift as behavior evolves: some fragment into sub-behaviors, some converge, some disappear, and new patterns emerge. Two paradigms dominate the maintenance of evolving clusterings. Threshold triggers fire a pre-specified action when a hardcoded statistic crosses a cutoff; refit-and-track (MONIC-style) re-clusters every window and infers transitions from how the new clusters align with the previous window's. Both hardwire the decision into a threshold or a re-clustering rule. We study an alternative in which a large language model (LLM) makes the lifecycle decision in place. Given a threshold-free, whole-population view of the current cluster set (statistical health, labels, member texts, residual mass), the LLM decides per cluster whether and how to act (split, merge, retire, create, or no-action) without re-clustering. Because the useful signal channel depends on how informative the labels are, we fix a richness-conditioned policy in advance: statistics when label distinctiveness is low, fused statistics+semantics when it is high. On three public datasets spanning label richness (YooChoose 0.17, MOOC 0.50, News-Category 0.70), the policy-selected LLM mode obtains the highest F1 on all three, with far fewer interventions and stronger restraint than tuned threshold heuristics and a MONIC steelman (paired Wilcoxon p <= 0.003, bootstrap 95% CIs excluding zero). The gain is concentrated more in precision than recall. The useful modality shifts from statistics to semantics/fusion as richness rises. Semantics hurt on YooChoose, fusion is roughly neutral on MOOC, and the semantic channel is required on News. One base prompt template and one fixed policy serve all three datasets, whereas the tuned baseline collapses when moved between them. We treat this shift as a practitioner diagnostic observed on three datasets, not a validated law.
