cross Finite speed Ornstein-Uhlenbeck-Kac processes and their applications in generative modeling

Authors: Richard Duong, Janis Auffenberg, Gabriele Steidl

Abstract: We study the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck-Kac (OU-K) process, where instead of a Brownian motion, a finite-speed Kac process defines the driving process. The OU-K process can be analyzed through a rescaled, time-inhomogeneous Kac process, characterized by a switching rate from the Kac part, and a convergence rate from the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) part. The law of this inhomogeneous Kac process describes the solution of a wave equation, the Euler-Poisson-Darboux (EPD) equation. For Kac switching rates being multiples of the OU convergence rate, we can explicitly derive this EPD solution via the sum of (the derivative of) two Legendre polynomials, restricted between two Dirac points representing the wave fronts. Using the framework of piecewise-deterministic Markov processes (PDMP), we describe the conditional velocity fields of the OU-K process via our polynomial-law-formula. This allows us to apply the OU-K process in a generative flow model. Since the OU-K velocity fields prove to be bounded by the finite Kac speed, the usual time truncations of diffusion models become unnecessary. We demonstrate advantages over diffusion and flow matching models in terms of generation sharpness on singular data sets, while achieving competitive results on CIFAR-10 image generation.

cross Binarization as an Attack Surface in Weightless Neural Networks

Authors: Lucas Senos Coutinho, Matheus Vargas, Pedro Teixeira, Heudson Tosta Mirandola, Ori Rottenstreich, Priscila Machado Vieira Lima, Leandro Santiago, Daniel Sadoc Menasche

Abstract: Weightless Neural Networks (WNNs) are lightweight classifiers that binarize their inputs and learn by storing binary patterns in lookup memories, which makes them attractive for resource-constrained tasks such as intrusion detection. Because a small perturbation that crosses no binarization threshold leaves the binary input unchanged, WNNs may appear naturally robust to adversarial examples; a perturbation that does cross a threshold, however, can still change the prediction. We study this tension in WiSARD, the canonical RAM-based WNN, and in two variants that replace its memories with Bloom filters, Bloom WiSARD and BTHOWeN. A continuous perturbation reaches these classifiers only by crossing an encoding threshold, changing at least one binary tuple, and redirecting a memory query; we formalize this mechanism with a threshold-crossing bound and a response-margin certificate for standard Boolean WiSARD. Controlled MNIST experiments compare linear and data-driven thermometer encodings under model-agnostic sensitivity probes, a score-based Simulated Annealing attack, and surrogate-transfer attacks. Changing only the encoding while keeping each model otherwise fixed shows that encoding granularity can trade a small clean-accuracy loss for a large decrease in attack success. On the Bot-IoT, CICIDS, and UNSW-NB15 intrusion-detection datasets, adversarial perturbations lead WNN detectors to classify many attacks as normal traffic and to raise false alarms, even when their aggregate accuracy remains high.

cross Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor: A Predictive Law for Perception Noise and Its Closed Loop Cost

Authors: Christopher Malili

Abstract: Imitation policies for robot manipulation are often trained on noisy object positions, and they learn to discount the noisy reading. We show how much they discount it, and why. Because demonstrators steer toward the object, the robot's own state carries information about where the object is. A trained policy combines this proprioceptive anchor with the reading and weights the reading by $\alpha = V/(V + s^2)$, where $s^2$ is the reading noise and $V$ the anchor's error variance. $V$ belongs to the learner and is far smaller than a linear model of the demonstrations implies, which is why classical errors-in-variables (EIV) regression overestimates $\alpha$. Measured once, $V$ predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners and four new noise levels, with mean absolute error 0.025 against 0.37 for classical EIV, and a parameter-free bias test rules out the simpler explanation that the policies just overestimate the noise. The discount does not carry over to deployment, where the policy produces its own state: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded $\alpha$ in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, although the stricter registered prediction, that all would follow the offset fully, held for 8 of them. Post hoc, lower $\alpha$ went with lower success, steeply for diffusion, and a registered prospective test confirmed this for diffusion on Can at two new noise levels: the measured $\alpha$ predicted success before any rollout, within 0.03 (95% intervals $\pm 0.08$), although training noise predicts it equally well. Every test was registered before its data, including the classical benchmark that failed, and all other analyses are labelled post hoc; all results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise.

cross Learn then Bet: Learning Interpretable Rules for Reliable Agent Intervention

Authors: You-Lin Chen, Xinyi Lisa Chen, Pedro Herrero-Vidal, Xiaojing Yu, Bingqing Song, Bin Xu

Abstract: Autonomous agents now run for hundreds of steps with little or no human supervision. In practice, practitioners use runtime monitors such as turn limits and keyword filters to decide when a run should be stopped, upgraded, or assisted. However, these heuristics may stop runs that would otherwise succeed or allow failing runs to continue. To address this limitation, we introduce LeBet (Learn then Bet), a statistical learning framework that derives its learning objective from sufficient conditions for valid and powerful intervention. In particular, LeBet learns an interpretable OR-of-AND monitor and accumulates evidence through a fixed betting statistic, intervening when the evidence reaches a prescribed threshold. Under finite within-class exchangeability and leave-one-out stability, we derive finite-sample guarantees for false-positive control (validity) and detection power conditional on the training data, using the same trajectories for learning and calibration without assuming independent and identically distributed trajectories. cross Provable Cross-Modal Transfer of Hierarchical Concept Structure in CLIP Embeddings

Authors: Nghia Nguyen, Tianjiao Ding, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, Ren\'e Vidal

Abstract: Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP are trained to align images and text, not to represent taxonomies. Yet empirical studies show that CLIP embeddings exhibit hierarchical structure aligned with the hierarchy of semantic concepts. A theoretical characterization of why such hierarchy emerges from a non-hierarchical objective remains open. In this paper, we formalize this phenomenon as Hierarchical Angular Separation (HAS), a geometric property whereby each non-leaf concept embedding is more similar to its descendants than to off-branch concepts. Empirically, we show that HAS holds approximately in raw images and randomly initialized CLIP image embeddings, while in text embeddings it holds approximately only after contrastive learning. To theoretically explain this asymmetry, we analyze an unconstrained features model in which the image embeddings are fixed and only the text embeddings are trained. At the global minimizer, the text-side HAS violation percentage at each node is at most the image-side HAS violation and near-violation percentage. The key mechanism is the hierarchical co-occurrence structure of image-text pairs, which biases text embeddings toward root-centered image prototypes. For a linear contrastive loss, we derive a closed-form solution that makes this mechanism explicit. We extend the analysis to the InfoNCE loss and establish analogous guarantees under sufficient regularization. Synthetic experiments validate the theory and show that HAS is transferred even beyond the regimes covered by our analysis.

cross RaFL: An Empirical Study of Random Filter Losses for Image Reconstruction

Authors: Ruibo Wang, Fengbei Liu, Aleksei Tiulpin, Mert R. Sabuncu, Heejong Kim

Abstract: Pixel losses train image reconstruction networks toward a point statistic of the target distribution, often averaging away fine textures under uncertainty. Perceptual losses alleviate this issue using pretrained features, but those features are inherently domain-specific. We study a random filter loss (RaFL) that compares prediction and target through filters with random weights, avoiding both pretrained features and task-specific loss engineering. RaFL combines random convolutions, random Fourier features, and random wavelet filters, automatically balancing their contributions through gradient-norm matching, eliminating manual loss-weight tuning and grid search. We systematically investigate three design choices: filter family, weight resampling schedule, and weight initialization. Based on validation PSNR and stability across random seeds, we select the configuration that uses all three filter families, redraws filters every optimization step, and employs Gaussian initialization. We evaluate RaFL on three 2D and two 3D image reconstruction tasks. RaFL outperforms a pixel loss on three tasks and matches it on two, and demonstrates stable performance across random seeds on all five tasks. RaFL adds less than 2% to the backbone's forward FLOPs and no inference cost, requiring $182\times$ fewer loss FLOPs than a VGG19 perceptual loss. These findings suggest that RaFL is a simple, tuning-free, and domain-agnostic loss objective for 2D and 3D image reconstruction.

cross Learning Anisotropic Prediction Targets for Flow Models

Authors: Rayhan Zirvi, Hansheng Chen, Gordon Wetzstein, Leonidas Guibas

Abstract: Flow-based generative models are trained on a hand-chosen prediction target, such as the velocity, the noise, or the clean data. This choice strongly affects training and sample quality. We introduce Anisotropic Flow Models (AnisoFlow), which replaces a fixed prediction target with a fully learnable one. AnisoFlow learns to scale the noise component of the target anisotropically, assigning a separate nonnegative weight to each direction in a jointly learned basis. A closed-form map recovers the velocity from the network's prediction, leaving the architecture, flow-matching loss, and sampler unchanged. Since recovery is exact, training optimizes the target purely to make it easier for the network to predict. In pixel-space text-to-image generation, AnisoFlow improves image quality and, through better-conditioned velocity recovery, largely removes patch-seam artifacts under reduced-precision inference.

Abstract: Typhoon is an audiovisual installation based on Joseph Conrad's novella, in which Captain MacWhirr steers the steamer Nan-Shan directly into and through a catastrophic storm. cross Beyond Prediction Gain: LASR as a Metric for Action-Grounded Latents in World Models

Authors: Yu Zhou, Changsong Lei, Yixiang Wei, Kairui Wen, Huankun Sheng, Ming Li, Yeying Fan, Yu-Hui Wen, Yong-Jin Liu

Abstract: World models that support planning, control, and interpretable reasoning require latent representations that genuinely encode action. Yet the large-scale video data from which such models are learned overwhelmingly lack action annotations. Latent-action models infer action-like variables from observation sequences, enabling action-aware learning from unlabeled videos or trajectories. A fundamental concern, however, remains: a latent that improves future prediction may simply encode the current state, episode progress, task identity, or other predictive shortcuts. Existing evaluation practices---reconstruction loss, rollout fidelity, action recovery, or downstream performance---cannot isolate this distinction. We introduce the Latent Action Support Ratio (LASR), a dimensionless metric that measures, under offline evaluation on held-out data, whether a learned latent provides predictive support for action-related targets beyond the current state and non-action alternatives. LASR provides two complementary summaries: GeoLASR captures typical support, while WorstLASR identifies the weakest case. We instantiate the LASR benchmark with recorded actions and corresponding physical state changes as targets, episode-held-out splits, one predictor type for all comparisons, and positive and negative controls. Experiments on recorded robot manipulation data and a matched multi-task panel demonstrate that LASR detects known action information, rejects representative non-action shortcuts, and enables graded comparisons among imperfect latent representations. We further use LASR to train and audit a lightweight residual adapter, obtaining descriptively better point estimates whose uncertainty is assessed with paired comparisons. LASR thus provides a principled offline protocol for measuring action-related predictive support in world-model latents.

cross LuthorSQL: A One-Pass Text-to-SQL Model as System 1 for a Slower LLM Agent

Authors: Ibrahim Memon, Armin Karamzade, M. Hanif Jhaveri, Roy Fox

Abstract: Frontier LLM agents that query databases with tools are among the most accurate text-to-SQL systems, but per-query calls to a hosted model are slow, costly, and expose sensitive organizational queries to external APIs. For example, a Claude Opus 5.5 agent serving 10,000 queries a day costs over $70,000 a year at list price. To what extent can local hardware already owned by an organization offload this burden without compromising the agent's accuracy? We present LuthorSQL, a non-autoregressive 7B text-to-SQL model that serves as a rapid System 1 gate for a slower LLM agent (System 2), escalating queries only when uncertain. On its own, LuthorSQL reaches 68.03% execution accuracy on BIRD's hidden test set at 0.84 s per question on a single GPU, maintains its accuracy in 9 GB with 8-bit quantized weights, and transfers zero-shot to Snowflake, BigQuery, MySQL, PostgreSQL, and DuckDB within 3.1 points of its native SQLite accuracy. Combined with Claude Opus 5.5 in a pre-registered confidence cascade over 227 questions on databases published after Opus's training cutoff, LuthorSQL answers nearly half of all questions locally and cuts API cost by 33.3% at the same accuracy. Finally, contamination probing shows that Opus reproduces 48-92% of public benchmark gold queries verbatim without seeing the schema; however, its tool-assisted accuracy holds on renamed schemas and reaches 95.6-97.4% on post-cutoff databases. Code and data: https://github.com/IAMIbrahimmemon/luthorsql

cross AutoRL: A Multi-Agent System towards Autonomous Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Simon Tang, Wenjie Shi, Yongjing Yin, Keheng Wang, Jialin Liu, Zheng Zhang, Linvo Lin, Xiangyuan Xue, Kan Ren, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is one of the most promising paths toward an AI singularity for superintelligence. Achieving this vision requires AI systems to undertake the full training pipeline for iterative improvement. Reinforcement learning (RL) plays an essential role in this cycle to improve model capabilities and generalization, yet automating RL remains difficult due to unstable training dynamics and the need for sustained supervision. We introduce AutoRL, a multi-agent system for automating RL training, as a cornerstone for future RSI. Different from conducting research by end-to-end complete experiments, AutoRL monitors the evolving training state and integrates process-level supervision and research into an ongoing RL training, following the practical workflow of an RL researcher. Specifically, AutoRL consists of three RL-specific agentic roles. Perceptors track training dynamics, rollouts, evaluation, and efficiency, triggering deliberation when they detect anomalies, degradation, or stagnation. Three complementary Proposers diagnose problems and refine candidate interventions through shared evidence and checks. The Orchestrator reviews their recommendation and applies approved changes through online hot updates or checkpoint-based resumption. Experiments on Qwen3 and LongCat-Flash-Lite demonstrate the superiority of AutoRL over static RL recipes and a general agent. The research records show that existing agents can handle unattended RL supervision and dynamic adjustment. The difficulty for future RSI lies in developing fundamental methods that are valuable for resource-consuming validation.

cross Multimodal Person Concept Model for Person Identification under Varying Observation Conditions

Authors: Mizuho Okitani, Yoshinobu Hagiwara

Abstract: Service robots in domestic and office environments must identify users to provide personalized services based on their preferences and previous interactions. cross Hierarchical Bayesian physics-augmented neural networks for populations of hyperelastic laws: specimen variability and uncertainty from scarce, noisy data

Authors: Vincent C. Scholz, Phaedon-Stelios Koutsourelakis

Abstract: The hyperelastic law of a material such as soft tissue differs between specimens, and often only a few specimens can be tested. Physics-augmented neural networks (PANNs) learn such laws from data without a prescribed functional form while keeping them physically admissible, and Bayesian versions of related models quantify the uncertainty of the law of one specimen or of a pooled group of specimens. A pooled fit, however, attributes the differences between specimens to measurement noise or to uncertainty about a mean law, and neither describes a new specimen. We first develop a Bayesian PANN for one specimen, whose priors keep the learned law plausible for few, noisy data. We then extend it to a hierarchical Bayesian PANN, a nonlinear mixed-effects model in which all specimens share learned polyconvex features and each specimen is a draw from a population of laws. Features, specimens, and population are inferred jointly by variational inference, with a separate model of the measurement noise, so that the variability between specimens is not mistaken for noise, the population carries its own uncertainty, and every specimen drawn from it is polyconvex. The population predicts the response of new specimens with credible intervals, without data or from a partial test, and assigns them to cohorts with quantified uncertainty, all without a prescribed form of the law. On synthetic populations, it recovers the population from few specimens and predicts new specimens more reliably than three published methods. On Treloar's rubber and on healthy and aneurysmal aortas, it reaches the accuracy of a deterministic PANN and assigns no aorta specimen to the wrong cohort with confidence, although it underestimates the uncertainty of untested curves.

cross Condensing Law of Security Information

Authors: Qi Tan, Tianyu Cui, Qi Li, Ke Xu

Abstract: Scaling laws indicate that the generalization error of a large language model (LLM) decreases monotonically with model size once data is sufficient, and the corollary that larger models outperform smaller ones is now widely accepted. We argue that this corollary fails in security: decisions there hinge on anomalous inputs, which are long-tailed (e.g., zero-day attacks) and therefore poorly served by likelihood fitting. Worse still, the capability brought about by scale (e.g., inferring the intended whole from a corrupted part) leads a LLM silently \emph{repair} the anomaly before any downstream security head ever sees it. We formalize this as a tradeoff between two quantities: the cognitive capability $K(W)=I(W;P)$ that a model holds about the world distribution, and the security-information retention $R(W)=\frac{I(Z;S)}{I(A;S)}$ that survives in its intermediate representation. We derive three information-theoretic bounds to connect them: an upper bound on restoration accuracy in terms of $K(W)$, an upper bound on $R(W)$ in terms of the restoration error, and a Fano lower bound on security-task error in terms of $R(W)$. These theorems approximately imply that usable security information is a product of how much security information survives and how well it can be extracted, indicating that optimal usable security information can be achieved at a certain model size. We state this as the \emph{condensing law of security information}: usable security information is unimodal in model size, so security generalization error is U-shaped and is minimized at a finite $N^{\star}$ rather than decreasing without bound. The experiments on various real-world LLMs validate the condensing law, indicating that there is an optimal model size to solve security issues.

cross Domain Adaptation Based Pipeline for Character Classification and Handwritten Text Recognition

Authors: Florent Imbert, Simon Corbill\'e, Hui Han, Elisa H. Barney Smith

Abstract: Character classification and handwritten text recognition, particularly in specialized tasks, often suffer from limited labeled data, while a large amount of unlabeled data remains unused. To address this challenge, we propose a novel unsupervised domain adaptation-based pipeline to reduce the distribution gap between source and target domains. Our pipeline follows three main steps. In the first step, we pretrained an expert model on source data, to learn a strong general representation. In the second step, we perform unsupervised domain adaptation to leverage unlabeled target data, adapting the model to the specific characteristics of the target domain. In the third step, we trained the expert model on source data in the target style. By aligning the feature distributions between the source and target, our method enables the model to generalize effectively across diverse datasets. The unsupervised training increases the computation time only during training not in inference. We evaluate the pipeline on both character-level and historical handwritten line recognition tasks, demonstrating its flexibility. We establish a new state-of-the-art on the Safran-MNIST-DLS dataset defined in Track 2 of the DAGECC competition with 76.17% Macro F1-score and achieve competitive results on each of the specific datasets of READ 2018 without using the labels of the target data. cross WebArena-Pro: A Heterogeneous, Multimodal, Reproducible Benchmark for Web Agents

cross Efficient and Practical Clustering of Variables in Causal Graphs

Authors: Otto Tabell, Jani Nyk\"anen, Santtu Tikka, Juha Karvanen

Abstract: Clustering variables in a graph may improve visual clarity and simplify causal inference. Harnessing the full potential of clustering requires an automated method to detect useful clusters in a graph. cross Algorithmic Opacity as a Principal-Agent Problem

Authors: Ignacio Cofone, Katherine J. Strandburg

Abstract: Concerns about gaming and trade secrecy, frequently invoked to justify algorithmic opacity, can also mask self-serving strategic behavior by decision-makers, who may prioritize private benefits over social welfare. We develop a principal-agent test for when algorithmic decision-making should trigger disclosure obligations even when concerns about gaming or trade secrecy exist. The test, which is anchored in decision quality, relies on two diagnostics that can be disclosed without enabling gaming or revealing trade secrets: the error profile (false positive and false negative rates, overall and for each group) and the distribution of decision outcomes across groups. These diagnostics make visible whether decision-makers align design and implementation with the public interest or instead use opacity to obscure bias, inefficiency, or strategic shirking. Strong disparities across social groups, and asymmetries in the distribution of decision errors across outcome types, should trigger rebuttable presumptions favoring further disclosure. Where ground-truth error rates are unavailable, outcome distributions across groups can support the same presumption, and a decision-maker's inability to produce an error profile is itself diagnostic. The test thus treats fairness metrics as triggers for further disclosure and extends to generative and agentic systems through analogous metrics. The framework informs differentiated disclosure regimes (staged, partial, mediated, or audience-segmented). Once such regimes are considered, the domain of justifiable algorithmic opacity is significantly narrower than commonly assumed: when gaming or trade secrecy risks are legitimate, actionable disclosures (such as training data characteristics and key system design parameters) ordinarily can be mandated without compromising innovation or enabling gaming, even for technically inscrutable algorithms.

cross LACCO: Local Accumulation with Computation--Communication Overlap

Authors: Daniele Kopyshevskiy (MLIA), Benjamin Th{\'e}rien (Mila, UdeM), Nicolas Pinto (MLIA), Edouard Oyallon (MLIA)

Abstract: Training language models across low-bandwidth networks requires reducing synchronization overhead. Local optimization methods such as DiLoCo communicate only after several local steps, but outer gradient averaging and the outer update remain on the critical path. Existing computation-communication overlap methods avoid this stall through delayed or replica-local updates, which can degrade convergence or cause replicas to drift. We propose LACCO (Local Accumulation with Computation-Communication Overlap), a two-stage algorithm that overlaps global communication with local computation while applying globally aggregated updates shared by every replica, without introducing staleness in the outer update. We establish convergence guarantees for smooth non-convex objectives. Experiments on language-model pre-training with FineWeb show that LACCO consistently outperforms communication-overlapped baselines such as Eager and Delayed DiLoCo. These gains remain robust as model size scales up to 0.5B parameters and across a range of communication intervals and numbers of replicas up to 16 workers, while incurring only negligible-or fully overlappable-computational or memory overhead, which demonstrates the strength of LACCO.

cross JaRS: Contrast-Free Localization of Memorized Regions in Diffusion Models via Jacobian Row Sums

Authors: Saisuchith Mahajan, Aditey Nandan, Rohit Singh, Nishanth Shetty, Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula

Abstract: Diffusion models sometimes memorize and reproduce regions of their training images, raising privacy and copyright concerns. We aim to localize these regions. Existing contrast-based methods use the diagonal of the denoiser Jacobian, which reflects the posterior variance of each pixel. However, a small diagonal entry is ambiguous: it signals either memorization or averaging over many neighboring pixels in smooth or predictable regions. To resolve this ambiguity, these methods subtract a reference map, which requires a second evaluation with a null prompt or an earlier checkpoint. Unconditional models have no null prompt, and earlier checkpoints are unavailable in general. We show that Jacobian row sums (JaRS), unlike the diagonal, separate memorization from local averaging without a reference map. Pairing JaRS with an image-level detection score that uses the same statistic at an early denoising step yields JaRS+. Across Stable Diffusion v1.4 and v2.1, Realistic Vision v5.1, and an unconditional diffusion Transformer (DiT), JaRS+ outperforms contrast-based localization methods.

Abstract: Large language and vision-language models have substantially advanced robotic behavior planning from natural-language instructions and visual observations. cross Beyond Bot Labels: Retrospective Consequence-Grounded Ranking of Attributed Cache Harm in E-Commerce Logs

Authors: Jisoo Kim, Youngwoo Seo, Seonghoon Jeong

Abstract: Web caches reduce origin load and latency, but automated traffic can either warm them or evict objects shoppers reuse, lowering the cache hit ratio (CHR). This comparison conditions on the victims that remain, so we also report victim bypass. cross Training-point consistency of kernel ridge regression with varying correlation scales

Authors: Weiyan Mu, Shifeng Xiong, Yuming Shen

Abstract: The scale parameter in kernel ridge regression plays a pivotal role in modeling the correlation structure of data. In modern applications, sampling density and the underlying correlation range often change simultaneously, making a fixed-scale assumption highly restrictive. However, establishing asymptotic theories under varying scale parameters is fundamentally challenging. To address this gap, this paper develops a unified theoretical framework for kernel ridge regression under varying scale parameters. We establish that, on uniform tensor grids with product exponential kernels, the consistency of the training-point risk holds if and only if the effective local sample size diverges to infinity. cross Muon Attains $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})$ Iteration Complexity under Shuffling

Authors: Kun Huang, Shi Pu, Karl Henrik Johansson

Abstract: Muon is a stochastic first-order optimizer for matrix-valued parameters. It maintains an exponential moving average of stochastic gradients in a momentum buffer with coefficient $\beta$, and orthogonalizes the buffer to obtain the update direction. Muon has been reported to accelerate and stabilize the training of large language models. In practice, it is often used with shuffling (sampling without replacement), where each epoch visits the components of a finite-sum objective in a permuted order. However, its iteration complexity in this setting remains open. We analyze Muon under shuffling, with the momentum buffer orthogonalized by the polar map. For smooth nonconvex finite-sum problems, we establish an $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})$ iteration complexity for finding an $\varepsilon$-stationary point. This guarantee holds for every sequence of permutations, covering random reshuffling, shuffle once, and the incremental gradient method. This complexity matches that of stochastic gradient descent under shuffling in its dependence on $\varepsilon$, and improves on Muon's $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-4})$ complexity under sampling with replacement. Our guarantee requires a problem-dependent $\beta$ that approaches one as the number of components grows. In contrast, no fixed $\beta$ guarantees convergence to stationarity for every number of components. We show this by constructing a family of smooth convex problems on which, for any fixed $\beta\in[0,1)$, Muon remains bounded away from stationarity once the number of components is sufficiently large. This failure occurs for every sequence of permutations and persists even with small stepsizes. These results suggest two practical tuning rules: if the training loss stops decreasing even after reducing the stepsize, increase $\beta$; when increasing $\beta$, reduce the stepsize accordingly.

Abstract: A model may produce a prediction with high confidence and still get the outcome wrong. Authors: Diane Bouchacourt, Ga\"etan Brison

Abstract: Medical AI systems that answer users' queries rely on retrieving medical knowledge from large document collections. Authors: Ga\"el Poux-M\'edard, Kaniav Kamary, Yohann De Castro, Vivian Viallon

Abstract: Mediation analysis seeks to understand how an exposure influences an outcome through intermediate variables. cross Online Switching Control as Learning with Experts

Authors: Masataka Nakamura, Kaito Ito, Hideaki Ishii

Abstract: This paper addresses the problem of online switching control for discrete-time nonlinear systems where a controller is sequentially selected from a finite set of candidates. The goal is to generate an adaptive sequence of controllers to minimize the cumulative cost under unknown disturbances and cost functions. Specifically, we consider a full-information feedback setting where realized disturbance effects and cost functions are available after each selection. Authors: Mehrdad Saberi, Vishal Asnani, Shruti Agarwal, Soheil Feizi, John Collomosse

Abstract: We explore the adaptability of neural image watermarking models; the extent to which one watermarking method can be transformed into another through lightweight adaptation. cross Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment

Authors: Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI), Soran Parsa (University of Huddersfield)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($\pi_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.

cross Structure makes quantum circuits trainable

Authors: Wenbo Xie, Wojciech Szpankowski, Ananth Grama

Abstract: The scalability of variational quantum algorithms is limited by barren plateaus, where gradients vanish exponentially in system size. This worst-case result raises fundamental questions about the trainability of quantum AI models. We present a major advance towards resolving this trainability question by answering whether a given quantum circuit has a barren plateau. We do this using an analytical framework that bounds the gradient variance for arbitrary input states $\rho$ and observables $\mathcal{M}$ by decomposing the unitary representation of the associated compact Lie group. When the circuit segments on both sides of a gate form $2$-designs, we obtain the variance exactly and prove a sufficient condition for trainability: if $\rho^{\otimes 2}$ and $\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}$ are sufficiently concentrated on the same irreducible invariant subspace of polynomial dimension, and the differentiated gate moves this subspace by an inverse-polynomial amount, then the gradient variance is at least inverse-polynomial. We further present a sampling algorithm that estimates the gradient variance from runs of the ansatz, without constructing the representation-theoretic projectors. Our algorithm decides whether the variance exceeds a tolerance $\tau$ with the number of runs polynomial in $1/\tau$, including cases in which the bounds are inconclusive. This resolves the question of the trainability of the circuit before expensive training procedures.

cross A systematic comparison of physics-informed neural networks and mechanistic models for epidemic modeling and forecasting

Authors: Martina Rama, Yuhan Li, Bruno Lepri, Nicola Perra, Michele Tizzoni

Abstract: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) have recently been applied to epidemic modeling and forecasting with promising results. These methods integrate mechanistic knowledge with data-driven learning. Yet, their comparative performance against well-established, and simpler epidemic models is underexplored. Here, we conduct a systematic comparison of a PINN-based framework and a stochastic mechanistic model. Both approaches share the same age-structured SEIRD core and rolling-window forecasting pipeline, in a deliberately minimal setting. We evaluate their retrospective fits and probabilistic forecasts in two synthetic scenarios and the first COVID-19 wave in London. Interestingly, while PINNs underperform in synthetic scenarios, they achieve better performance on real data. Furthermore, PINNs provide narrower uncertainty intervals and sharper estimation of parameters in synthetic scenarios. Our findings confirm PINNs as a promising tool in epidemiology. At the same time, the results reaffirm the value of simpler approaches such as traditional compartmental models, and promote additional benchmarking studies.

cross Lipschitz-Constrained Discriminators in Generative Modeling: Asymptotic Behavior and Support Mismatch

Authors: Eduardo Pinto, Panagiota Birmpa

Abstract: We study the asymptotic behavior of optimal discriminators associated to $L-$Lipschitz regularized KL-divergences of two probability distributions $Q$ and $P$ that interpolate between the rescaled Wasserstein$-1$ metric by some parameter $L>0$ and the KL divergence. The parameter $L$ plays the role of a Lipschitz constraint and is viewed as a regularization parameter: small values of $L$ enforce a more geometry-aware discriminator, while as $L$ increases the geometric regularization is progressively relaxed. It is a known result [3] that such divergences converge to the KL divergence as $L\to\infty$. In this paper, we characterize the asymptotic behavior of the optimal discriminators as $L\to\infty$ in different regimes: Firstly, under $Q\ll P$ and the Radon-Nikodym derivative $\frac{dQ}{dP}$ is bounded from above and bounded away from zero. Secondly, when $Q$ and $P$ are discrete distributions satisfying $Q\ll P$, and finally when $Q$ and $P$ are non absolutely continuous discrete distributions. This result motivates training strategies which involve discriminators, such as adversarial generative models. In practice, the data distribution is often represented by a discrete empirical measure, while, especially at the early stages of the training, the model distribution may have little or no overlap with the empirical data distribution.

cross CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning

Authors: Tao Hu, Ling-Xiang Zhang, Da-Wei Zhou

Abstract: Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.

cross Graph diffusion transformer for interpretable generation and inverse design of disordered atomic structures

Authors: Jiani Hu, Chenyan Wang, Yongkun Yang, Guanyu Hou, Jihaochen Zhang, Lei Liu, Ji Chen

Abstract: Atomic disorder offers a potent degree of freedom to tailor material properties, yet the resulting vast configuration space obscures structure-property relations and hinders rational inverse design. Here we introduce Graph Amorphous Diffusion Transformer (GaDiT), a generative model that combines dynamic periodic graphs, masked conditioning and an interval-weighted training-noise distribution. GaDiT generates high-quality disordered structures while accelerating sampling by two orders of magnitude compared with existing corrector-based approaches, as demonstrated across two-dimensional amorphous carbon and three-dimensional amorphous silicon and silica. Moreover, GaDiT supports conditional generation under single or combined structural features, physical properties and preparation conditions in a unified model, and enables inverse design that expands accessible structure-property ranges beyond the training distribution. To interpret these structures and the trained network, we propose a conditioned atomic response mapping analysis, revealing both the global structural descriptors and the local atomic environments that govern target properties. Overall, GaDiT establishes a general framework for interpretable generation, inverse design and structure-property discovery for disordered atomic structures.

cross The State Is Not the Bottleneck: A Paged-State Gated-DeltaNet Tile on a Tri-Mode Multicast Mesh, Bit-Exact from RTL to Silicon

Authors: Cong Xu, Ravi Sankar

Abstract: Linear-attention language models replace a growing key-value cache with a recurrent state of fixed size. On an edge FPGA, this state can exceed the on-chip memory and must be paged to external memory. This paper asks whether such paging dominates the cost of decoding (generating tokens). We built a hardware tile that computes one complete layer of a custom 369-million-parameter hybrid Gated DeltaNet model on an Agilex 5 FPGA and pages its recurrent state. The model state needs 6.2 MiB, whereas the device has 0.87 MiB of block memory. Paging adds 26.0 MB of memory traffic per decoded token, at most 11.2 percent of the calculated total. On the board at 75 MHz, with synthetic weights, one token takes 11.15 ms in this layer. The matrix-vector products with the weights take about 62 percent of this time and the state phase, including the paging, 13 percent. The tile is one node of a multicast mesh of tri-mode processing elements. We measure the error of the fixed-point arithmetic against floating point and check that the hardware agrees bit for bit with a fixed-point reference. In simulation, the state logic agrees with the reference on all 18 recurrent layers. On the board, six builds pass all 30 runs of 64 tokens and two runs of 1,024 tokens. For this model and memory interface, the delivery of the weights limits the decoding performance, and the recurrent state does not. State paging is therefore practical for such a model on an edge FPGA.

cross Callo: Serving Cache-Miss Requests with Speculative Decoding on Invalidated KV under Prefill-Decode Disaggregation

Authors: Jianjun Ou, Matthew Lentz

Abstract: Agent sessions may involve frequent context edits, which effectively invalidate cached KV state and force requests to wait for re-prefill before decoding. We present Callo, a disaggregated serving system that leverages invalidated KV for speculative drafts to reduce this latency, while verifying drafts against the exact KV after prefill before committing tokens. Callo makes effective use of decode resources by scheduling speculations during periods of underutilization and preempting speculations when drafts are likely to not be accepted. We evaluated Callo on a two-GPU testbed using SWE-agent sessions across three model families, observing all-request speedups of 1.05-1.19x over vLLM at moderate load. We demonstrate that these gains are also realized at scale through cluster-level simulations driven by real-world traces.

cross Score-based neural ordinary differential equations for reaction-diffusions

Authors: Jianguo Hou, Wuchen Li

Abstract: A diffusion equation can be rewritten as a transport equation driven by the score function of the evolving density. Conventional probability-flow formulations, which move particles deterministically with this score-driven velocity, typically conserve total mass. In this work, we develop a Lagrangian method for reaction-diffusion equations in which each particle carries a position and a physical weight. The characteristic ODEs incorporate source terms into the evolution of the log-density and its gradient, while particle weights track changes in mass. We combine this representation with a learned velocity field and weighted particle training to approximate these dynamics. For non-increasing reaction rates, a relative-entropy inequality supports the mass-normalized self-consistency residual as the training objective. This objective can control the error of the induced density when the growth rate is bounded. The algorithm divides the time interval into short windows. In each window, a frozen velocity generates the particle data for a regression of the network velocity. We draw the particles from period-adapted initial sampling distributions and weight them in the loss partly by their predicted future mass, because the reaction can make regions with little initial mass important later. We numerically investigate the method on the Ornstein--Uhlenbeck (OU) equation with a linear source term, Fisher--KPP equations, and Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao gradient flows.

cross Contrastive latent representation learning with denoising score matching

Authors: Christopher Marouani, Grigory Bartosh, Christian A. Naesseth

Abstract: Contrastive representation learning methods operate by pulling different views of the same data sample together while pushing them away from views of different samples. A fundamental challenge is the estimation of the repulsive force, which typically requires large batch sizes, can exhibit high variance, and is susceptible to the curse of dimensionality. We introduce ScoreCLR, a contrastive representation learning method that offers a novel way to estimate this repulsion term. We consider the framework of representation learning by maximizing the mutual information between two latent views of a sample. By deriving the gradient of the Shannon entropy with respect to the encoder parameters, we obtain an equivalent objective that involves the score of the latent representation. We then use denoising score matching techniques to estimate this score with a lightweight network that is trained jointly with the encoder. We further propose regularization techniques using different noise levels and a Gaussian prior on the latent representation. ScoreCLR replaces the batch of explicit negative samples in contrastive methods with a learned repulsion whose cost is linear in the batch size rather than quadratic. On CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-100 it does so with minimal loss of linear-probe accuracy compared to SimCLR under a shared recipe, while producing projector outputs of higher effective rank, closer to the Gaussian prior.

cross Evaluating Feedback-Driven Black-Box Processes via Stochastic Modeling and Simulation

Authors: Mengfan Xu, Zirui Liu, Juan Zhai, Shiqing Ma, Barry Nelson

Abstract: We study feedback-driven black-box processes across software engineering and machine learning. These processes are inherently sequential: policies generate inputs, observe outputs/feedback from systems, and use the feedback to guide the design of subsequent inputs. While many policies are developed under structural assumptions on the underlying systems, many real-world systems operate without such knowledge, necessitating black-box policies that lack a unified evaluation framework. We introduce a stochastic framework that models and evaluates such policies using Markov chains and simulation, thereby connecting empirical practice with theory. We instantiate the framework in mutation-based fuzzing (widely used for discovering software bugs via sequential input mutation), by a novel Markov chain model of fuzzing whose transitions are governed by the mutation strategy and whose feedback is given by program execution. We develop a rigorous evaluation paradigm with new performance measures, sound estimators, and evaluation procedures with provable guarantees. Guided by the theory, we evaluate 9 fuzzers on 18 widely used fuzzing benchmarks and conduct case studies in 4 non-fuzzing domains. These results show that the framework provides a principled basis for evaluation while connecting to practice, with implications beyond fuzzing.

cross Bregman geometry in regular exponential families: dual representations and relative smoothness

Authors: Mame Diarra Fall, \'Eric Barat, Nicolas Dobigeon

Abstract: Regular exponential-family distributions are ubiquitous in statistics due to their appealing and convenient properties. Notably, they can be expressed in terms of Bregman divergences, revealing fundamental connections between statistics, convex analysis, and information geometry. However, this geometric structure has not been systematically exploited to characterize these distributions with respect to their expectation parametrization. Building on a projective characterization, we establish a general construction of equivalent dual Bregman representations, together with normalization and uniqueness results. These representations express log-densities as ordinary Bregman divergences in dual coordinate systems, providing geometric characterizations of the parameter space. So-called $\beta$- and $\alpha$-scaled dual representations emerge as particular instances of this framework. We further derive dual Bregman representations of conjugate priors and establish that they preserve conjugacy. Applications to classical distributions illustrate the geometric structures underlying seemingly different statistical models. Moreover, we show that these representations provide natural reference geometries for establishing relative smoothness of the primal Bregman representation of the distribution, with explicit bounds that are sharp in the scalar and separable settings. An illustrative application to the Poisson distribution sheds new light on several works in the literature, providing complementary geometric justifications for reference geometries while deriving existing algorithms.

cross Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors

Authors: Robin Shing Moon Chan, Ryan Cotterell

Abstract: Program induction refers to the process in which a learner aims to recover a program from a few given input-output examples. This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. We view this problem from a Bayesian perspective, where, given a prior $p^0$ over programs, identifying the target program means concentrating posterior probability mass on it by observing additional input-output examples. In the interactive program induction setting, the learner chooses maximally informative inputs and queries an oracle, such as the user, for their outputs. We derive a greedy query-selection algorithm and show that it terminates after $\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\gamma}\log 1/p^0(f^\star))$ informative oracle queries, and returns the target program $f^\star$ up to approximate equivalence when no other class outweighs it under the prior. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. On the CodeARC benchmark, the algorithm improves accuracy over the LM only agent by up to 22 percentage points while observing up to 10.5 fewer input-output examples.

cross StreamEmbed: Real-Time Indexing from Pixels for Screenshot Retrieval

Authors: Dayoon Ko, Yichuan Wang, Soyeong Jeong, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Gunhee Kim, Sewon Min

Abstract: Screen history can serve as searchable visual memory for people and agents, but unlike an offline corpus, it must be encoded and indexed as each screenshot arrives. OCR-based indexing is fast but loses visual information; visual encoding takes over two seconds. Yet screens change incrementally: most pixels repeat within and across screenshots. This raises a question: do visual encoders need to recompute their representations from scratch for every new screenshot? We present StreamEmbed, a system for incremental visual embedding that exploits this redundancy. Guided solely by pixel differences, it combines spatial token pruning, which skips blocks that repeat a neighbor, with temporal KV reuse, which reuses cached keys and values for blocks unchanged across screenshots. A small learned adapter compensates for pruning and reuse to preserve fidelity to full-screenshot embeddings. On ScreenMemEval, our benchmark of 10,426 questions over 184 hours of screen histories from two humans and a computer-use agent, StreamEmbed sets a new accuracy-latency Pareto frontier on an M2 Pro GPU: it encodes up to 3.7x faster than full-screenshot encoding without sacrificing accuracy and improves accuracy over OCR-based indexing by up to 6.3 Recall@10 points at matched latency. A memory-efficient vector index stores a simulated year of screenshots in 0.13 GB of RAM. Together, these results make real-time indexing of screen history in its native visual form practical, laying a foundation for fully local visual assistants.

cross Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction

Authors: Abhilekha Dalal, Avishek Das, Samatha Ereshi Akkamahadevi

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) can classify images using background-dominated patches that contain little or none of the target object, showing that they exploit contextual information. Previous studies detected this behavior through classification accuracy but say little about what CNN hidden layers represent. We investigate whether Concept Induction can reveal interpretable visual properties associated with hidden-neuron activations on contextual image patches. Using five agricultural datasets with high, moderate, and low contextual bias, we analyze four CNN architectures at three network depths and five patch locations. Quantitative image descriptors of each patch are encoded in a knowledge graph and used as background knowledge for Concept Induction, which derives logical expressions characterizing neuron activation. These expressions are tested on held-out images using the Mann-Whitney U test and Cliff's delta. Of 48,013 tested neuron-feature pairs, 13,164 (27.4%) were validated. Validation was most frequent at intermediate depths (mean 40.2% at mid layers versus 12.0% at deep layers), and no patch location dominated across datasets. Among the tested descriptors, edge, Gabor, and Tamura texture features had the highest validation rates. Stronger contextual bias did not correspond to higher neuron-feature validation rates: moderately biased Sugarcane had the highest rate (40.5%) and highly biased Cassava the lowest (19.2%). These results suggest that behavioral context dependence and the semantic interpretability of hidden neurons capture different aspects of CNN behavior, and that Concept Induction can complement patch-based bias detection.

cross ModHarness-RL: Recasting Harnesses as Learnable and Evolving Programs for Agentic RL

Authors: Zelin He, Siyu Zhu, Jacob Helwig, Yujian Liu, Li An, Rohit Jain, Shiyu Chang, Alborz Geramifard

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly relies on a harness, the scaffold around the model that decides what it sees of its own interaction and what guidance it receives. Most existing approaches train under a hand-written harness that stays fixed throughout. However, an effective harness depends on the domain, the data and the model, and keeps changing as the model trains. We introduce ModHarness-RL, which recasts the harness as programs that are learned from data and evolve as the model trains with RL. ModHarness-RL records the interaction as structured State, a set of typed blocks, and two core components of the harness are programs over these blocks, a Compression program that assembles the accumulated history into the model's context and a Steering program that supplies guidance when behavior deviates. During RL training, candidate programs are proposed from recent trajectories, verified by execution, and replayed over past trajectories, so the harness tracks how the model's behavior shifts. We study CodeSearch in depth and further analyze ALFWorld and BIRD SQL, with models from 1.5B to 8B. The proposed Compression and Steering mechanisms improve held-out performance over the fixed-harness baseline in most settings. These results suggest that a harness for agentic RL can be learned from the task's data and evolved with the model.

cross Why Finite Intelligence Works in an Infinite World: A Mathematical Theory

Authors: Mojtaba Soltanalian

Abstract: An information system often has to learn before it knows what it will be asked. A finite representation can succeed without recording an infinite environment in full when those asks share collective structure, rather than demand unrelated facts scattered arbitrarily across it. We formalize this phenomenon through future-task information, the minimum retained information needed to preserve decision value relative to the available evidence. A joint decision-regret state records posterior comparisons among actions for every permitted task. Under compactness assumptions, every retained representation admits a calibrated replacement based on this state, preserving all task regrets without increasing information; the resulting information-regret curve equals an operational coding rate. Two exact boundaries govern finite preservation: weak-star closure for a fixed action count, and the singular mass of the source-evidence law when all finite action menus are admitted. Collective structure determines information requirements beyond individual-question difficulty. Families with identical source laws, pairwise task metrics, and individual-task rates can have different high-resolution exponents. Covariance-identical coordinate and parity families have different exact rate-distortion curves, with optimal parity retention characterized by a sharp Fourier-entropy inequality. For balanced nested task families, refinement sizes and weighted reach determine the rate within an additive constant, attained by prefix coding under explicit decay conditions. Nonlinear learning and computational separations distinguish preservation from efficient discovery and use. The proposed mathematical theory identifies which shared decision requirements allow finite information to remain useful for infinitely many possible purposes.

cross Elliptic Sampling: Optimal Interpolant Schedules for Gaussians and Beyond

Authors: Cole Becker, Taylan Kargin, Kimia Hassibi, Asuman Ozdaglar, Pablo A. Parrilo

Abstract: Generative modeling based on stochastic interpolants requires specifying two key choices: an interpolant schedule bridging the initial and target distributions, and an efficient discretization of the resulting probability-flow ODE. In this work, we introduce a new framework for joint schedule/discretization design based on the analyticity properties of the (complexified) ODE trajectories, which in turn ensure good numerical behavior. Our formulation can be solved exactly via conformal mapping, yielding a natural family of variance-preserving elliptic schedules, parametrized by Jacobi elliptic functions. Pairing an elliptic schedule with a tailored discretization, we obtain Zoloflow, a new sampling algorithm closely connected to Zolotarev's optimal rational function approximants. Crucially, both the elliptic schedule and Zoloflow explicitly incorporate the condition number $\kappa$ of the target distribution and reduce to the classical trig schedule in the limit $\kappa \rightarrow 1$. For Gaussian targets, Zoloflow achieves minimax optimality among all oblivious score-span algorithms, attaining total-variation error $\varepsilon$ using $\Theta(\log\kappa\,\log(\sqrt d/\varepsilon))$ (exact) score queries. For the log-concave case, the elliptic schedule allows sampling with at most $\varepsilon$ 2-Wasserstein-error by requiring merely $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d} \, \mathrm{polylog}(\kappa, 1/\varepsilon))$ score evaluations. Complementing these theoretical results, we empirically evaluate the elliptic schedule on general (non-log-concave) target distributions, including detailed comparisons with alternative schedule proposals on both synthetic benchmarks and existing image generation datasets.

cross BAL: Bidirectional Autoregression in Latent Space for Learning Human Movement Representations

Authors: Genki Kinoshita, Ko Nishino

Abstract: Learning semantically meaningful human movement representations from 3D pose sequences is essential for human behavior modeling. Existing self-supervised methods derive supervision from artificial assumptions, e.g., invariance to augmentation within a certain range or recoverability of masked regions at a certain temporal scale, introducing heuristic hyperparameters that make the learned semantics sensitive to their settings. We propose BAL, a self-supervised framework that instead exploits supervision intrinsic to human movement: the natural ordering structure of everyday behavior. BAL autoregressively predicts in a semantically abstract latent space, analogous to how humans anticipate others' actions rather than their precise joint configurations. Bidirectional autoregressive prediction, both forward and backward in time, fully leverages these ordering constraints while enabling representation extraction using both past and future context at inference. To suppress representation collapse, BAL decomposes each prediction target into a global component capturing sequence-level semantics and a local component capturing context-independent movement. We prove that the local targets retain nontrivial angular diversity unless the representations are completely collapsed, preserving a meaningful training signal. Experiments on large-scale datasets demonstrate that BAL's representations are highly effective for motion captioning, forecasting, and interpolation, outperforming baselines on most evaluation metrics.

cross FETTUCCINE: Fast and efficient brain-to-text decoding on mobile devices

Authors: Jonathan D. McCart, Pranav I. Deevi, Mehdi Azabou, Nanda H. Krishna, Caleb A. McKinney, Nicholas S. Card, Sergey D. Stavisky, Chethan Pandarinath

Abstract: Recent advances in speech neuroprostheses have enabled new communication avenues for people who have lost the ability to speak due to neurological illness or injury. These systems can decode neural activity during attempted speech into text by relying on language priors to achieve high decoding accuracy. This performance currently comes at the cost of using a compute-intensive language modeling stack, presenting a major bottleneck for real-world use. Further, current language models incur highly variable and disruptive inference latencies, limiting communication throughput and preventing users from reliably participating in natural conversation. We introduce FETTUCCINE, an end-to-end brain-to-text framework that leverages the speed and portability of automatic speech recognition (ASR) models to address the aforementioned practical challenges, while achieving usable (<10%) word error rates (WER), efficient adaptation to neural non-stationarities, and reliable performance on mobile devices. When evaluated using publicly available data from an intracortical speech neuroprosthesis user, FETTUCCINE outperforms previous end-to-end methods, achieving as low as 4.39% WER. Most notably, our models successfully run on a commercially-available mobile device with throughput reaching up to 370x real-time, providing the first demonstration of brain-to-text decoding on a mobile device. We also show that our models can be successfully finetuned to extend usable performance to future days. Beyond performance, our approach enables localization of the neural features most relevant for decoding via gradient-based saliency maps, which we show align with well-established physiological priors across different ASR model families. Taken together, our results show that FETTUCCINE overcomes core infrastructural and computational barriers, yielding a new class of portable brain-to-text communication.

cross Training Reasoning Models in Simulation to Natively Use Real Robots

Authors: Jiaming Tang, Zhuoyang Zhang, Junxian Guo, Fangzhou Zhao, Yilong Zhao, Zekai Li, Xingyang Li, Yuyang Zhao, Song Bian, Zhijian Liu, Song Han

Abstract: Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. However, strong performance has so far been achieved only by frontier proprietary models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2, whereas open-weight models remain substantially less capable. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. Like a computer-use agent (CUA), the model observes the environment, reasons in a chain of thought, and generates the next end-effector pose, without any harness. We build a Post-training Engine for Agentic Robotics (PEAR) and use it to train the model on simulated robot tasks at scale with robot-use reinforcement learning (RURL), mirroring how frontier models acquire computer use. We find that the skills acquired in simulation generalize well beyond the training tasks: they transfer to an unseen robot, to the real world, and to novel instructions. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $\pi_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $\pi_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.

replace Robust Long-Tailed Learning under Label Noise

Authors: Tong Wei, Jiang-Xin Shi, Min-Ling Zhang, Yu-Feng Li

Abstract: Long-tailed learning aims to enhance the generalization performance of underrepresented tail classes. However, previous methods have largely overlooked the prevalence of noisy labels in training data. In this paper, we address the challenge of noisy labels in long-tailed learning. We identify a critical issue: the commonly used small-loss noisy label detection criterion fails to perform effectively in long-tailed class distributions. This failure arises from the inherent bias of deep neural networks, which tend to misclassify tail class examples as head classes, leading to unreliable loss calculations. To mitigate this, we propose a novel small-distance criterion that leverages the robustness of learned representations, enabling more accurate identification of correctly-labeled examples across both head and tail classes. Additionally, to improve training for tail classes, we replace discrete pseudo-labels with label distributions for examples flagged as noisy, resulting in significant performance gains. Based on these contributions, we introduce the Robust Long-Tail learning framework, designed to train models that are resilient to both class imbalance and noisy labels. Extensive experiments on benchmark and real-world datasets demonstrate that our approach outperforms previous methods, offering substantial performance improvements.

replace Benign Overfitting for Regression with Trained Two-Layer ReLU Networks

Authors: Junhyung Park, Patrick Bloebaum, Shiva Prasad Kasiviswanathan

Abstract: We study the least-square regression problem with a two-layer fully-connected neural network, with ReLU activation function, trained by gradient flow. Our first result is a generalization result, that requires no assumptions on the underlying regression function or the noise other than that they are bounded. We operate in the neural tangent kernel regime, and our generalization result is developed via a decomposition of the excess risk into estimation and approximation errors, viewing gradient flow as an implicit regularizer. This decomposition in the context of neural networks is a novel perspective of gradient descent, and helps us avoid uniform convergence traps. In this work, we also establish that under the same setting, the trained network overfits to the data. Together, these results, establishes the first result on benign overfitting for finite-width ReLU networks for arbitrary regression functions.

replace Mapping Seven Decades of Philosophy in Colombia: Dynamic Topic Modelling of Ideas y Valores

Authors: Juan R. Loaiza, Miguel Gonz\'alez-Duque

Abstract: Data-driven approaches to philosophy have emerged as a valuable tool for studying the history of the discipline. However, most studies in this area have focused on a limited number of journals from specific regions and subfields. We expand the scope of this research by applying dynamic topic modeling techniques to explore the history of philosophy in Colombia and Latin America. Our study examines the Colombian philosophy journal Ideas y Valores, founded in 1951 and currently one of the most influential academic philosophy journals in the region. By analyzing the evolution of topics across the journal's history, we identify various trends and specific dynamics in philosophical discourse within the Colombian and Latin American context. Our findings reveal that the most prominent topics are value theory (including ethics, political philosophy, and aesthetics), epistemology, and the philosophy of science. We also trace the evolution of articles focused on interpreting a specific philosopher's work rather than proposing new positions, and we note a salient emphasis on German philosophers such as Kant, Husserl, and Hegel across various topics throughout the journal's lifetime. Given the journal's founding aspiration toward more original, non-exegetical philosophy, we investigate whether exegetical topics became comparatively less prominent over time. Our analysis suggests no significant decline in such topics. Finally, we propose ideas for extending this research to other Latin American journals and suggest improvements for natural language processing workflows in non-English languages.

replace Bidirectional Information Flow (BIF) - A Sample Efficient Hierarchical Gaussian Process for Bayesian Optimization

Authors: Juan D. Guerra, Thomas Garbay, Numa Dancause, Guillaume Lajoie, Marco Bonizzato

Abstract: Hierarchical Gaussian Process (H-GP) models divide problems into different subtasks, allowing for different components to address each part, making them well-suited for problems with inherent compositional structure. However, existing H-GP frameworks typically employ one-way information sharing - either top-down or bottom-up - which limits sample efficiency and slows convergence. We propose Bidirectional Information Flow (BIF), which establishes continuous two-way communication. BIF retains the modular structure of hierarchical models - the parent conditions its own posterior on child summaries, treating them as structured priors - while introducing top-down feedback to softly decompose environment observations from the parent into sub-responses. This mutual exchange improves sample efficiency, enables robust training, and allows modular reuse of learned subtask models. We prove analytically the regret of a GP with a learned kernel scales linearly with the mismatch to the true kernel, tightening in the hierarchical case to the sum of child-level errors. Ablation shows removing the downward pathway collapses child $R^2$ by up to 58%. Across synthetic, neurostimulation, and HPO benchmarks, BIF achieves up to $4\times$ higher parent $R^2$ and roughly 100% AUC improvement over vanilla GPBO, and outperforms or is the runner-up in 38/40 tests compared to the state-of-the-art baselines while supporting modular child transfer to novel composite tasks.

replace AbFlowNet: Optimizing Antibody-Antigen Binding Energy via Diffusion-GFlowNet Fusion

Authors: Abrar Rahman Abir, Haz Sameen Shahgir, Md Rownok Zahan Ratul, Md Toki Tahmid, Greg Ver Steeg, Yue Dong

Abstract: Complementarity Determining Regions (CDRs) are critical segments of an antibody that facilitate binding to specific antigens. Current computational methods for CDR design utilize reconstruction losses and do not jointly optimize binding energy, a crucial metric for antibody efficacy. Rather, binding energy optimization is done through computationally expensive Online Reinforcement Learning (RL) pipelines rely heavily on unreliable binding energy estimators. In this paper, we propose AbFlowNet, a novel generative framework that integrates GFlowNet with Diffusion models. By framing each diffusion step as a state in the GFlowNet framework, AbFlowNet jointly optimizes standard diffusion losses and binding energy by directly incorporating energy signals into the training process, thereby unifying diffusion and reward optimization in a single procedure. Experimental results show that AbFlowNet outperforms the base diffusion model by 3.06% in amino acid recovery, 20.40% in geometric reconstruction (RMSD), and 3.60% in binding energy improvement ratio. ABFlowNet also decreases Top-1 total energy and binding energy errors by 24.8% and 38.1% without pseudo-labeling the test dataset or using computationally expensive online RL regimes.

replace Chi-Square Wavelet Graph Neural Networks for Heterogeneous Graph Anomaly Detection

Authors: Xiping Li, Xiangyu Dong, Xingyi Zhang, Kun Xie, Yuanhao Feng, Bo Wang, Guilin Li, Wuxiong Zeng, Xiujun Shu, Sibo Wang

Abstract: Graph Anomaly Detection (GAD) in heterogeneous networks presents unique challenges due to node and edge heterogeneity. Existing Graph Neural Network (GNN) methods primarily focus on homogeneous GAD and thus fail to address three key issues: (C1) Capturing abnormal signal and rich semantics across diverse meta-paths; (C2) Retaining high-frequency content in HIN dimension alignment; and (C3) Learning effectively from difficult anomaly samples with class imbalance. To overcome these, we propose ChiGAD, a spectral GNN framework based on a novel Chi-Square filter, inspired by the wavelet effectiveness in diverse domains. Specifically, ChiGAD consists of: (1) Multi-Graph Chi-Square Filter, which captures anomalous information via applying dedicated Chi-Square filters to each meta-path graph; (2) Interactive Meta-Graph Convolution, which aligns features while preserving high-frequency information and incorporates heterogeneous messages by a unified Chi-Square Filter; and (3) Contribution-Informed Cross-Entropy Loss, which prioritizes difficult anomalies to address class imbalance. Extensive experiments on public and industrial datasets show that ChiGAD outperforms state-of-the-art models on multiple metrics. Additionally, its homogeneous variant, ChiGNN, excels on seven GAD datasets, validating the effectiveness of Chi-Square filters. Our code is available at https://github.com/HsipingLi/ChiGAD.
