replace Attributing Cohen's d: Training Data Attribution for Disease-Related Effects in Normative Age Biomarkers

Authors: Jakob Snel, Marc-Andre Schulz

Abstract: Normative age models are trained to predict chronological age in a nominally healthy cohort. In patients, the gap between predicted and chronological age is read as disease risk. We attribute the disease-related effect size of the age gap directly to individual training samples, rather than using a prediction-level loss as the attribution target. For the Cohen's d effect size, the resulting closed-form influence functional, validated against leave-one-out retraining, ranks training samples by their effect on held-out case-control separation. Across four diseases and two biomarker modalities in UK Biobank, removing the 10% most influential training samples raises held-out disease-related effect size in every seed. It more than doubles the metabolomic-age effect for type 2 diabetes and raises the brain-age effect for multiple sclerosis by roughly a third. Random removal leaves effect size flat even at 50% removal, confirming the gain comes from which samples are removed, not how many. Flagged subjects, missed by diagnosis-based exclusion, show elevated cardiometabolic markers the model never sees, consistent with subclinical contamination of the reference cohort. For type 2 diabetes, where the method gains most, the marker recovered is HbA1c, the standard measure of blood sugar control. We release our influence-function package pyinfluence.

replace Beyond Embedding Transfer: Architectural Interfaces, Iso-Parameter Scaling, and Preconditioner-Governed Consolidation in Grokking

Authors: Zeyu Jia

Abstract: Grokking, or delayed generalization after memorization, incurs substantial training steps across algebraic tasks. Cross-operator transfer from a grokked donor accelerates target acquisition, yet post-grokking AdamW continuation triggers test-accuracy relapse as unannealed updates drift across residual interfaces and decaying second moments amplify late gradients. Across depths one to three on modular arithmetic ($\mathbb{Z}_{113}$), non-Abelian $S_5$ conjugation, and real-sensory MNIST modular arithmetic, we separate representational transfer from preconditioner-governed consolidation across thirteen experimental batteries. First, co-transferring internal blocks accelerates one-layer and two-layer modular takeoff beyond boundary interfaces, while graded prefix pinning bounds depth-three drift under unannealed continuation. Second, an iso-parameter $S_5$ scaling grid separates attention head dimension from MLP width: widening two-head attention to dimension $64$ reaches $98.54\%$ cold accuracy at $427\mathrm{k}$ parameters and $85.95\%$ boundary-only transfer, while final-slot representations refine conjugation directly across blocks without an intermediate binary-product bottleneck. Third, Plateau-Gated Leaky Consolidation pairs validation-envelope step damping with leaky second-moment decay, reaching $84.85\%$ zero-leakage validation-gated risk-constrained accuracy across five pre-saturation two-layer $S_5$ suites while cutting step budget relative to hard anchoring. Fourth, transferring a grokked visual trunk and blocks to image-disjoint MNIST targets reaches $92.27\%$ and $90.76\%$ accuracy, prunes odd centroid harmonics, and reaches $90.42\%$ risk-constrained accuracy across $60/60$ branches without hyperparameter retuning.

replace Hidden not Deleted: How Networks Suppress Entangled Features

Authors: Akash Samanta, Manish Pratap Singh, Debasis Chaudhuri

Abstract: When a neural network stops reporting a feature, has it forgotten the feature or merely hidden it? Shared representations complicate the answer: removing one feature's direction can also damage another feature. We study this problem in a small autoencoder that compresses and reconstructs inputs, allowing individual feature contributions to be traced directly. With the representation fixed, restoring a single bias partially recovers target detection by exposing a signal hidden behind the output's activation threshold. Tests on target-absent inputs distinguish this recovery from interventions that simply increase output. The same bias intervention becomes less effective when the representation is allowed to change. We find that training only the other outputs can weaken the target's contribution, even without a loss on its own output. A conditional theoretical analysis explains how interference with retained predictions creates this pressure. Yet more flexible predictors still detect residual target information. These findings show how recovery depends on feature geometry and on which parameters are trained, and why a silent output is insufficient evidence of forgetting.

replace ReCo: When to Relocate Sensor Kits under Deployment Constraints -- A NILM Case Study

Authors: Haokun Chen, Yu Tong, Yehai Chen

Abstract: Many sensing tasks obtain training labels only by deploying instruments in the field. With a limited number of sensor kits, a collection deadline, and measurement downtime at every move, the collector must repeatedly decide whether to stay at the current site or relocate. We study this decision in non-intrusive load monitoring (NILM), which estimates the power drawn by individual appliances from a home's main meter and is trained on data from homes temporarily fitted with appliance-level sub-meters. In NILM, appliance usage varies with the appliance, season and climate, and the value of new data depends on how diverse the combinations of target operation and background load are. To address this, we propose a constraint-based relocation framework and instantiate it for NILM as ReCo (Relocation by Coverage gain). ReCo counts new operating regimes in a joint target--background feature space, forecasts each home's future gain from the data collected so far, and each night weighs the gain of staying against the gain of moving elsewhere after the downtime. In replayed deployments on the Plegma dataset under two kit counts and two downtime costs, ReCo outperforms fixed-dwell and count-based schedules and a threshold rule using the same metric in every setting. Its advantage is not explained by collecting more days alone and is consistent with allocating the days to more valuable homes and periods.

replace From Data to Program: Fast & Direct Generative Program Inference from Empirical Data

Authors: Simon Kl\"uttermann, Xueying Ding, Leman Akoglu

Abstract: Estimating probability densities from a finite set of samples typically requires dataset-specific model fitting. We introduce PRODiGI, a pretrained data-to-program model that infers an explicit, executable generative program in a single forward pass. Pretrained on synthetic datasets paired with their ground-truth programs, PRODiGI accommodates diverse generative families and data dimensionalities through template prediction and non-autoregressive program parameter decoding. Its inferred programs support direct sampling, density and score evaluation, and inspection independently of the pretrained model. We further introduce program-space fine-tuning, which refines differentiable program parameters by maximizing the likelihood of empirical data while keeping model parameters intact. Experiments show that PRODiGI-FT achieves better density and score metrics than existing pretrained models, while offering multi-fold speedups over its closest competitors. Program-space fine-tuning further reduces generation MMD by 90%. By turning empirical data into explicit, reusable programs, PRODiGI introduces a new direction for fast, interpretable tabular generative modeling.

replace When a Flatness Proxy Is Not a Function: Robustness Certificates and Training Interventions

Authors: Vicente Opazo

Abstract: A valid curvature upper bound need not justify either a robustness certificate or an intervention on an intrinsic predictor property. We demonstrate this distinction for a last-layer relative-flatness proxy used in both settings. First, empirical-risk stationarity does not eliminate pointwise first-order loss terms: at a finite global empirical-risk minimum, the retained certificate expression underestimates a loss increase by over $210\times$. We derive a globally valid, gauge-invariant feature-space repair. Second, common-row softmax shifts preserve predictions and the exact contraction while making the proxy unbounded. Even standard reference-class choices double it on average relative to the centered representation. For a single fixed-feature example with at least three classes, scalar retuning generically cannot align the induced probability updates. Row centering gives the orbit-minimized bound and restores value and full-model gradient invariance under this symmetry. Across 45 paired one-step tests on algorithmic and image models, amplified shifts separate raw-regularized predictors while quotient-regularized predictors remain aligned. Long-horizon CIFAR-10 experiments show substantial, reversible suppression of generalization, while evidence for selective delay after memorization is less consistent. Together, these results show that validity as a curvature upper bound does not by itself justify either inversion into a robustness certificate or differentiation into an intrinsic training intervention.

replace Parameterization method of reservoir properties for ensemble-based data assimilation using intermediate latent space of StyleGAN

Authors: Marcio A. Sampaio, Paulo H. Ranazzi, Martin J. Blunt

Abstract: Ensemble smoothers are the most successful and efficient techniques currently available for history matching. However, because these methods rely on Gaussian assumptions, their performance is severely degraded when the prior geology is described in terms of complex facies distributions (non-Gaussian). In this way, for these methods, we need to apply efficient parameterization techniques. Currently, the most efficient methods for performing parameterization are deep learning models. However, given the variety of existing deep learning models, studies have not identified which is most suitable for use with ensemble-based methods, although some important models had already been evaluated. Based on a recent literature review, the most promising models selected were VAE-GAN, Latent Diffusion, and StyleGAN models. As a novel aspect of this work, data assimilation with the second generation of StyleGAN (StyleGAN2) model was performed using the latent z-space and intermediate w-space, separately. They were applied in two 2D case studies: one categorical (three facies) and the other continuous. The results demonstrated that all three models are highly efficient, with the StyleGAN2 model standing out for generating samples with geological realism and achieving excellent data matching in the cases studied. Our findings show that performing data assimilation with StyleGAN2 using the intermediate space (w-space) yielded better results than the traditional application in the latent space (z-space). This is due to the fact that ESMDA uses linear updates and the w-space is much more linear and disentangled than the highly entangled z-space, thereby ensuring that the updated vectors remain close to realistic geological patterns. These results were validated using main geostatistical and history matching metrics.

replace Cross-Benchmark Transfer from RL on Agentic Coding Tasks

Authors: Sushant Mehta, Logan Ritchie, Edwin Chen

Abstract: Coding agents often fail in the last mile: they build most of a feature but drop a requirement, test only the cases their implementation already handles, break behavior that was supposed to stay intact, or validate against an unchecked assumption. We ask whether reinforcement learning (RL) on expert-built agentic coding tasks closes this gap, and whether what the agent learns transfers beyond the training distribution. We post-train Kimi K2.7 Code, a 1T-parameter (32B active) open-weight mixture-of-experts model, with RL alone on 1,700 tasks: 1,000 repository tasks graded by hidden fail-to-pass tests and by pass-to-pass tests of existing behavior, and 700 terminal tasks graded by expert-written hidden verifiers. The reward is the fraction of target checks passed and drops to zero if any pass-to-pass test fails. One epoch of GSPO on a rank-32 LoRA adapter improves pass@1 on each of the six external benchmarks we evaluated, across three agent harnesses: SWE-Bench Pro (60.1 to 64.8), DeepSWE (31.0 to 43.4), Terminal-Bench 2.1 (67.4 to 82.0), Terminal-Bench 3 (1.4 to 12.1), Terminal-Bench 4 (0.0 to 7.6), and SWE-Marathon (5.0 to 25.0). Pooled over the five independent task sets (Terminal-Bench 4 revises Terminal-Bench 3), the improvement is significant (p < 0.001), and it remains significant on the three sets released after the training data was collected (p = 0.004); the model also improves under both harnesses never used in training. Median trajectories on DeepSWE and Terminal-Bench 3 are 24-35% shorter in agent steps. The base model's failed DeepSWE runs are mostly near-misses, and on the tasks the trained model newly solves, paired trajectories show it avoiding each of the four failure modes above.

replace Nearly Optimal Fixed-Confidence Best-Arm Identification with 1-Bit Feedback

Authors: Khang Luong, Dinh Thai Son, Hoang Ta, Hung The Tran, Tuan Quang Dam

Abstract: We study fixed-confidence best-arm identification under strict 1-bit feedback constraints. At each round, the learner selects an arm and a query set, and receives only a single bit indicating whether the sampled reward belongs to that set. We consider a distribution-free finite-variance setting with arm-wise localization, where direct empirical mean estimation is no longer available and clipping becomes unavoidable. We first formulate a time-uniform 1-bit mean-estimation primitive based on randomized threshold queries and a clipped tail-integral identity. We then embed this primitive into candidate-challenger best-arm identification algorithms. A fixed-clipping algorithm gives a simple anytime $(\epsilon,\delta)$-PAC guarantee, while a phased adaptive-clipping algorithm matches the clipping level to the current resolution and yields a gap-adaptive sample complexity. We also prove a $K$-arm worst-case information-theoretic lower bound showing that the logarithmic penalty caused by finite-variance 1-bit feedback is intrinsic. This bound matches the leading dependence of the phased algorithm up to logarithmic factors.

replace What Should World Models Forget? Stratified Retention for Continual Adaptation

Authors: Nishit Anand, Ramani Duraiswami, Dinesh Manocha

Abstract: Continual learning treats any drop in performance on previously seen data as forgetting, a convention inherited from settings where the prediction target is stationary. World models violate this assumption: their target is the environment, which changes, so knowledge that was accurate when learned can later become false, and revising it is correct behavior rather than a defect. Non-stationary ground truth is well studied in the concept drift literature and in the temporal factuality of language models, but it has not been formulated for world models, which are distinctive in that they also encode structural knowledge, such as physical laws and object permanence, that must never be degraded. We argue that continual world models need retention stratified by invariance timescale, the time over which knowledge stays true: invariants should be refined but never degraded, while instance-level facts should be revised once the environment changes. We show that standard forgetting metrics, used alone, count correct revision as forgetting and so rank a frozen model highest. We propose differential retention, which reports two quantities separately: invariant retention, checked after every adaptation round, and revision latency, the number of observations a model needs to pick up a change.

replace Lightweight Semantic EEG Foundation Model for Frozen Cross-Disorder Transfer

Authors: Rita Huan-Ting Peng, Nhat Bui

Abstract: Large-scale EEG foundation models have demonstrated promising transferability across neurological disorders, but often require millions of parameters and substantial computational resources. In this paper, we present the Universal Semantic EEG Foundation Model (USE-FM), a lightweight EEG foundation model that learns transferable neural representations through self-supervised signal reconstruction on the Temple University Hospital EEG Corpus (TUEG). After pretraining, the encoder is frozen and evaluated on two clinically distinct downstream tasks, abnormal EEG detection (TUAB) and epileptic seizure recognition (TUEP), using a unified frozen-transfer protocol against recent EEG foundation models, including LUNA-Base and CBraMod. With only 1.46 million parameters, approximately one-fifth the size of existing models, USE-FM achieves competitive overall performance, including strong sensitivity and F1-score on TUEP (SEN $75.00 \pm 14.14$, F1 $70.37 \pm 4.01$), while maintaining competitive performance on TUAB (AUC $85.24 \pm 5.61$). Beyond downstream classification, latent representation analysis using $k$-means clustering together with PCA and t-SNE demonstrates that USE-FM learns organized semantic EEG representations comparable to substantially larger foundation models. These results suggest that large-scale self-supervised pretraining enables lightweight architectures to learn transferable semantic EEG representations, providing a computationally efficient foundation for cross-disorder analysis and future clinical decision support in neurological disorders.

replace Similar Predictive Fit but Different Latent Dynamics: Characterizing Learned Dynamical Structure in Personalized Models of Brain Disorders

Authors: Rita Huan-Ting Peng, Nhat Bui

Abstract: As AI models move toward clinical decision-making and personalized treatment, understanding \emph{what} a model learns is important beyond predictive accuracy alone. We investigate whether personalized latent dynamics reveal clinically associated differences even when predictive fit is similar. A lightweight CNN--Transformer EEG foundation model pretrained on the Temple University EEG Corpus (TUEG) extracts segment-level representations. Using the Temple University Epilepsy Corpus (TUEP), representations are mapped to a shared latent-state space, and sparse multinomial logistic transition distributions (mLTD) are fit independently to each subject to obtain personalized transition-dependency graphs $W_n$. Analyses include $n{=}198$ subjects (99 epilepsy / 99 non-epilepsy). At $k{=}4$, epilepsy subjects exhibit substantially denser learned dependency structure ($p{=}1.1\times10^{-7}$), with the same pattern at $k{=}6$ (19.90 vs. 13.46; $p{=}5.2\times10^{-5}$). Graph-derived features provide moderate group discrimination under 5-fold subject-wise cross-validation (AUROC 0.68 at $k{=}4$; 0.65 at $k{=}6$). In contrast, held-out log-likelihood is nearly identical between groups at $k{=}4$ ($-0.992$ vs. $-0.991$; $p{=}0.95$), with similarly matched next-state prediction (AUROC 0.855 vs. 0.861; $p{=}0.54$). Thus, similar predictive fit does not imply similar learned dynamics: groups can be comparably predictable while differing substantially in the internal dynamical structure learned by personalized models. This distinction motivates evaluating learned structure alongside predictive performance in personalized clinical models.

replace PATTERN Can Be Solved from Memory: Testing Graph Neural Networks on Unseen Patterns

Authors: Amin Najafgholizadeh, Mohsen Afsharchi

Abstract: PATTERN asks graph neural networks to find a planted subgraph in a new host graph. The hosts change, but the benchmark draws just 100 patterns and reuses them across training and test splits. A lookup that extracts these patterns from the training labels achieves 96.6% class-weighted accuracy on the official test split, above every published result, without fitting a model or consulting the generator. On unseen patterns, the same lookup scores 50.6%. A matcher that also uses the generator reaches 99.2% on the official split. Among five graph networks we retrained, GRASS and PPGT go from the top two on the official split to the bottom two on unseen patterns. Each loses 8.1 percentage points even when familiar and unseen patterns are matched in size. Logistic regression on two-hop node statistics scores above every run of the five networks on unseen patterns. For CKGConv, the advantage follows the training patterns: it reappears with each of three independently drawn pattern libraries and disappears when every training and validation graph gets a new pattern. Generator-based inference also scores 82.3% on CLUSTER, compared with a published best of 81.6%. On Minesweeper's released board, four of eight planned chains produced samples within the time budget; none converged, but their pooled predictions reached 99.86% ROC AUC, above the published best of 99.55%. PATTERN's official split tests its intended task of finding familiar patterns in new graphs. We recommend reporting the same checkpoint's score on unseen patterns alongside it.

replace Mode Dynamics: A Framework for Neural Network Learning

Authors: Hossein Honarvar

Abstract: We introduce Mode Dynamics, a framework that connects coordinated parameter motion during neural network learning to changes in model outputs. Starting from a recorded parameter trajectory, we decompose its updates into collective modes and map each mode to its state-dependent output effect. The framework organizes this link through three mechanisms. Decomposition identifies collective modes in parameter motion and their time-varying amplitudes. Reconstruction tests whether the effects induced by those modes recover local and accumulated output change. Concentration quantifies how behavioral contribution is distributed across modes. Controlled scalar regression and next-token experiments directly test these mechanisms. They show that small sets of leading modes reconstruct behavior, induced effects depend on the training path, and modal complexity varies with task and data. In a controlled induction task, suppressing the leading modes during training impairs held-out accuracy, whereas matched controls do not. We then evaluate the framework on held-out image and language-model outputs using CIFAR-100 autoencoding and a public Pythia-70M pretraining trajectory. Across these settings, the results connect compact modal subspaces of recorded parameter motion to evolving behavior.

replace Causal-prior-constrained deep learning for rapid-intensification warning of western North Pacific tropical cyclones

Authors: Xinrui Li, Yijun Jin, Yifan Dong, Shoujuan Shu

Abstract: Rapid intensification (RI) is one of the most dangerous and unpredictable phases of tropical cyclones, and its environmental and structural drivers are difficult to identify and separate in observations. Here we introduce CausalVisionRI, a causal-prior-constrained deep-learning system for 24-h RI warning in the western North Pacific. It integrates infrared remote sensing imagery, atmospheric and oceanic fields, and an observationally discovered causal graph into a unified model. The graph and eight sign-verified effects on 24-h intensity change provide an auditable causal prior for training. Discovered from 522 cyclones in 2000-2017, it identifies causal pathways linking atmospheric and oceanic conditions, the cyclone's dynamical and structural state, and its 24-h intensity change. On a forward holdout covering 2020-2024 and 3,004 records, the five-member ensemble achieves a Brier skill score (BSS) of 0.259 relative to a fixed development climatology, significantly exceeding four reference systems; the three LDA baselines reach at most 0.105. It also attains higher detection rates than a published knowledge-block scheme and a reconstructed contrastive-learning scheme at matched false-detection rates under our USA 30-kt standard and the published RITCF standard. Ablations show that the graph pathway adds 0.032 BSS across the holdout and 0.063 over 2020-2021, indicating that the gain concentrates in the time period adjacent to the estimation window. A deep-learning model constrained by observationally discovered causal structure thus offers a new route to physically informed RI warning.

replace Watch Your Step: Adam's Margin Maximization Bias Depends on the Learning Rate Schedule

Authors: Eitan Gronich, Gal Vardi

Abstract: We study the implicit bias of Adam and its similarity to sign gradient descent on homogeneous models in binary classification tasks. Perhaps surprisingly, we uncover that these depend on the learning rate schedule. While previous works studied strongly decaying learning rate schedules $\eta(t)=\mathrm o \left(t^{1/L-1}\right)$, where $L$ is the homogeneity degree, and showed $\ell_\infty$-margin-maximization biases, we focus on the more common complementary regime, including constant and weakly decaying schedules. For a polynomial schedule $t^{\alpha}$ and homogeneity degree $L$, we show that when $\alpha > \frac 1L - \frac12$, Adam has no trajectory-independent margin-maximization bias for deep linear networks (in the KKT sense): first, there is no non-trivial, locally Lipschitz, convex implicit regularizer in this setting, including any squared norm. Second, on width-$1$ networks, the result holds without requiring convexity, implying that any implicit regularizer for wide networks must be trivial when restricted to a width-$1$ network. Nevertheless, we positively characterize the implicit bias in this regime as maximizing a trajectory-dependent weighted-$\ell_2$ margin for any $L$-homogeneous model. The fundamental mechanism behind our results is convergence of Adam's preconditioner to a trajectory-dependent vector, which also allows showing that the limiting parameter direction depends on initialization. In the intermediate regime $\frac 1L - 1 \leq \alpha \leq \frac1L - \frac12$ we prove an $\ell_\infty$ bias, conditional on gradient directional convergence to a full-support vector. Of independent interest, we establish a loss decay rate for full batch Adam on general homogeneous models, whose leading order depends only on the second-moment parameter.

replace An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Jianhong Wang, Yang Li, Samuel Kaski, Jonathan Lawry

Abstract: Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We propose an axiomatic framework that derives value function learning from the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry and Linearity), treating them as structural constraints rather than heuristic design choices. This formulation builds on a novel NAHT decomposition, which characterises the fundamental difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and establishes a cooperative game-theoretic foundation for learning. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published on https://github.com/hsvgbkhgbv/axiomatic-rl-naht.

replace FedSharp: Byzantine Robust Decentralized Federated Learning Under Noisy Channel

Authors: Ojaswi Sinha, Amin Farajzadeh, Animesh Yadav, Melike Erol-Kantarci

Abstract: Decentralized federated learning (DFL) eliminates the central aggregation server but remains vulnerable to non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) data, Byzantine attacks, and channel noise. We show that channel noise accumulates as models propagate across nodes and becomes increasingly significant as the learning rate decays. Moreover, accumulated noise enables Byzantine attackers to exploit loss-based model selection by mimicking the average of the honest models. To address these challenges, we propose FedSharp (screened, hop-aware, averaged, ranked, proximal), a robust DFL framework. In FedSharp, each node keeps the latest models received from its 2b predecessors, which guarantees at least b honest candidates when at most b nodes are Byzantine. FedSharp screens suspicious models using their deviations from the coordinate-wise median, averages the b lowest-loss candidates to reduce the fresh channel-noise variance by a factor of b, and applies hop-aware gradient-norm regularization and proximal training to mitigate accumulated noise and non-IID drift. Experiments on a 100-node ring with 33 Byzantine nodes show that FedSharp achieves 93.5 to 94.9 percent accuracy on MNIST, compared with 29.4 to 38.1 percent for a state-of-the-art single-model-selection benchmark, and 32.8 to 48.8 percent on CIFAR-10, compared with 10.0 to 30.5 percent, across Gaussian and hidden attacks under IID and non-IID data. On MNIST, FedSharp comes within 1.3 percentage points of the benchmark operating without channel noise, and exceeds it under the hidden attack.

replace Who Pays for the Knowledge Models Copy? Model Collapse as Lossy Transmission and Rogers' Paradox

Authors: Nam H. Le

Abstract: Generative models trained on the output of earlier models lose knowledge across generations, rare knowledge first. The remedies that work all bring in information from outside the loop, such as fresh human data, verifiers, or curators, and the theory of model collapse treats that supply as given. We argue that cultural evolution has a theory of the supply, and that it distinguishes two ways copying loses knowledge. In a fixed world, copies are lossy, as in Henrich's account of skill loss in small populations. In a changing world, even perfect copies go stale, which is Rogers' paradox. We write both as one chain with separately measurable parameters and test it on lineages of language models (word trigrams and a character transformer on two books, and OPT-125m on wikitext-2, on arXiv abstracts written after its pretraining, and on a world of 2,000 fictional facts that change at a set rate). In a fixed world, neutral drift on word frequencies predicts, with no free parameter, the rare-word coverage of every trigram lineage to within 0.03. In the fact world, the model's accuracy falls and its outdated answers rise with the rate of change; the chain calibrated on the static world predicts both for mid- and low-frequency facts to within 0.03 on average, with the rate of change set, not fitted. Models that inherit their weights keep more in a static world and carry more outdated facts in a changing one, as Rogers' theory says of individual memory. Finally we close the loop with the people who write the data. When they can ask the model instead of finding out, the model ends up exactly as reliable as finding out is costly, and in both regimes someone must keep paying to learn; we give the share in closed form, extend it to lossy copying, heterogeneous costs, aggregating models, and users who check, and show when checking is a stable way out.

replace PINR-ESI: Physics-Informed Self-Supervised Implicit Neural Representation Framework for EEG Extended-Source Imaging

Authors: Zhiyong Zhou, Sheng Liao, Xiaolin Li, Youshen Xiao, Bin Dai, Surui Liu, Yakang Dai

Abstract: EEG extended-source imaging aims to localize active cortical regions and characterize their extent, yet supervised inverse networks typically depend on synthetic EEG-source pairs and may be sensitive to synthetic-to-real mismatch. We propose a physics-informed self-supervised implicit neural representation (INR) framework for spatial source reconstruction without paired source labels or simulation-based supervised pretraining. The lead-field matrix encodes the source-to-sensor volume-conduction model and constrains the INR through agreement between predicted and measured EEG. Cortical current density is represented as a continuous coordinate-based neural field. Forward EEG prediction samples the field at cortical vertices and applies local smoothing before lead-field mapping. A classical inverse estimate provides warm initialization and observation-specific spatial guidance through prior-guided support regularization. Simulations across varying SNRs, source extents, and source counts showed low spatial dispersion and good localization performance. Quantitative evaluation on clinical EEG recordings from epilepsy patients and qualitative analysis of scalp EEG during intracranial stimulation supported feasibility for real-world source imaging. These findings suggest the potential of physics-informed self-supervised INR for extended-source imaging.

replace How Much Is Left Matters: False Positives and False Negatives in Partial-Prompt Contamination Tests

Authors: Abhishek Anand

Abstract: Partial-prompt completion, where a model receives the first part of a benchmark problem and is checked for whether it reproduces the rest, is a widely used test for benchmark contamination. We audit this test on seven open-weight models using MATH-500 and recent AIME 2025/2026 problems, which serve as true negatives. Memorization flags depend strongly on how much text remains: for every model, problems with at most 8 leftover words are flagged several times more often than problems with at least 25 (31.5% vs. 5.5% for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), and half to three-quarters of all ROUGE-based flags come from short leftovers. On true negatives, a ROUGE-L >= 0.75 threshold flags up to 6.7% of problems at a 60% prefix, and at an 80% prefix even exact match flags 8.6% (pooled over models; 6.2% on AIME 2026, which postdates every model), against 0% at 40-60%. The test also misses contamination upstream: models distilled from Qwen2.5-Math, whose base is heavily flagged in prior work, show almost no model-specific recall. Calibrating each model against models from other families removes most flags while isolating a consistent Qwen2.5-specific signal at every prefix length. We recommend length-stratified reporting, prefixes of at most 60%, style-matched post-cutoff controls, and cross-family calibration.

replace Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks

Authors: Tingcheng Bian, Kangye Gong, Jinchang Luo, MingQuan Cheng, Feiran Liu, Ni Li, Xiaoling Xia, Haiwei Wang, Hui Li

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.

replace GigaEdge Spench: An SDK for Practical Streaming Speech Enhancement

Authors: Artem Sokolov, Artem Khaidarov, Yana Lysova, Alexander Gladkikh, Vitaliy Yulis

Abstract: We present GigaEdge Spench, an open-source SDK for streaming speech enhancement on CPU servers, mobile devices, and in browsers. Applications can build enhancement channels from neural denoising, normalization and dynamics blocks, sample-rate conversion, and acoustic echo cancellation where supported. These stages can be combined, while normalization can also run without a neural model. The release includes an 8 kHz INT8 denoiser and discriminative and hybrid 48 kHz FP32 models. A shared C++ core supports ONNX Runtime and TensorFlow Lite through native interfaces, Android and iOS SDKs, and WebAssembly integration. Independent stream contexts share model resources, and consecutive frames from one stream can be processed in a single inference call. We evaluate the released models on EARS-WHAM v2 and compare their quality and processing costs with RNNoise and DPDFNet2. All tested native and Web configurations process files faster than real time, although larger chunks require more time to collect input. We also measure how many streams a shared model can process on a CPU. Our contribution is a configurable deployment system and an experimental study of how model choice, chunk size, and execution environment affect its use. All reported SDK timing uses ONNX Runtime. Index Terms: speech enhancement, streaming inference, cross-platform deployment, temporal frame grouping, computational efficiency

replace Stochastic Projections and Rank-Aware Learning Rates for Federated LoRA

Authors: Saber Malekmohammadi, Kevin Kuo, Virginia Smith, Golnoosh Farnadi

Abstract: Fine-tuning large-scale foundation models across resource-constrained, distributed clients presents significant computational and communication challenges. While merging Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with Federated Learning (FL) enables rank-based flexibility, the interdependence of the adaptation matrices ($\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$) often complicates optimization. In this work, we theoretically demonstrate that LoRA fine-tuning with Gaussian initialization and a frozen $\mathbf{A}$ matrix acts as a stochastic approximation of full fine-tuning, where batch gradients are subject to a random, rank-dependent perturbation. Motivated by this insight, we propose freezing and periodically resampling $\mathbf{A}$ matrices at the client level. This strategy decouples the $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ gradients, diminishes LoRA low-rank constraints and reduces both computational and uplink communication overheads by 50\%, allowing for the reallocation of resources toward higher adaptation ranks. Furthermore, we show that in this frozen-$\mathbf{A}$ regime, the adaptation rank $r$ directly governs the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the perturbed gradients. Specifically, we find that the SNR increases linearly with $r$, implying that clients' learning rates must be dynamically scaled with their adaptation ranks for higher utility. Building on these foundations, we introduce SPRA-LoRA (Stochastic Projection Rank-Aware LoRA), a federated rank-aware fine-tuning strategy that utilizes frozen, resampled $\mathbf{A}$ matrices and rank-dependent learning rates for clients. We also prove its convergence with heterogeneous client resources. Our experiments across benchmark datasets demonstrate that SPRA-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with half the overhead on clients.

replace Conformal Risk Control for QoT-Aware Lightpath Feasibility Under Technology-Driven Domain Shift

Authors: Kiarash Rezaei, Omran Ayoub, Paolo Monti, Carlos Natalino

Abstract: Artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML)-based regression models support operational decision-making in optical networks but typically expose only point predictions, which carry no statistically controlled information about their reliability. This paper proposes a post-hoc conformal risk control (CRC) framework that keeps a pretrained AI/ML regressor fixed, provides a finite-sample guarantee on uncertainty band miscoverage, and maps the band to operational decisions through policy rules. The guarantee applies to interval miscoverage, while decision-error rates are evaluated empirically. We instantiate the framework for quality of transmission (QoT)-aware lightpath (LP) feasibility assessment, in which a regressor predicts optical signal-to-noise ratio (OSNR) under domain shifts caused by fiber, amplifier, and switching upgrades. Feature shift contribution scores identify the LP features whose relation to QoT changes the most across a technology transition, and two policies translate the bands into feasibility decisions, allowing operators to tune the trade-off between conservatively rejecting or aggressively accepting LP realizations. Experiments on open space-division multiplexing (SDM) QoT datasets over the CONUS topology show that, for a target coverage of 90%, the uncertainty bands meet the target in all 170 trials spanning 17 domain shifts and ten calibration-test partitions, whereas a source-only quantile regression (QR) baseline meets it in none. Under severe shifts, the efficiency-oriented (EO) policy empirically improves zero-shot (ZS) feasibility-decision accuracy from about 85% to above 99%, approaching a transfer learning (TL) policy while avoiding retraining. The framework provides finite-sample control of uncertainty band miscoverage for regression-based optical network decision support, with potential applications beyond QoT-aware LP feasibility assessment.

replace Profile-Based Auditing of Domestic Violence Survey Selection Across South Asia

Authors: Mohammad Abu Obaida Mullick, Rezaur Rahman Ratul, Mymuna Nourin, Md. Motaharul Islam

Abstract: Domestic violence (DV) data in the Demographic and Health Surveys (DHS) are collected through a specialized module whose household-level sampling and privacy requirements create a distinct observation process. We ask whether demographic, socioeconomic, and household characteristics form reproducible population profiles and whether these profiles contain information about selection into the DV module across South Asia. Using DHS Individual Recode data for 796,374 women aged 15-49 across Afghanistan, India, Myanmar, Nepal, and Pakistan, we harmonize 61 numeric and 8 categorical features, reduce the profile space with PCA, and identify six interpretable profiles. We then evaluate 22 machine-learning models under primary-sampling-unit-aware holdout validation. The best model, HistGradientBoosting, achieves ROC-AUC 0.9167 and PR-AUC 0.9612, versus 0.8475 and 0.9203 for a design-only baseline. Adding woman-level characteristics yields the largest incremental gain, while explicit interaction terms add little out-of-sample performance. Country-specific analyses reveal substantial heterogeneity, and leave-one-country-out evaluation shows that transferability of the incremental profile signal is limited. These results demonstrate that interpretable ML can audit differential coverage in sensitive survey modules beyond the formal sampling design.

replace Stealthy, High-Impact False Data Injection Attacks in Smart Grid Using Physics-Constrained GAN with Limited System Knowledge

Authors: Md. Mainul Islam, Hasan Kurban, Abdulrahman Takiddin, Muhammad Ismail, Rachad Atat, Erchin Serpedin

Abstract: False data injection (FDI) attacks can distort power system state estimation while retaining plausible measurement patterns. Existing FDI methods trade stealth for impact or vice versa. They often assume access to internal grids, all-bus sensing, or defender models. In this paper, we propose a detector-agnostic physics-constrained generative adversarial network (PCGAN) that learns decoupled power-flow sensitivities from historical measurements of deployed sensors. The learned active-power-angle and reactive-power-voltage-magnitude relationships guide perturbations restricted to compromised channels, while adversarial training promotes realistic spatiotemporal patterns. We implement PCGAN and seven existing FDI attacks on the IEEE 300-bus system using one year of observed hourly demand from PJM Interconnection, a regional transmission organization in the United States, and synthetic bus-level load profiles. We compare their performance across 10%, 30%, 60%, and 100% compromised sensors. Attack stealth is assessed against ten machine-learning (ML) detectors and conventional bad-data detection (BDD) methods, while physical impact is quantified through nonlinear alternating-current weighted least-squares state estimation. PCGAN achieves 84.28% mean ML evasion and 22.31% mean spatial load misperception across the four scenarios. Its mean stealth-impact trade-off score reaches 18.69%, compared with 5.61% for the strongest competing attack. PCGAN also achieves 83.45% chi-square BDD evasion and 86.02% largest-normalized-residual BDD evasion. These results demonstrate a favorable stealth-impact balance under varying sensor-access levels and provide challenging attack cases for evaluating FDI detectors.

replace CastWorld: Benchmarking Context-Aware Agentic Time Series Forecasting Beyond Predictive Accuracy

Authors: Bokai Pan, Ruijie Yang, Mingyue Cheng, Jiahao Wang, Xiaoyu Tao

Abstract: Agentic time series forecasting extends conventional approaches beyond one-shot numerical prediction toward autonomous, context-aware interaction. However, existing benchmarks offer limited integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. In this paper, we introduce CastWorld, an agentic benchmark for context-aware time series forecasting. First, its context-rich dataset suite spans 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, covering external variables, events, and historical cases under a unified context specification. Second, a lightweight, modular forecasting-native harness provides 49 core tools and a state-adaptive loop that tracks forecast versions and enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. Intermediate forecasts can expose information gaps, while newly acquired evidence guides subsequent revisions and final version selection. Third, model-level evaluation assesses forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. Also, systems-level evaluation compares predictive accuracy across forecasting systems. Experiments reveal distinct decision behaviors among agents with comparable accuracy. Additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts, and agents may fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models. CastWorld provides a reusable foundation for evaluating agentic time series forecasting under controlled conditions.

replace JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents

Authors: Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Ting-Yao Hu, Sanjoy Chowdhury, Fatih Ilhan, Oncel Tuzel, Raviteja Vemulapalli

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.

replace Rectified Looped Transformer

Authors: Musheng Li, Jiawei Zhang, Ziyuan Liu, Yuantao Gu

Abstract: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.

replace Detection Is Not Early Warning: Evaluating Token-Level Alarms on Language Model Failures

Authors: Marco De Negri, Luca Sartori, Lorenzo Baggi, Moritz Reihs, Eduard Frank \v{D}urech, Mennatallah El-Assady, Valentina Pyatkin, Anna Hedstr\"om

Abstract: Many failures of language models begin at a specific token, the onset. A monitor that reads the model during generation can classify the finished response, detect the failure once it is under way, or warn before it begins, and only a warning leaves time to stop or steer the generation. Monitors are usually evaluated with response-level metrics such as AUROC, which ignore when the monitor fires. We introduce PREWARN, an evaluation protocol for any failure with an annotated onset. It calibrates each monitor's threshold on validation data to an error rate chosen for the application, runs the monitor, and scores its alarms relative to the onset on all three tasks. As a case study we apply it to repetitive degeneration in three open language models, with early warning as the main task, using linear probes on activations and six untrained baselines. Response-level metrics saturate on this failure, and a rule that reads only the response length scores almost perfectly, while probes that AUROC ranks as equal warn very differently under PREWARN. Probes warn before the onset on most degenerate responses of two of the three models, low-rank adapters below the probe more than double the share of tokens just before the onset that it flags, largely by flagging degenerate responses long before the onset, and no untrained baseline warns much more often than random alarms. We release the probes with calibrated thresholds at https://huggingface.co/collections/marcodene/prewarn-degeneration-probes

URLs: https://huggingface.co/collections/marcodene/prewarn-degeneration-probes

replace Time Flies When You Parallelise: Pareto-efficient Time Series Foundation Models with Linear Recurrences

Authors: Torben Berndt, Lingyi Yang, Federico Nardi, Yi Heng Lim, AJ Shears, Benjamin Walker

Abstract: Time series foundation models have demonstrated strong zero-shot forecasting performance, but computational efficiency remains a central architectural challenge. Temporal self-attention incurs quadratic cost in the number of temporal tokens, while nonlinear recurrent state transitions restrict parallel computation along the temporal dimension. We introduce ci-proto, a family of time series foundation models based on Mamba-3, whose linear state recurrences support chunk-wise parallel-in-time computation during training, and inference costs that scale linearly with sequence length. The architecture alternates recurrent temporal processing with cross-variate attention, supporting multivariate probabilistic forecasting with past-only and future-known covariates. Across fev-bench's 100 forecasting tasks, ci-proto XL sets a new state of the art among time series foundation models. The five ci-proto variants also make up the entire Pareto frontier for mean scaled quantile loss and mean absolute scaled error against both parameter count and median runtime.

replace LazyWeights: Decode-on-Demand Edge LLMs

Authors: Arnab Sanyal, Gourav Datta, Sandeep P. Chinchali

Abstract: Memory decides which LLMs an edge device runs, and how fast: capacity on a 4 GB Jetson Nano, bandwidth on an 8 GB Jetson Orin Nano Super. Deployed formats (GPTQ, AWQ, llama.cpp's K- and I-quants) spend equal bits on every integer code of a matrix, although the codes are far from uniform. LazyWeights keeps these formats but tunes GPTQ so the codes compress, Huffman-codes them losslessly in interleaved lanes, and decodes them on demand in a small unit between DRAM and L2, so GPU kernels run unchanged and DRAM moves only coded bytes. At 1% and 3.5% perplexity budgets, coded Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B and SmolLM-1.7B are 5.7-31.4% smaller than the best llama.cpp format. A bit-exact 0.21 mm^2 ASIC decoder unit (1 GHz, 7 nm) cuts Orin latency and energy per token by up to 36% and 38% (54% and 44% with prompt-lookup speculation). On the Nano, LazyWeights needs 58-64% less time and 53-57% less energy per token than CPU llama.cpp, and runs a streamed Mistral-7B 6.6x faster at 3.89 bits per weight. A Huffman decoder beats an rANS decoder in rate and area at equal throughput; the ASIC decoder unit runs bit-exact on a Zynq UltraScale+ FPGA (AMD ZCU102).

replace A Convex-Analytic Reformulation for Poisoning Attacks on Logistic Regression

Authors: Carina Moreira Costa, Maria Eduarda Pinheiro, Martin Schmidt

Abstract: We study data poisoning attacks on logistic regression with $\ell_2$-regularization, where an attacker chooses the features of a fixed number of injected observations with prescribed labels to maximize the logistic loss on the clean training data. Strong convexity of the defender's problem yields an exact reformulation through its first-order optimality conditions. Using convex conjugacy of the softplus function, we replace the sigmoid relations by a single convex optimality-gap inequality and express the attacker's objective as a concave function. A change of variables makes the stationarity condition affine, leaving bounded bilinear equalities as the only nonconvex constraints. We derive finite bounds on all variables involved in these equalities, including a global bound on the coefficient norm and tighter bounds obtained by preprocessing based on convex optimization. Experiments on eight binary classification datasets evaluate attack effectiveness and compare the two reformulations when solved with SCIP. Attacks obtained by local optimization degrade predictive performance on most datasets, while the convex-analytic formulation produces substantially stronger reported dual bounds within a fixed time limit.

replace-cross LEGAL-UQA: Testing Constitutional Question-Answering Systems for Urdu Speakers with a Parallel English-Urdu Benchmark

Authors: Faizan Faisal, Umair Yousaf, Muhammad Hassnain, Zeerak Babar

Abstract: Legal information assistants could help Urdu speakers understand their constitutional rights, but building reliable systems requires test data and evaluation methods suited to the language. We present LEGAL-UQA, a parallel English-Urdu benchmark of 619 question-answer pairs linked to passages from Pakistan's Constitution. Each example includes the question, reference answer, and source passage in both languages. We evaluate two components separately: answering Urdu questions when the source passage is supplied, and retrieving the passage from which each question was generated. We also compare JEV, a decision model used as an automated judge, with human grades. The findings show that model rankings depend on how answer similarity is measured, while a simple word-matching method retrieves source passages more effectively than the embedding methods tested. At the evaluated cutoff, JEV agrees with human grades less often than a baseline that gives every answer full credit. An audit also identifies cases where generated references omit information supported by the source. Together, these findings highlight the need to test retrieval and answering separately, include simple retrieval baselines, and validate automated judges and reference answers with human review when engineering legal QA software for Urdu speakers.

replace-cross Bias-Optimal Bounds for Stochastic Gradient Descent

Authors: Daniel Cortild, Lucas Ketels, Juan Peypouquet, Guillaume Garrigos

Abstract: The non-asymptotic analysis of Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) typically yields bounds that decompose into a bias term and a variance term. In this work, we focus on the bias component and study the extent to which SGD can match the optimal convergence behavior of deterministic gradient descent. Assuming only (strong) convexity and smoothness of the objective, we derive new bounds for the full range of constant step-sizes $\gamma L \in (0,2)$. This includes critical and large step-size regimes that were previously unexplored without additional variance assumptions, in both the convex and strongly convex cases. Those bounds are bias-optimal, in the sense that the bias term coincides with the worst-case rate of gradient descent, for all non-critical step-sizes. For critical step-sizes, we show that no bounds can be obtained with an optimal bias term, but that this optimal bias can be approximated arbitrarily closely. As an immediate application, we provide new complexity bounds for the Stochastic Proximal algorithm, without the need of regularity assumptions.

replace-cross Recovering the Euclidean Geometry of High-Dimensional Data Under Heterogeneous Noise

Authors: Keyi Li, Yuval Kluger, Boris Landa

Abstract: Pairwise Euclidean distances provide a simple geometric representation of high-dimensional data and underlie many data analysis methods, including clustering, visualization, and manifold learning. However, in real-world applications, these distances can be severely corrupted by heterogeneous noise, where different data points are subject to different, and potentially large, noise levels. Such noise distorts the distances inhomogeneously, leading to a misrepresentation of the underlying data geometry. In this work, we ask whether the underlying clean Euclidean geometry can nevertheless be recovered from the corrupted pairwise distances. We answer this question affirmatively, showing that, perhaps surprisingly, such recovery is possible across a broad class of high-dimensional settings with underlying low-dimensional geometry, even when the noise magnitudes vary substantially across observations. We develop a principled approach for recovering the clean pairwise distances, without prior knowledge of either the clean data structure or the noise distribution, and establish probabilistic recovery guarantees. In particular, the recovery error converges to zero at polynomial rates as the feature dimension and dataset size increase simultaneously. Experiments on synthetic datasets demonstrate accurate recovery of the Euclidean geometry in challenging regimes and improved robustness of downstream methods that rely on pairwise distances. When applied to real single-cell RNA sequencing data, our method reliably recovers local neighborhood structure, enabling accurate nearest-neighbor identification used in many downstream analyses.

replace-cross Cyber-Zero: Training Cybersecurity Agents without Runtime

Authors: Terry Yue Zhuo, Dingmin Wang, Hantian Ding, Varun Kumar, Zijian Wang

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in software engineering tasks when trained with executable runtime environments, particularly in resolving GitHub issues. However, such runtime environments are often unavailable in other domains, especially cybersecurity, where challenge configurations and execution contexts are ephemeral or restricted. We present Cyber-Zero, the first runtime-free framework for synthesizing high-quality agent trajectories to train cybersecurity LLMs. Cyber-Zero leverages publicly available CTF writeups and employs persona-driven LLM simulation to reverse-engineer runtime behaviors and generate realistic, long-horizon interaction sequences without actual environments. Our best model, Cyber-Zero-32B, establishes new state-of-the-art performance among open-weight models, matching the capabilities of proprietary systems like DeepSeek-V3-0324 and Claude-3.5-Sonnet while offering superior cost-effectiveness, and demonstrating that runtime-free trajectory synthesis can effectively democratize the development of state-of-the-art cybersecurity agents.

Authors: Terry Yue Zhuo, Dingmin Wang, Hantian Ding, Varun Kumar, Zijian Wang

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional capabilities when trained within executable runtime environments, notably excelling at software engineering tasks through verified feedback loops. replace-cross Toric geometry of ReLU neural networks

Authors: Yaoying Fu

Abstract: Given a continuous piecewise linear function $f:\mathbb{R}^{n_0} \to \mathbb{R}$ and a fixed architecture of feedforward ReLU neural networks, the exact function realization problem asks when a network of that architecture realizes $f$. In contrast, expressivity concerns characterizing all functions exactly realizable by a given architecture. To address these questions systematically, we establish a connection between toric geometry and ReLU neural networks, allowing tools from algebraic geometry to analyze the structures. Starting with an unbiased ReLU network with rational weights, we define the ReLU fan, the ReLU toric variety, and the ReLU Cartier divisor associated with the network. This work also relates the tropical and the toric geometry: the former captures combinatorial data of ReLU networks, while the latter recovers the underlying geometric structure. As an application, we obtain a complete characterization of the functions realizable by unbiased shallow ReLU networks with rational weights. The characterization is expressed in terms of intersection numbers of ReLU Cartier divisors with torus-invariant curves and leads to a decomposition of piecewise-linear functions into shallow and non-shallow components.

replace-cross E0: Expressive Fine-Grained Discrete Action Prediction for Vision-Language-Action Models via Tweedie Discrete Diffusion

Authors: Zhihao Zhan, Jiaying Zhou, Likui Zhang, Qinhan Lyu, Hao Liu, Weizheng Li, Jusheng Zhang, Ziliang Chen, Tianshui Chen, Ruifeng Zhai, Keze Wang, Liang Lin, Guangrun Wang

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a unified framework for robotic manipulation by integrating visual perception, language understanding, and control generation. However, existing VLA systems still struggle to generalize across diverse tasks, scenes, and camera viewpoints, and often produce coarse or unstable actions. We argue that these limitations are closely tied to the structural properties of actions in VLA settings, including the inherent multi-peaked nature of action distributions, the token-based symbolic reasoning of pretrained VLM/VLA backbones, and the effective finite resolution imposed by real-world robotic control. Motivated by these properties, we introduce E0, a tweedie discrete diffusion framework that formulates action generation as iterative denoising over quantized action tokens. Experiments on LIBERO, VLABench, ManiSkill, and a real-world Franka arm demonstrate that E0 achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse environments, outperforming strong baselines by 8.1% on average.

replace-cross Model-Free Assessment of Simulator Fidelity via Quantile Curves

Authors: Garud Iyengar, Yu-Shiou Willy Lin, Kaizheng Wang

Abstract: As generative AI models are increasingly used to simulate real-world systems, quantifying the ``sim-to-real'' gap is critical. For each input setting of interest -- which we call a \emph{scenario}, such as a survey question or operating condition -- the real and simulated systems are associated with unobserved latent population parameters, and their discrepancy varies across scenarios. A fundamental challenge is that, for any given scenario, this discrepancy cannot be observed directly, since both systems are accessible only through finite samples, often of heterogeneous sizes across scenarios. Standard predictive inference methods are therefore ill-suited, as they quantify uncertainty in observable outputs rather than latent population parameters. To address this, we construct confidence sets for these latent parameters and use them to derive a robust proxy for the sim-to-real discrepancy. We then estimate the quantile function of this proxy to obtain a distribution-level risk profile of the simulator, which supports a broad range of statistical summaries, including statistical inference for the real output distribution in a new scenario, the calculation of risk measures like Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR), and principled comparisons across simulators. Our method is model-agnostic and handles general output spaces, such as categorical survey responses and continuous multi-dimensional data. We demonstrate the practical utility of this method by evaluating the alignment of four major LLMs with human populations on the WorldValueBench dataset.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is central to embodied cognition, yet contemporary AI systems still struggle to reason about physical interactions in open-world human environments. Despite strong performance on controlled benchmarks, vision-language models often fail to jointly model physical dynamics, reference frames, and the latent human intentions that drive spatial change. To evaluate TSI, we present EscherVerse, a large-scale open-world resource built from 11,328 real-world videos, including an 8,000-example benchmark and a 35,963-example instruction-tuning set. Fine-tuning on real-world, intent-aware data narrows this gap for open-weight models, but does not close it. replace-cross Tracing 3D Anatomy in 2D Strokes: Can Projection-Derived Masks Replace 3D Segmentation for Cervical Spine Fracture Recognition?

Authors: Fabi Nahian Madhurja, Rusab Sarmun, Muhammad E. H. Chowdhury, Adam Mushtak, Israa Al-Hashimi, Sohaib Bassam Zoghoul

Abstract: Cervical spine fractures require rapid and accurate diagnosis, yet automatic CT interpretation remains challenging as subtle injuries must be assessed across large 3D volumes. In the proposed multi-stage pipeline, a YOLOv8 detector localizes the cervical spine from multi-view variance projections (3D mean Intersection over Union 94.45%). No AUPRC difference was statistically significant, and fold-wise 95% confidence intervals excluded vertebra-level losses larger than 1.46 and 2.81 points, respectively. replace-cross HetCCL: Efficient LLM Training on Heterogeneous Vendor GPUs

Abstract: The rapid growth of large language models is driving organizations to expand their GPU clusters, often with GPUs from multiple vendors.
