replace-cross Adaptive Power Iteration Method for Differentially Private PCA

Authors: Ta Duy Nguyen, Alina Ene, Huy Le Nguyen

Abstract: We study $\left(\epsilon,\delta\right)$-differentially private algorithms for the problem of approximately computing the top singular vector of a matrix $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ where each row of $A$ is a data point in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$. Following Dwork-Talwar-Thakurta-Zhang (STOC 2014), we consider the privacy model where neighboring inputs differ by one single row. We give a novel algorithm that achieves beyond-worst-case guarantees for input matrices with low coherence, which is a structural property of matrices in many applications, including but not limited to i.i.d. data. Our algorithm contributes to the extensive literature on private power iteration methods, where we introduce a new filtering technique which adapts to this coherence parameter. Our work departs from and complements the work by Hardt-Roth (STOC 2013) which achieves beyond-worst-case guarantees for the more restrictive privacy model where neighboring inputs differ in one single entry by at most 1.

replace-cross On sparsity, extremal structure, and monotonicity properties of Wasserstein and Gromov-Wasserstein optimal transport plans

Authors: Titouan Vayer (COMPACT)

Abstract: This note gives a self-contained overview of some key properties of the Gromov-Wasserstein (GW) distance, compared with the standard linear optimal transport (OT) framework. More specifically, I explore the following questions: are GW optimal transport plans sparse? under what conditions does there exist an optimal plan supported on a permutation? do optimal plans satisfy a form of cyclical monotonicity? In particular, I present the conditionally negative/positive definite (CND/CPD) properties and show that, when they hold, there exist GW optimal plans that are sparse and supported on a permutation. In the last section, I also give examples where no permutation is optimal when the CND or CPD assumption is dropped.

replace-cross Uncertainty Quantification for Multimodal Large Language Models with Incoherence-adjusted Semantic Volume

Authors: Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Hieu Dao, Nicole Kan Hui Lin, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low

Abstract: Despite their capabilities, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) may produce plausible but erroneous outputs, hindering reliable deployment. Accurate uncertainty metrics could enable escalation of unreliable queries to human experts or larger models for improved performance. However, existing uncertainty metrics have practical constraints, such as being designed only for specific modalities, reliant on external tools, or computationally expensive. We introduce UMPIRE, a training-free uncertainty quantification framework for MLLMs that works efficiently across various input and output modalities without external tools, relying only on the models' own internal modality features. Inspired by Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), UMPIRE computes a novel incoherence-adjusted semantic volume of sampled MLLM responses for a given task instance, which couples semantic embedding dispersion with the quadratic entropy of the responses. This approach effectively captures both the global semantic diversity of samples and the local incoherence of responses based on internal model confidence. We provide theoretical analysis motivating UMPIRE's design, and extensive experiments show that UMPIRE consistently outperforms baseline metrics in error detection and uncertainty calibration across image, audio, and video-text benchmarks, even with small sample budgets. We also demonstrate UMPIRE's generalization to non-text output tasks, including image and audio generation. Our code is available at https://github.com/daohieu17ctt/UMPIRE.

URLs: https://github.com/daohieu17ctt/UMPIRE.

replace-cross Auto-differentiable data assimilation: Co-learning of states, dynamics, and filtering algorithms

Authors: Melissa Adrian, Daniel Sanz-Alonso, Rebecca Willett

Abstract: Data assimilation algorithms estimate the state of a dynamical system from partial observations, where the successful performance of these algorithms hinges on costly parameter tuning and on employing an accurate model for the dynamics. This paper introduces a framework for jointly learning the state, dynamics, and parameters of filtering algorithms in data assimilation through a process we refer to as auto-differentiable filtering. The framework leverages a theoretically motivated loss function that enables learning from partial, noisy observations via gradient-based optimization using auto-differentiation. We further demonstrate how several well-known data assimilation methods can be learned or tuned within this framework. To underscore the versatility of auto-differentiable filtering, we perform experiments on dynamical systems spanning multiple scientific domains, such as the Clohessy-Wiltshire equations from aerospace engineering, the Lorenz-96 system from atmospheric science, the generalized Lotka-Volterra equations from systems biology, and the two-layer quasi-geostrophic model from geophysical fluid dynamics. Finally, we provide guidelines for practitioners to customize our framework according to their observation model, accuracy requirements, and computational budget.

replace-cross DRTriton: Large-Scale Synthetic Data Driven Reinforcement Learning for Triton Kernel Generation

Authors: Siqi Guo, Ming Lin, Tianbao Yang

Abstract: Developing efficient CUDA kernels is a fundamental yet challenging task in the generative AI industry. Recent research leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically convert PyTorch reference implementations to CUDA kernels, significantly reducing engineering effort. State-of-the-art LLMs, such as GPT-5.2 and Claude-Sonnet-4.5, still struggle with this task. To address this challenge, we propose DRTriton, a scalable learning framework for training LLMs to convert PyTorch programs into highly optimized Triton kernels, which are then compiled to CUDA kernels at runtime. DRTriton consists of three key components: (i) a data synthetic algorithm CSP-DAG that guarantees full coverage and unbiased uniform sampling over the operator space with controlled difficulty; (ii) a curriculum RL framework with decoupled rewards that jointly optimizes conversion success rate and execution speed; and (iii) a test-time search algorithm that further improves the execution speed of the generated Triton kernels. With a warmup stage of SFT on limited PyTorch-Triton pairs curated using existing LLMs, DRTriton trained by RL on synthesized PyTorch programs generalizes effectively to real-world CUDA kernels that are challenging even for human experts. Experimental results show that DRTriton-7B achieves speedup over PyTorch on 92% of KernelBench Level 2 tasks, compared to 23% for GPT-5.2 and 19% for Claude-Sonnet-4.5.

replace-cross Minimizing classical resources in variational measurement-based quantum computation for generative modeling

Authors: Arunava Majumder, Hendrik Poulsen Nautrup, Hans J. Briegel

Abstract: Measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC) is a framework for quantum information processing in which a computational task is carried out through one-qubit measurements on a highly entangled resource state. Due to the indeterminacy of the outcomes of a quantum measurement, the random outcomes of these operations, if not corrected, yield a variational quantum channel family. Traditionally, this randomness is corrected through classical processing in order to ensure deterministic unitary computations. Recently, variational measurement-based quantum computation (VMBQC) has been introduced to exploit this measurement-induced randomness to gain an advantage in generative modeling. A limitation of this approach is that the corresponding channel model has twice as many parameters compared to the unitary model, scaling as $N \times D$, where $N$ is the number of logical qubits (width) and $D$ is the depth of the VMBQC model. This can often make optimization more difficult and may lead to poorly trainable models. In this paper, we present a restricted VMBQC model that extends the unitary setting to a channel-based one using only a single additional trainable parameter. We show, both numerically and algebraically, that this minimal extension is sufficient to generate probability distributions that cannot be learned by the corresponding unitary model.

replace-cross mlr3torch: A Deep Learning Framework in R based on mlr3 and torch

Authors: Sebastian Fischer, Lukas Burk, Carson Zhang, Bernd Bischl, Martin Binder

Abstract: Deep learning (DL) has become a cornerstone of modern machine learning (ML) praxis. We introduce the R package mlr3torch, which is an extensible DL framework for the mlr3 ecosystem. It is built upon the torch package, and simplifies the definition, training, and evaluation of neural networks for both tabular data and generic tensors (e.g., images) for classification and regression. The package implements predefined architectures, and torch models can easily be converted to mlr3 learners. It also allows users to define neural networks as graphs. This representation is based on the graph language defined in mlr3pipelines and allows users to define the entire modeling workflow, including preprocessing, data augmentation, and network architecture, in a single graph. Through its integration into the mlr3 ecosystem, the package allows for convenient resampling, benchmarking, preprocessing, and more. We explain the package's design and features and show how to customize and extend it to new problems. Furthermore, we demonstrate the package's capabilities using three use cases, namely hyperparameter tuning, fine-tuning, and defining architectures for multimodal data. Finally, we present some runtime benchmarks.

replace-cross TransitLM: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Map-Free Transit Route Generation

Authors: Hanyu Guo, Jiedong Yang, Chao Chen, Longfei Xu, Kaikui Liu, Xiangxiang Chu

Abstract: Public transit route planning traditionally depends on structured map infrastructure and complex routing engines, and no existing dataset supports training models to bypass this dependency. We present TransitLM, a large-scale dataset of nearly 13 million transit route planning records from four Chinese cities covering 120,845 stations and 13,666 lines, released as a continual pre-training corpus and benchmark data for three evaluation tasks with complementary metrics. Experiments show that an LLM trained on TransitLM produces structurally valid routes at high accuracy and implicitly grounds arbitrary GPS coordinates to appropriate stations without any explicit mapping. These results demonstrate that transit route planning can be learned entirely from data, enabling end-to-end, map-free route generation directly from origin-destination information. The dataset and benchmark are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/GD-ML/TransitLM, with evaluation code at https://github.com/HotTricker/TransitLM.

URLs: https://huggingface.co/datasets/GD-ML/TransitLM,, https://github.com/HotTricker/TransitLM.

replace-cross SA-RAG: Structuring Noisy Conversations for Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Authors: Kaiqiao Han, LuAn Tang, Renliang Sun, Peng Yuan, Wei Cheng, Haoyu Wang, Wei Wang, Yizhou Sun, Haifeng Chen

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have been widely adopted in conversational applications. However, their reliance on parametric knowledge limits reliability in real-world scenarios that require dynamic or domain-specific information. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses this limitation by incorporating external knowledge during generation, but existing RAG methods often struggle with noisy or irrelevant contexts, as they typically assume that high-quality structures are already available. In this work, we propose Structure-aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation (SA-RAG), a principled structure induction and generation framework that constructs reliable intermediate structures directly from noisy conversational data. SA-RAG uses tables as compact and controllable structured representations. We introduce a quality-aware structure induction framework that jointly models semantic and structural consistency. Furthermore, we explore both training-free and training-based structure generation strategies to further improve structure quality while maintaining fidelity. Experiments on two noisy real-world datasets show that SA-RAG significantly outperforms existing RAG baselines. Further analysis highlights that high-quality structural representations are crucial for robust RAG applications.

replace-cross Kernel-based potential mean-field games with unbiased random Fourier $U$-statistics

Authors: Yumiharu Nakano

Abstract: We study the subclass of potential mean-field games in which the running interaction cost and the terminal target cost are both expressed through reproducing-kernel maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) penalties, and develop a computational framework that exploits this kernel structure. Both costs are estimated from finite-sample empirical distributions using a random Fourier U-statistic representation that is unbiased and has linear cost in the batch size. The drift of the controlled diffusion is parametrized by a neural network and trained via stochastic gradient descent. For population near-minimizers we prove convergence to the terminal-constrained problem as the penalty diverges, and show that the same limit is recovered almost surely when such controls are evaluated on independent finite samples under explicit coupling conditions on the penalty, random-feature count and sample size. The framework includes the kernel-MMD-penalty Schr{\"o}dinger bridge problem as the special case of a vanishing interaction cost. Numerical experiments illustrate the method on the Schr{\"o}dinger bridge problem in dimensions up to one hundred, and on an electric vehicle charging coordination problem with per-vehicle physical heterogeneity, where a control-dependent aggregate-demand cost produces a trade-off between peak normalized charging power and the terminal service level.

replace-cross Certified Policy Optimisation for Nested Causal Bandits via PAC-Bayes Risk

Authors: Tim Woydt, Paul-David Zuercher

Abstract: Critical sequential decisions are rarely single-timescale: a strategic decision causally shapes the context in which every subsequent tactical choice is made; standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory does not capture this causal coupling between timescales. We formalise the problem class as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs), a hierarchical SCM where each level's action sets the next level's context distribution, and propose Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS), which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively under it. Our main theoretical result is a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound that certifies any candidate deployment policy from historic data alone, off-policy and anytime, answering the deployment question: can we trust this agent here, and at what risk? Experiments on a hierarchical SCM show that, against a matched RFF-GP joint regression on the same function class, the factorised SCM-mechanism posterior transfers significantly better zero-shot under exogenous distribution shifts, the recursive meta-to-inner commit significantly dominates the joint-commit alternative in distribution, and the certificate significantly contracts as offline data accumulates. Combining these results, we establish progressive certified handover, a safe-deployment method: each timescale flips from a legacy controller to NCTS when gains can be certified, independently of the others.

replace-cross Larch: Learned Query Optimization for Semantic Predicates

Authors: Fuheng Zhao, Pawel Liskowski, Zihan Li, Benjamin Han, Puxuan Yu, Varich Boonsanong, Dimitris Tsirogiannis, Anupam Datta

Abstract: With the advent of Large Language Models (LLMs), many database systems introduced semantic operators that enabled analytical queries over unstructured data (e.g. text, images, videos). Semantic operators typically incur high inference costs and latencies making semantic (AI) SQL queries challenging to apply on large scale datasets. At the same time, their semantic nature leads database engines to treat them as black boxes, making AISQL queries difficult to optimize. In this paper, we introduce Larch, a framework for optimizing the execution of semantic filters in AI SQL queries. Larch was inspired by two key observations: i) the high latency of semantic operators leaves significant room for computationally-heavy runtime optimization techniques, ii) unstructured data are typically accompanied by semantic information in the form of embeddings allowing for efficient semantic comparisons between AI_FILTER prompts and data values. Based on these two key observations, we present two Larch variants: Larch-A2C and Larch-Sel. Larch-A2C encodes arbitrary semantic filters expression tree using an embedding-augmented Gated Graph Neural Network and formulates the filter evaluation order as a Markov decision process. In contrast, Larch-Sel leverages a supervised learning model to predict filter selectivities, subsequently applying dynamic programming to find a near-optimal evaluation order for each input row. Evaluated across diverse real-world datasets and comprehensive synthetic workloads, both Larch variants always outperform existing semantic filter optimization techniques in terms of token usage on every dataset and workload. Our results demonstrate that Larch is robust across diverse workloads across text and image modalities, 6%--30% lower total token costs and 3x--19x lower token costs overhead than Palimpzest and Quest.

replace-cross MMAO-Cls: Metabolic Multi-Agent Optimization for Joint Feature Selection and Classifier Tuning

Authors: Jinliang Xu, Liping Ma

Abstract: This paper studies whether the Metabolic Multi-Agent Optimizer (MMAO) can act as a credible outer-loop optimizer for classification model selection. We propose MMAO-Cls, a mixed-space realization in which each agent jointly encodes a binary feature mask and classifier hyperparameters, while private energy, communal budget, role drift, and lifecycle turnover are mapped to the accuracy-complexity tradeoff of wrapper learning. The implementation is strengthened by deriving feature-budget adaptation from feature-information priors and by regularizing validation reward with both subset compactness and train-validation overfitting gap. We evaluate MMAO-Cls on seven standard tabular benchmarks with three seeds each and compare it against RandomSearch, GA-lite, PSO-lite, and an endogenous no-sharing ablation. On the aggregate validation objective, MMAO-Cls ranks second ($0.9433$) behind GA-lite ($0.9446$). On held-out test performance, it reaches mean score $0.8882$, improving over RandomSearch ($0.8808$) and GA-lite ($0.8857$), remaining close to PSO-lite ($0.8874$) and the no-sharing ablation ($0.8900$), while using the most compact mean held-out feature subset among all compared methods (feature ratio $0.4881$). Pairwise tests show that these margins are not yet statistically significant. The results support MMAO's applicability to classification and compact mixed-space search, while the contribution of communal sharing to held-out performance remains unresolved.

replace-cross BBOWP-Bench: Evaluating LLMs on Black-Box Optimization Word Problems

Authors: Yutaro Yamada, Kei Hiroshima, Nozomu Yoshinari, Kento Uchida, Shinichi Shirakawa

Abstract: Formulating an optimization problem strongly affects final solution quality, yet good formulations usually require substantial expertise. Recent studies have explored automatically deriving optimization problems from natural-language descriptions, but existing benchmarks focus on settings where objectives and constraints can be written explicitly as mathematical expressions. Many practically important problems are naturally treated as black-box optimization (BBO) problems, in which only objective values are observable, and the functional form is unavailable. In BBO, search space design (as part of problem formulation) and optimization algorithm selection are crucial for problem-solving. Automating these processes with large language models (LLMs) remains challenging. We introduce Black-Box Optimization Word Problems (BBOWP), a novel problem setting in which a system must infer both a search space and an optimization algorithm from a natural-language description of a black-box optimization task. To support research on this setting, we establish the BBOWP Benchmark Suite (BBOWP-Bench), a dataset and evaluation framework for BBOWP. Each instance combines a natural-language problem description, an executable evaluation environment, and a human-designed reference formulation, allowing evaluation of both search-space design and algorithm selection. Using this benchmark, we evaluate LLMs on the joint formulation of search spaces and optimizers from natural-language descriptions across multiple BBO domains. We show that current LLMs vary their optimizer choice with the evaluation budget, though they sometimes struggle with search space design, particularly in identifying important variables and balancing their ranges when the problem description is less informative or the search space is highly problem-specific. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/shiralab/bbowp-bench.

URLs: https://github.com/shiralab/bbowp-bench.

replace-cross Local Spike Timing Enables Gradient-based Learning in Neural Microcircuits

Authors: Xiangnan Zhang, Jingxin Liu, Ranqi Lu, Jingyu Liu, Qunxi Dong, Fuze Tian, Lixian Zhu, Bin Hu, Bj\"orn W. Schuller

Abstract: The brain uses discrete spikes for dynamic computation, yet in the fields of computational neuroscience and brain-inspired computing, how neural microcircuits (NMCs) solve temporal credit assignment using local spike timing remains a fundamental open question. Dominant spiking neural network (SNN) approaches circumvent this by approximating backpropagation through surrogate gradients, decoupling learning from biological spike timing. In order to integrate seemingly incompatible spike timing and continuous gradients, we reformulate temporal credit assignment as a state separation problem: extracting task-required components induced by historical perturbations directly from the current neural state. This enables an online feedback learning framework for NMCs through a gradient tunneling (GT) algorithm and the lead-lag expansion technique that derives credit assignment from local synaptic spike timing, while remaining compatible with ANN-SNN hybrid architectures. Experimentally, GT-trained NMCs excel at long-timescale evidence integration and noise-robust memory retention, and perform comparably to leading SNN online learning methods on real-world benchmarks with far fewer parameters. The proposed framework addresses the two-decade-old NMC feedback learning problem and suggests a computational theory for the brain's temporal credit assignment mechanisms.

replace-cross Self-Supervised Learning for Robust Resonance Mass Regression in Cascade Decays

Authors: Ho Fung Tsoi, Alex Yang, Luis Felipe Gutierrez Zagazeta, Shion Chen, Dylan Rankin

Abstract: Reconstructing the mass of a heavy resonance from its decay products with missing energy is one of the central tasks that directly determine the sensitivity in new physics searches at collider experiments. Supervised learning approaches to this problem often struggle to generalize well due to the presence of various systematic uncertainties and distribution shifts. Exhausting all possible variations in the labeled data can be very compute-intensive, while a failure of the model to generalize can corrupt the reconstructed resonance widths that are critical in peak-hunting analyses. In this work, following the foundation model paradigm, we use a self-supervised approach to pre-train a transformer encoder with VICReg to learn an embedding invariant to various corruptions, then fine-tune it for mass regression on a heavy resonance with masses ranging from 2.5 to 6.5 TeV and a SUSY-like cascade decay into an eleven-body final state. We show that the pre-trained model reconstructs sharper resonance peaks and has a more stable performance under various realistic corruptions, compared to a supervised model of the same architecture trained on the same augmented data from scratch.

replace-cross Similarity Pairing with Energy Mover's Distance for Self-Supervised Pre-Training at the LHC

Authors: Ho Fung Tsoi, Dylan Rankin

Abstract: Many self-supervised methods for training foundation models at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) rely on data augmentations to encourage the model to embed events into a representation space invariant to certain physical or detector symmetries. A common challenge arises from the large freedom in choosing a proper set of augmentations on which downstream performance depends. The implementation of augmentations involves either modifying existing events, potentially breaking the event fidelity, or simulating more event variants, which is computationally intensive. In this work, we present a data-driven method of pairing events by their similarity via the energy mover's distance (EMD), which measures how similar two events are in terms of the work required to transform one into the other. With this approach, distinct events are sampled and matched by their similarity to serve as views for learning invariance, keeping the physics content of each event intact without handcrafted distortions. We demonstrate this augmentation-free pairing method by pre-training on QCD jets via self-distillation and show that it can yield semantic jet embeddings with downstream discrimination power comparable to or better than an augmentation-based baseline.

replace-cross UltraBench 2: Towards Robust Evaluation of Vision Foundation Models on Ultrasound

Authors: Ashwath Radhachandran, Adam Tupper, Christian Gagn\'e, William Speier

Abstract: Benchmarking is an increasingly critical part of research in machine learning and the domains where it is applied, including healthcare. Yet, despite the steady development of new ultrasound foundation models in recent years, the development of well-designed benchmarks to evaluate them has lagged behind. This deficiency has led to fragmented and inconsistent evaluations of competing models, making it difficult to measure progress. To address this issue, we introduce UltraBench 2, a comprehensive benchmark with wide anatomical and task coverage, and a focus on standardization, reproducibility, and ease-of-use. Using this benchmark, we compare existing vision foundation models for ultrasound image analysis. Our analyses demonstrate that ultrasound-specific pretraining still leads on classification, but that state-of-the-art general-purpose models have drawn level on segmentation.

replace-cross The Alignment Illusion in Multimodal Large Language Models

Authors: Hong-Han Wang, Yuntao Wang, Hu Ding

Abstract: Layer-wise visual-text similarity in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is widely interpreted as evidence that the language model progressively integrates visual content into a shared representation space. This reading rests on the assumption that scalar alignment scores reflect content-level cross-modal interaction. To test this assumption, we apply controlled interventions to the visual stream. Across 13 MLLMs from five families spanning 0.5B to 72B parameters, replacing projector-output visual tokens with Gaussian noise sharply reduces task accuracy, yet four standard scalar measures (CKA, SVCCA, MIR, and the leading principal-angle cosine) fail to consistently separate the corrupted stream from the original. We call this failure the alignment illusion and trace it to the shared language-model pathway: anisotropic MLP down-projections pull visual and text tokens toward common output directions, producing weight-induced alignment. We introduce the principal-angle gap (PA gap), defined as the difference between the top two principal-angle cosines, to distinguish leading-direction dominance from the broader overlap observed with structured visual content. Under graded visual corruption, the PA gap tracks task accuracy more consistently than the scalar scores we consider; under a structured but irrelevant image, it further exposes regimes in which internal geometry and task accuracy come apart. Internal visual-text alignment in MLLMs is therefore best read as a geometric diagnostic of the visual stream inside the language model rather than a direct proxy for content-level cross-modal interaction, and is most informative when calibrated by controlled task evidence.

replace-cross Generative sequence modeling for infinite memory processes via predictive states

Authors: Michael Wieck-Sosa, Cosma Rohilla Shalizi

Abstract: We consider estimating the one-step-ahead conditional distribution of a multivariate stochastic process. Many existing approaches rely on assumptions such as finite-range memory, sparsity, or additivity, which can be poorly suited to processes with long-range nonlinear interactions. However, without such structural assumptions, nonparametric estimation is challenging due to the curse of dimensionality. To address this challenge, we introduce a new estimator based on learning representations of the \emph{predictive states} of a stochastic process. We show that the statistical complexity of the estimation problem is determined by the dimension of this predictive state representation. Furthermore, we show that a $p$-dimensional predictive state space can be represented in a $2p+1$-dimensional feature space. Our results imply that faster convergence rates are achievable when the predictive state space is lower-dimensional, i.e., when the past history can be compressed into a lower-dimensional statistic that is sufficient for predicting the future. We establish theoretical convergence rate guarantees for an instantiation of our method based on deep neural network estimators. We implement our method in PyTorch and evaluate its performance on synthetic data and real \emph{C. elegans} data.

replace-cross High-Dimensional Online Calibration from Harmonic Weights

Authors: Maxwell Fishelson, Mehryar Mohri

Abstract: We study the online calibration of multidimensional forecasts over an arbitrary convex set $Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^d$ relative to an arbitrary error norm $\|\cdot\|_L$. For forecasting $d$ binary outcomes simultaneously ($Y=[0,1]^d$), we give the first algorithm that achieves $\varepsilon$-calibration in a number of rounds that is polynomial in $d$ for every fixed accuracy. It requires $d^{O(1/\varepsilon)}$ rounds, exponentially improving the dimension dependence of previous bounds. For multi-class forecasting ($Y=\Delta_d$), we obtain the same $d^{O(1/\varepsilon)}$ rate, improving the $d^{\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^2)}$ bounds of Peng and Fishelson, Golowich, Mohri, and Schneider. Our algorithm is simple: on each round, it outputs a harmonically weighted distribution over harmonically smoothed past outcomes. The same algorithm works for every forecast set and error norm. More generally, it achieves $\varepsilon$-calibration after $\exp(O(\gamma(Y,L)/\varepsilon))$ rounds, where $\gamma(Y,L)$ is a geometric parameter defined by a matrix discrepancy problem. The harmonic weights are motivated by the fact that the discrete Hilbert transform matrix achieves the optimal discrepancy up to a universal constant, simultaneously for every $L$. This optimality result may be of independent interest. The same harmonic responses also yield an algorithm for high-dimensional Blackwell approachability. Its guarantees are governed by a sharpened geometric parameter that captures the joint geometry of the outcome and action sets.

replace-cross Similar Models, Broken Odds: Where Luce's Axiom Fails in Chatbot Arena

Authors: Soovadeep Bakshi, Apurba Bose, Aishwarya Bose, Sandip Nandi

Abstract: Chatbot Arena gives each language model one Bradley-Terry score, which fixes how often any model should beat any other. Choice theory predicts where this fails: near-copies are compared on the few details in which they differ, so votes between them are more decisive than their scores imply, exactly for the comparison a lab cares about, a new version against the old one. On 1.8 million Arena votes, votes between versions of one model are 1.37 times as decisive as Bradley-Terry predicts, between sizes of one generation 1.26 times and between models of one lab 1.12 times; a release a year later points the same way, and the effect survives tie, prompt, topic and timing checks. How alike two models' answers are worded and formatted tracks the effect. Plain scores leave the odds between relatives about 9% too close to even on held-out votes; a correction with four to six parameters roughly halves this and predicts held-out votes better, mostly by modeling ties, while the leaderboard order barely moves; frozen and carried to new releases, its gains in head-to-head odds are too small for our test to establish. With language-model judges and three-answer menus, a near-copy takes votes mostly from its twin when choices are pooled over prompts (log-odds shift +0.10, 95% interval 0.05 to 0.16), less so within a prompt.

replace-cross Learning to Read Out: Unembedding Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining

Authors: Matteo He, William F. Shen, Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanovic, Xinchi Qiu, Nicholas D. Lane

Abstract: When does a language model acquire a capability? Developmental studies usually answer this by tracking when models start producing the correct token. But that prediction passes through the unembedding matrix, or readout, which is itself still being learned during pretraining. We show that a model's hidden states can encode task information before its own readout expresses it, so timelines read from outputs conflate what the model encodes with what its readout expresses. To track the readout directly, we introduce parameter-trajectory crosscoding, which fits one sparse dictionary to every token's readout row at every checkpoint. Unlike crosscoders on activations, it needs only checkpoint weights. We use it to locate a narrow early window, 0.4-1.1% of the way through Pythia's pretraining, in which the readout reorganizes and WordNet word categories become decodable from its rows. By then, linear probes on the hidden states already decode task information that the model's own outputs do not yet show. To test whether such gaps depend on how fast the readout learns, we pretrain small models from scratch that differ only in the readout's learning rate. We find that raising it makes the readout develop earlier and the gap close sooner, with little effect on final loss. Together, these results imply that early in training, where many developmental transitions are dated, a behavioral date can reflect when the readout learns to express task information and not when the model learns it.

replace-cross Brain2Vec: A Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Structural Brain MRI

Authors: Spiros Maggioros, Guray Erus, Gareth Harman, George Aidinis, Alexander Getka, Aristeidis Sotiras, Christos Davatzikos

Abstract: Deep learning models have become essential tools in medical imaging, yet training them end-to-end for diverse tasks remains challenging due to limited labeled data and computational constraints. To address this, we introduce Brain2Vec, a foundation model pre-trained on 74,425 T1-weighted Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans pooled from multiple studies. Brain2Vec can serve both as a weight initialization backbone for fine-tuning downstream tasks and as a standalone feature extractor. We evaluated Brain2Vec in three tasks: downstream predictive performance, convergence speed, and embedding quality. Using Brain2Vec's pretrained weights as initialization and fine-tuning on labeled data for a brain age prediction task, results demonstrate improved final accuracy and faster convergence compared to random initialization. Additionally, its frozen embeddings outperform anatomically defined volumetric features on four of five downstream classification tasks and perform competitively with a state-of-the-art foundation model pre-trained on substantially more data. On the independent BrainFMBench benchmark, Brain2Vec ranked first among six pretrained brain MRI foundation models on 9 of 15 sex, age, and BMI prediction tasks across five cohorts. Lastly, we show that Brain2Vec encodes information primarily from a spatially localized and neuroanatomically meaningful set of brain regions, and that manipulating a subset of latent features produces monotonic, region-specific effects on the reconstructed anatomy. Brain2Vec code and trained models are available as an open-source package on https://github.com/CBICA/Brain2Vec. Users can apply Brain2Vec locally by installing the package, or directly through the NiChart cloud platform https://cloud.neuroimagingchart.com, where Brain2Vec features can be derived without requiring local installation or specialized infrastructure.

URLs: https://github.com/CBICA/Brain2Vec., https://cloud.neuroimagingchart.com,

replace-cross EmbodiExp: Compositional Experience Transfer for Embodied Agents

Authors: Zhifeng Gu, Ying Chen, Rui Jiang, Lei Yi, Bing Wang

Abstract: Experience from past tasks can help embodied agents perform new tasks, but it may not remain applicable or effective when conditions change. Tasks with different overall goals often share subgoals, providing a basis for cross-task experience transfer. We present EmbodiExp, a framework for constructing outcome-grounded subgoal experiences and composing them in new tasks. Each experience describes how to pursue a subgoal, when the guidance applies, and what outcome must be verified for completion. Verified outcomes from applying one experience can satisfy the conditions for applying another, allowing experiences from different past tasks to be composed online. By examining the actions and observed outcomes at the subgoal level, EmbodiExp retains experience from successful subgoals and uses explicit action failures to identify actions to avoid under specific conditions. Experiments on EmbodiedBench and VIGIL assess cross-task transfer with experiences kept fixed throughout held-out evaluation. EmbodiExp achieves success rates of 68.0% on EB-Habitat and 68.3% on EB-ALFRED, compared with 50.0% and 54.7% for the agent without stored experience.

replace-cross NaijaFacts: A Framework for Disentangling Language from Knowledge in Facts about Nigeria

Authors: Zainab Afolabi, Nikita Kozodoi, Jack Butler, Babs Khalidson, Sabastine Dekaa Francis, Christiana I. Ezeilo, Olosunde Akinlolu Adeseye

Abstract: Multilingual benchmarks for African languages mostly translate generic questions into local languages, measuring linguistic competence but not whether models know facts about Africa. We introduce NaijaFacts, a benchmark of factual and statistical questions about Nigeria, authored by native speakers and grounded in authoritative official statistics, with every question available in English, Nigerian Pidgin, Yor\`ub\'a, Igbo, and Hausa. Answering similar questions for the UK and US yields a country by language matrix that disentangles a language effect (the model knows the fact but degrades in a low-resource language) from a knowledge effect (the fact is absent in every language). Across eight frontier and Nigeria-adapted models, the knowledge effect dominates: English-query accuracy is 49-50% for the US/UK but 20% for Nigeria. The language effect is smaller and tracks web representation, degrading from English to Yor\`ub\'a (9 points on Nigeria, up to 24 on US/UK questions). We find scale does not help: the strongest models reach 66-79% on the UK and the US but none exceed 31% on Nigerian statistics. Extending to all 54 African countries with our best performing frontier model shows the same profile continent-wide, 96% on culture and 98% on politics against 26% on statistics, so the gap is not specific to Nigeria.

replace-cross LEGIT: Live Eligibility Gating over Identifier Tries for Constrained Decoding of Heterogeneous Pages

Authors: Felix Fang, Zheng Liu, Robert Saliba

Abstract: A generative recommender names each candidate with a short token sequence, a Semantic ID, and recommends by generating those tokens. Some generative recommenders, including the Uber Eats search engine we are building, write a whole results page, stores with their menu items, in one decoding pass. Every Semantic ID on such a page must name a real store or item that is eligible for the search, such as a store that delivers to the user's location, and that is not already on the page. Each item must also belong to the store above it. A trie over the catalog keeps identifiers real, but eligibility and repetition change with every request and token, and the grammar-constrained decoder we tried could not enforce these changing rules exactly and quickly. We call this constraint LEGIT, for live eligibility gating over identifier tries. Numbering trie leaves depth first turns each rule into a range lookup over one store trie and one item trie, and every generated page passes an independent catalog check. On one NVIDIA B200 GPU with one or two requests per decode step, a direct implementation that walks the trie on the GPU makes each token about 5 times as slow as unconstrained decoding. Across 36 implementations, hardware counters and ablations are consistent with kernel launches and CPU-GPU waits as the source of this slowdown. Our fastest implementation walks the trie on the CPU while the GPU runs the model, needs no custom kernel, and cuts time per output token by 65.9 percent against the direct GPU implementation, to about 1.7 times the unconstrained cost. This implementation is also the fastest on a page layout that puts each item before its store.

replace-cross on\'ona: Twin Embeddings with Tied Unembeddings

Authors: Cheng Luo, Xinnan Dai, Zhongzhu Zhou, Zefan Cai, Ruijie Zhu, Tianyu Zhang, Junjie Hu

Abstract: Weight tying reduces the parameter cost of language models, but the usual tied output head is limited to a single softmax. We introduce on\'ona, a two-table parameterization that increases output expressiveness while preserving input-output weight sharing. The Transformer takes the average of two embedding tables as input, and the output head combines the softmax distributions defined by those same tables. A gate conditioned only on the current input token determines the mixture weight. Its values can be precomputed for inference, and the Transformer body remains unchanged. We evaluate on\'ona across Dense, Gated DeltaNet hybrid, mixture-of-experts, and Attention Residual architectures at 142M, 330M, and 1B scales. Under matched token budgets, on\'ona lowers final validation loss and external perplexity in 11 of 12 comparisons and raises the average accuracy over eight downstream tasks in 10 of 12. The one exception on loss is the 1B MoE model. The cost is one extra embedding table and one extra vocabulary projection.

replace-cross Trajectory Density Ratios under Weak Support: A Survey across the Sciences

Authors: Wei Chen, Lihao Zhuang, Yubing Wu, Delu Zeng

Abstract: Many problems across the sciences compare two stochastic processes through the sequences they generate. Reinforcement learning asks what a new policy would return from the trajectories of an old one; molecular simulation asks how much more a ligand binds from samples of the unbound state; climate attribution asks how a heat wave shifted under warming; and language-model post-training asks what a deployed agent will do from logged interactions. In every case, one quantity would answer all these questions at once: the ratio of the two probability laws over their generated sequences, the trajectory density ratio. This ratio exists only under strong conditions. When one process reaches states the other cannot, the ratio does not exist; when their overlap is thin, its variance grows without control with the sequence length; and when they concentrate on different lower-dimensional sets, the ratio is undefined altogether. Weak support of this kind is the norm for sequence data, not a sampling accident, and its price is paid in identifiability rather than in noise alone. This survey organizes the field around a three-level diagnosis of weak support and a map of the nine retreats that methods take when the full ratio is out of reach, from direct estimation and reweighting through staged decomposition, stationarization, and partial-identification bounds to smoothing, transport couplings, operator spectra, and direct estimation of a single functional. Each retreat keeps part of what the ratio offers and pays for the rest, and each application domain has standardized the retreat its data force. The survey closes with open problems: testable diagnostics at every level, partial identification at the trajectory level, a rate theory of time-amplified error, and benchmarks with ground truth.

replace-cross Before Words, Beyond Speech: Evaluating Nonverbal Social Reasoning in Early Childhood

Authors: Marie Amale Huynh, Laura Bravo-S\'anchez, Lauren Klein, Nick Haber, Philip Fisher, Serena Yeung-Levy

Abstract: Children's earliest years are a period of intense social learning, during which meaning is built through gaze, gesture, and physical contact. Yet, this critical aspect of social interaction remains under-evaluated in multimodal video understanding, as existing benchmarks prioritize the conversation-driven interactions of adults. We address this gap with \bench ($\textbf{N}$aturalistic $\textbf{E}$arly-childhood $\textbf{S}$ocial in$\textbf{T}$eractions), the first child-centric benchmark for nonverbal social reasoning. \bench comprises \totalvids manually annotated clips ($\sim$12s) curated for diversity across naturalistic settings, cultural contexts, and developmental stages. Beyond a lack of data, targeted model improvement in this domain is hindered by coarse evaluation schemas that conflate basic perception with higher-order reasoning. To resolve this, \bench introduces a compositional schema that builds atomic interaction cues into progressive levels of social reasoning: contextual, behavioral, interpersonal, and open-ended descriptions. Extensive analysis of state-of-the-art Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reveals a stark performance collapse: despite strong contextual recognition, models exhibit substantial gaps in decoding precise behavioral and interpersonal cues. We trace these failures to specific biases, including poor consequence grounding and temporal sensitivity. Finally, we ground \bench in real-world applications by evaluating VLMs using domain adaptation strategies. Ultimately, \bench provides a rigorous testbed for advancing socially grounded artificial intelligence in early childhood. Project page: https://laubravo.github.io/nest_website/.

URLs: https://laubravo.github.io/nest_website/.

replace-cross ForeSpark: Joint Draft and Verification Budget Scheduling for Parallel Speculative Decoding

Authors: Jaeyong Song, Daniel Wang, Pratyush Patel, Jonny Kong, Preyas Shah, Shobhit Kanaujia

Abstract: Parallel drafters accelerate LLM decoding by proposing a whole block of draft tokens in a single forward pass, making them attractive for LLM serving. Recent systems optimize goodput (i.e., accepted tokens per second) because rejected drafts steal compute from other requests. However, these systems control only how many tokens are verified, so every request still drafts a full block. At high serving load, we find that drafting consumes up to 40% of step time, of which a majority is discarded because it is never verified. Yet, draft-length control from autoregressive drafters does not carry over, because a parallel drafter commits to its length before its single pass. Moreover, draft and verification lengths are coupled across the batch, so they must be selected jointly for every request while keeping the decision computationally cheap at every step. In this paper, we propose work-conserving speculation (WCS), a design that jointly allocates draft and verification budgets per request in LLM serving, and present ForeSpark as its prototype. ForeSpark realizes WCS with (i) a context-aware pre-draft estimator that predicts acceptance for every candidate length from the target model's context features, (ii) a lightweight joint draft-verify scheduler that selects both lengths through a global sort, and (iii) a post-draft budget distribution that spends verification on the most promising tokens. Implemented on vLLM, ForeSpark improves throughput by up to 1.77x over the state-of-the-art DSpark and by 2.49x over autoregressive decoding at a matched per-request generation rate, without changing the output distribution. It also tolerates higher load, sustaining up to 1.51x the request rate of DSpark under latency service level objectives (SLOs).

replace-cross Benchmarking AI risk behaviors

Authors: Luhan Mikaelson, Robert Adragna, Emmett Brosowsky, Hayley Clatterbuck

Abstract: We evaluate LLM risk propensities across a range of contexts and task demands. We evaluate models for how risk averse or risk tolerant they are in various conditions, as well as how stable and consistent their behavior is across conditions. We present a toolkit for developing model risk profiles that can be used to predict model behavior and which can be adapted to include new evaluations of risk propensities. We also examine how these properties have changed across model generations and families. We observe that 2026 state-of-the-art models tend to be more stable, more consistent, and more risk neutral than older models. These trends appear to be related; newer models are more stable and consistent because they are more likely to use expected value maximization as a decision procedure.

replace-cross An Invariant Neural-Network Eddy-Viscosity Closure with Iterative Solver Coupling for Molten Salt Fast Reactor Thermal-Hydraulics

Authors: Zaid Abulawi (Department of Nuclear Engineering, Texas A&M University), William Oliveira (Department of Energy, Nuclear Engineering Division, Politecnico di Milano), Akzhol Almukhametov (Department of Nuclear Engineering, Texas A&M University), Jun Fang (Nuclear Science and Engineering Division, Argonne National Laboratory), Ling Zou (Nuclear Science and Engineering Division, Argonne National Laboratory), Rui Hu (Nuclear Science and Engineering Division, Argonne National Laboratory), Stefano Lorenzi (Department of Energy, Nuclear Engineering Division, Politecnico di Milano), Yang Liu (Department of Nuclear Engineering, Texas A&M University)

Abstract: System thermal-hydraulics codes such as the System Analysis Module (SAM) close turbulence on coarse meshes with algebraic zero-equation mixing-length models. These models cannot represent the non-equilibrium turbulence, flow separation, and large-scale recirculation of open-cavity advanced reactors. We develop an invariant neural-network eddy-viscosity closure for SAM and apply it to the Molten Salt Fast Reactor (MSFR). The closure predicts the turbulent kinetic energy and timescale of each element from the flow and geometry of its neighbors, without solving turbulence transport equations. The resulting non-negative eddy viscosity enters the implicit diffusive operator, which keeps the momentum equations well conditioned. We prove that the closure is exactly invariant to translation, Galilean boost, rotation, reflection, and neighbor ordering. A network trained on high-order Nek5000 fields sees a different feature distribution on the coarse SAM mesh. We remove this covariate shift by iteratively retraining the network on SAM flow states against fixed Nek5000 targets. For a held-out steady state, the coupled closure reproduces the single recirculation cell and eddy-viscosity plume of the Nek5000 reference. In a 120 s pump-coastdown transient, it halves the velocity error of the mixing-length model, from 37.6% to 17.7%, and matches the Nek5000 eddy viscosity with a time-averaged relative error of 12%. The remaining velocity error comes from the coarse mesh. The coupled transient takes about 0.2 core-hours, compared with about 330 core-hours for Nek5000. The results show that the closure can recover two-equation RANS turbulence behavior in a coarse-mesh system code at a small fraction of the CFD cost.

replace-cross ArchSpace: Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing LLM Architecture Research

Authors: Jie Wang, Tong Zhu, Jiahao Kuang, Zhe Xu, Mowen Ruan, Yunkai Chai, Yuliang Liu, Dehong Zheng, Rubin Wei, Yu Cheng, Bowen Zhou, Tao Ji, Zhouhan Lin, Qi Zhang

Abstract: Model architecture shapes the scaling behavior, training efficiency, and inference cost of LLMs, making it a central focus of both industry and academia. In industry, teams such as those behind DeepSeek, Qwen, and Kimi routinely introduce architectural innovations, yet public reports rarely document which ideas succeeded, which were abandoned, and why. In academia, limited compute and inconsistent baselines often restrict evaluations to small-scale, single-point comparisons, making it difficult to establish whether improvements hold at larger scales or translate into broader impact. ArchSpace addresses both gaps as a public platform for Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing Architecture Research: each proposal is recorded with its experiments and reasoning, reviewed by the community, and referenced by later work. To evaluate ideas before costly large-scale training, ArchSpace leverages scaling laws to predict model loss at larger scales from smaller experiments, a practice widely adopted in industry. We provide initial references that make the platform usable: Qwen3.5 and OLMo3 baselines, fitted scaling laws, and a reusable training recipe. Ideas validated through this review can in turn become new baselines, and ArchSpace thereby aims to broaden the range of ideas that shape the next generation of models.
